Clustering Systems of Phylogenetic Networks
Marc Hellmuth, David Schaller, Peter F. Stadler

Abstract
Rooted acyclic graphs appear naturally when the phylogenetic relationship of a set X X of taxa involves not only speciations but also recombination, horizontal transfer, or hybridization, that cannot be captured by trees. A variety of classes of such networks have been discussed in the literature, including phylogenetic, level-1, tree-child, tree-based, galled tree, regular, or normal networks as models of different types of evolutionary processes. Clusters arise in models of phylogeny as the sets 𝙲 ⁡ ( v ) \CC(v) of descendant taxa of a vertex v v . The clustering system 𝒞 N \mathscr{C}_{N} comprising the clusters of a network N N conveys key information on N N itself. In the special case of rooted phylogenetic trees, T T is uniquely determined by its clustering system 𝒞 T \mathscr{C}_{T} . Although this is no longer true for networks in general, it is of interest to relate properties of N N and 𝒞 N \mathscr{C}_{N} . Here, we systematically investigate the relationships of several well-studied classes of networks and their clustering systems. The main results are correspondences of classes of networks and clustering system of the following form: If N N is a network of type 𝕏 \mathbb{X} , then 𝒞 N \mathcal{C}_{N} satisfies 𝕐 \mathbb{Y} , and conversely if 𝒞 \mathscr{C} is a clustering system satisfying 𝕐 \mathbb{Y} then there is network N N of type 𝕏 \mathbb{X} such that 𝒞 ⊆ 𝒞 N \mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N} .This, in turn, allows us to investigate the mutual dependencies between the distinct types of networks in much detail.



Keywords:
compatibility, level-k, hybrid, evolution, cluster, network,
phylogenetics, least common ancestor

1
Introduction

Networks used to model phylogenetic relationships typically are directed
acyclic graphs (DAGs) with a single root, i.e., a unique vertex from which
all other vertices can be reached from. As usual in phylogenetics, the
subset
X
of vertices without descendants (the leaves of the network)
represents the extant taxa, while the remaining vertices model their
ancestors. In this contribution, we are interested in the relationships
between the structure of networks
N
with leaf set
X
and their
associated clustering systems
\mathscr{C}_{N}
, which contains, for each
vertex
v
of
N
, the subset
\CC(v)\subseteq X
of leaves that can be
reached from
v

[
45
,
26
]
. In the literature on
phylogenetic networks, the sets
C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
are often called the
“hardwired clusters” of
N
.

As a special case, there is a well-known 1-to-1 correspondence between
rooted phylogenetic trees
T
and hierarchies
\mathscr{C}

[
48
]
, i.e., clustering systems that do not contain pairs of
overlapping clusters. Therefore,
T
is uniquely determined by

\mathscr{C}_{T}=\mathscr{C}
. This simple correspondence, however, is no
longer true for (phylogenetic) networks. Nevertheless, it is not difficult
to find some network
N
for a given clustering system
\mathscr{C}
such
that
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
: If suffices to determine the Hasse
diagram
N=\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
of the inclusion partial order of the
clustering system
\mathscr{C}
to obtain such a network. For a
phylogenetic trees
T
, the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{T})
and
T

are isomorphic. For general networks, however, the situation is much more
complicated
[
45
,
26
,
59
]
.

A broad array of different types of networks have been studied in the
literature in order to model different modes of non-tree-like evolution
such as horizontal gene transfer, recombination, or hybridization, see

[
43
]
for a current review. Naturally, the question arises how
much information about the structure of
N
is contained in the clustering
system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
. We will in particular be concerned with the
following, inter-related questions

1.

Which types of networks
N
satisfy
N\simeq\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
?

2.

What are necessary properties of the clustering systems

\mathscr{C}_{N}
obtained for networks
N
of a given class?

3.

Which types of networks
N
, if any, can be characterized in
terms of properties of their clustering systems
\mathscr{C}_{N}
?

4.

When is a network
N
uniquely determined by
\mathscr{C}_{N}
or
by the corresponding multiset of clusters
\mathscr{M}_{N}
?

5.

When is a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
compatible with a specified
type of network
N
in the sense that there is network
N
of given type
such that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
?

While addressing these questions, we will also consider the implications
between the defining properties of the various network classes. To help the
reader navigate this contribution, we summarize the properties of interest
in Table
1
and point to their formal definitions.
Complementarily, properties of clustering systems are compiled in
Table
2
. Many of the results established here
are summarized in Table
3
.

It is important to note that the literature on phylogenetic networks does
not always utilize the same nomenclature. In particular, properties such as

binary
,
separated
,
conventional
, or

phylogenetic
are – more often than not – taken for granted in a
given publication and explicitly or tacitly included in the definition of
“phylogenetic network”. Here, we start from a very general setting of
rooted DAGs, called “networks” throughout. All additional properties are
made explicit throughout. We furthermore strive to prove all statements as
general as possible. The reader will therefore on occasion find results
that are well known in the field, although earlier proofs pertain to a more
restrictive setting.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we provide
the basic terminology and definitions used throughout this paper. In
Section
3
, we start with a closer look at
phylogenetic networks (Sect.
3.1
) and related
concepts which includes graph modifications such as arc-contractions or
expansions (Sect.
3.2
) as well as the structural properties of
non-trivial biconnected components (called blocks) in networks
(Sect.
3.3
). We then continue in
Section
3.4
to characterize the structure of the Hasse
diagram of clustering systems. In particular, we provide new
characterizations of regular networks, that is, networks that are
isomorphic to the Hasse diagram of some clustering system.

In Section
4
, we consider semi-regular networks, i.e.,
networks that do not contain so-called shortcuts and satisfy the
path-cluster-comparability (PCC) property as introduced in
Section
4.1
. (PCC) simply ensures that one of the clusters

\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)
is subsets of the other one whenever the vertices
u

and
v
are connected by a directed path in
N
. Although this property
does not seem to have been studied so far, it turns out to play a
fundamental role in the relationships between networks and their clustering
systems. Regular networks, as it turns out, are precisely the semi-regular
networks that do not contain vertices with outdegree
1
. In addition, we
show how to obtain regular networks
N^{\prime}
from networks
N
that only
satisfy (PCC) such that
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. In
Section
4.2
, we consider separated networks (networks for
which each vertex with indegree greater than
1
has outdegree
1
) and
cluster networks in the sense of
[
26
]
(whose definition is
somewhat more involved). As we shall see, cluster networks are precisely
the networks that are semi-regular, separated, and phylogenetic. We then
show in Section
4.3
, that semi-regular networks are
uniquely determined by their multiset of clusters and that, in turn,
cluster networks, as a subclass of regular networks, are uniquely
determined by their clustering systems.
Section
5
makes a short excursion to
so-called tree-child, normal, and tree-based networks and
their mutual relationships.

In Section
6
, we then have a closer look at the concept
of least common ancestors (
\lca
) in networks. In contrast to rooted
trees, the
\lca
of a pair of leaves (or more generally, a subset of
leaves) is in general not uniquely defined. We introduce in
Section
6.1
several classes of networks in which the

\lca
is unique for at least certain subsets of leaves. This leads to the
cluster-lca property (CL) which is satisfied by a network
N
whenever

\lca(\CC(v))
is uniquely determined for all
v\in V(N)
. We shall see
that every network that satisfies (PCC), and this in particular includes
all normal networks, also satisfy (CL). In Section
6.2
, we
consider lca-networks, i.e., networks in which
\lca(A)
is uniquely
determined for all leaf sets
A\subseteq X
. Among other results, we show
that a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is closed (under intersection) if
only if it is the clustering systems
\mathscr{C}_{N}
of an lca-network. We
then consider in Section
6.3
the subclass strong
lca-networks, in which, for all
A\subseteq X
, it holds that

\lca(A)=\lca(\{x,y\})
for a suitably chosen pair of leaves
x
and

y
. These are closely related to weak hierarchies.

A very prominent role in phylogenetics is played by level-1 networks.
Section
7
is devoted to establishing structural results for
level-1 networks and their underlying clustering systems.
After establishing basic results, we derive in Section
7.3
a
simple condition, called property (L), for clustering systems that is defined
in terms of the intersection of its elements. As a main result of this
contribution, we obtain in Section
7.4
a simple
characterization of the clustering systems of (phylogenetic, separated)
level-1 network as the ones that are closed and satisfy (L). We then
show in Section
7.5
that property (L) is sufficient to
ensure that clustering systems are “compatible” with a (phylogenetic,
separated) level-1 network.
Moreover, we provide a polynomial-time algorithm to check if

\mathscr{C}
is compatible with some level-1 network and, in the
affirmative case, to construct such a network. We finally consider in
Section
8
several subclasses of
level-1 networks as e.g. galled trees or binary, conventional, separated
or quasi-binary level-1 networks and characterize
their clustering systems. Finally, we show that quasi-binary level-1
networks are encoded by their multisets of clusters.
In Section
9
, we provide a summary of the main results (see also
Table
3
).

2
Preliminaries

The power set of a given set
X
is denoted by
2^{X}
. Two sets
A
and
B

overlap if
A\cap B\notin\{\emptyset,A,B\}
.

We consider graphs
G=(V,E)
with vertex set
V(G)\coloneqq V
and arc set

E(G)\coloneqq E
. A graph
G
is
undirected
if
E
is a subset of
the set of two-element subsets of
V
and
G
is
directed
if

E\subseteq(V\times V)\setminus\{(v,v)\mid v\in V\}
. Thus, arcs
e\in E
in an undirected graph
G
are of the form
e=\{x,y\}
and in directed
graphs of the form
e=(x,y)
with
x,y\in V
being distinct. The

degree
of a vertex
v\in V
in an undirected or directed graph
G
,
denoted by
\deg_{G}(v)
, is the number of arcs that are incident with

v
. If
G
is directed, we furthermore distinguish the indegree

\indeg_{G}(v)=|\left\{u\mid(u,v)\in E\right\}|
and the
outdegree
\outdeg_{G}(v)=|\{u\mid(v,u)\in E\}|
. We write

H\subseteq G
if
H
is a subgraph of
G
and
G[W]
for the subgraph in

G
that is induced by some subset
W\subseteq V
. Moreover
G-v
denotes
the induced subgraph
G[V\setminus\{v\}]
.

A path
P
in an undirected (resp. directed) graph
G
is a subgraph of

k\geq 1
vertices and arcs
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}\in E

(resp.
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
) for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
. Moreover,
(directed) paths connecting two vertices
x
and
y
are also called

xy
-paths
. We will often write
undirected path
for a
subgraph
P
of a directed graph
G
that has vertices

\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
,
k\geq 1
, and the
forward arc

(v_{i},v_{i+1})
or the corresponding
backward arc

(v_{i+1},v_{i})

for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
. The vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{k}
in a directed or
undirected path
P
are the
endpoints
of
P
and all other vertices
(in
P
) are its
inner vertices
. A path
P
with vertices

\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
in a directed graph G is
induced (in
G
)
if

(v_{i},v_{j})\in E(G)
precisely if
j=i+1
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
.

A directed cycle
K
in a directed graph
G
is a subgraph with vertices

\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
,
k\geq 2
, and arcs
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
for all

1\leq i\leq k-1
and additionally
(v_{k},v_{1})\in E
. In analogy to
undirected paths, an
undirected cycle

K
in a directed graph
G
is
a subgraph with
k\geq 3
vertices that can be ordered in the form

\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
such that the forward arc
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
or the
corresponding backward arc
(v_{i+1},v_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
, as well as
the forward arc
(v_{k},v_{1})
or the backward arc
(v_{1},v_{k})
are exactly
the arcs of
K
.

An undirected graph
G=(V,E)
is
bipartite
if there is a partition
of
V
into subsets
W
and
W^{\prime}
such that every arc in
G
connects one
vertex in
W
to one vertex in
W^{\prime}
. If in addition,
x\in W
and
x^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
implies
\{x,x^{\prime}\}\in E
, then
G
is
complete bipartite
.

Graph connectivity.

An undirected graph is
connected
if, for every two vertices
u,v\in V
, there is a path connecting
u
and
v
. A directed graph is

connected
if its underlying undirected graph is connected. A
connected component of
G
is a maximal induced subgraph that is connected.
A vertex
v
is a cut vertex in a graph
G
if
G[V(G)\setminus\{v\}]

consists of more connected components than
G
. Similarly, a directed or
undirected arc
(u,v)
is a cut arc in
G
if the graph
G^{\prime}
with vertex
set
V(G^{\prime})=V(G)
and arc set
E(G^{\prime})=E(G)\setminus\{(u,v)\}
consists of
more connected components than
G
.

An undirected or directed graph is
biconnected
if it contains no
vertex whose removal disconnects the graph. A
block
of an undirected
or a directed graph is a maximal biconnected subgraph. A block
B
is
called
non-trivial
if it contains an (underlying undirected)
cycle. Equivalently, a block is non-trivial if it is not a single vertex or
a single arc. An arc that is at the same time a (trivial) block is a cut
arc. For later reference, we state here the following observations that
are immediate consequences of the fact that two distinct blocks in a graph
share at most one vertex
[
55
, Prop. 4.1.19]
:

Observation 2.1
.

If two biconnected subgraphs share two vertices, then their union is
contained in a common block.

Observation 2.2
.

If
B
and
B^{\prime}
are distinct blocks of a directed graph, then
B
and

B^{\prime}
are arc-disjoint.

The latter is justified by the fact that if blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
share a
common arc, then
B\cup B^{\prime}
is biconnected and thus,
B=B^{\prime}
since blocks
are always
maximal
biconnected subgraphs.
We will frequently make use of

Theorem 2.3
.

[
55
, Thm. 4.2.4]

For a graph
G
with at least three vertices, the following statements are
equivalent:

1.

G
is biconnected.

2.

For all
x,y\in V(G)
, there are at least two internally
vertex-disjoint (undirected) paths connecting
x
and
y
.

3.

For all
x,y\in V(G)
, there is an (undirected) cycle containing
x

and
y
.

Corollary 2.4
.

Any two vertices of a non-trivial block
B
lie on a common (undirected)
cycle in
B
.

The following well-known result will also be useful throughout:

Proposition 2.5
.

[
21
, Prop. 3.1.1]

Let
H
be a biconnected subgraph of
G
and
P
be a path in
G
that only
shares its endpoints with
H
. Then the subgraph obtained by adding
P
to

H
is again biconnected.

Directed acyclic graphs.

A directed graph
G=(V,E)
is
acyclic
if it does not contain a
directed cycle. In particular, every undirected cycle in a directed acyclic
graph (DAG) contains at least one forward and one backward arc. In a DAG

G
, a vertex
u\in V
is called an
ancestor
of
v\in V
and
v
a

descendant
of
u
, in symbols
v\preceq_{G}u
, if there is a
directed path (possibly reduced to a single vertex) in
G
from
u
to

v
. We write
v\prec_{G}u
if
v\preceq_{N}u
and
u\neq v
. If
u\preceq_{N}v
or
v\preceq_{G}u
, then
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{G}
-comparable
and otherwise,

\preceq_{G}
-incomparable
. Moreover, if
(u,v)\in E
, we say that
u

is a
parent
of
v
,
u\in\parent_{G}(v)
, and
v
is a
child

of
u
,
v\in\child_{G}(u)
. Following
[
32
]
, we call a vertex

v
that is
\preceq_{G}
-minimal in a block
B
a
terminal
vertex
(of
B
). Note that every terminal vertex
v
of a non-trivial
block
B
must always have indegree at least
2
since, by
Cor.
2.4
,
v
lies on some undirected cycle in
B
and,
by
\preceq_{G}
-minimality of
v
in
B
, its two incident vertices on
this cycle must both be in-neighbors. Below we will consider DAGs
in which terminal vertices are a type of so-called hybrid vertices.

An arc
(u,w)
in a DAG
G
is a
shortcut
if there is a vertex

v\in\child(u)\setminus\{w\}
such that
w\prec_{G}v
(or, equivalently, if
there is a vertex
v^{\prime}\in V(G)
such that
w\prec_{G}v^{\prime}\prec_{G}u
). A DAG
without shortcuts is
shortcut-free
.

Observation 2.6
.

Let
G
be a DAG. The following statements are equivalent:

1.

N
is shortcut-free.

2.

For all
u\in V(N)
,
v,w\in\child_{G}(u)
are

\preceq_{G}
-comparable if and only if
v=w
.

3.

For all
u\in V(N)
,
v,w\in\parent_{G}(u)
are

\preceq_{G}
-comparable if and only if
v=w
.

3
Networks and Clustering Systems

3.1
Basic Concepts

We define (phylogenetic) networks here
as a slightly more general class of
DAGs than what is customarily considered in most of the literature on the
topic.

Definition 3.1
.

A
(rooted) network
is a directed acyclic graph
N=(V,E)
such that

(N1)

There is a unique vertex
\rho_{N}
, called the
root

of
N
, with
\indeg(\rho_{N})=0
.

A network is
phylogenetic
if

(N2)

There is no vertex
v\in V
with

\outdeg(v)=1
and
\indeg(v)\leq 1
.

A vertex with
v\in V
is a
leaf
if
\outdeg(v)=0
, a

hybrid vertex
if
\indeg(v)>1
, and
tree vertex
if

\indeg(v)\leq 1
. The set of leaves is denoted by
X
.

We note that a leaf
x\in X
is always either a hybrid vertex or a tree
vertex. A leaf
x\in X
is a
strict descendant
of
v\in V
if every
directed path from the root
\rho_{N}
to
x
contains
v
. In contrast to
the even more general definition
[
37
, Def. 3]
, we use the term
“phylogenetic” here to mean that vertices with indegree
1
and outdegree

1
do not appear. Moreover, the root is either the single leaf or has

\outdeg(\rho_{N})\geq 2
. Rooted phylogenetic networks thus generalize rooted
phylogenetic trees. Since the root is an ancestor of all vertices,
N
is
connected. We emphasize that all networks considered here are rooted and
thus, we always use the term “network” instead of “rooted network”.

For a vertex
v
of
N
, the
subnetwork
N(v)
of
N
rooted at
v
,
is the network obtained from the subgraph
N[W]
induced by the vertices in

W\coloneqq\{w\in V(N)\mid w\preceq_{N}v\}
and by suppression of
w
if it
has indegree
0
and outdegree
1
in
N[W]
or hybrid-vertices of
N
that
have in- and outdegree
1
in
N[W]
.

Summary of networks considered in this paper.

N
is/satisfies

0
)

separated

all hybrid vertices of
N
have outdegree

.

Def.
3.2

binary

every tree vertex
v
is either a leaf or
has
\outdeg(v)=2
, and every hybrid vertex

\indeg(v)=2
and
\outdeg(v)=1
.

Def.

level-
k

each block
B
of
N
contains at

k

of
B
)

regular

there is a prescribed isomorphism between

N
and the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
of the clusters in
N
.

Def.
3.41

path-cluster-comparability (PCC)

for all
u,v\in V(N)
,

u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}

\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
or
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
.

Def.
4.1

semi-regular

N
is shortcut-free and satisfies
(PCC).

Def.
4.2

cluster network

N
satisfies (PCC), and, three additional
properties based on the clusters and respective vertices in
N

Def.
4.18

tree-child

for every

v\in V^{0}(N)
, there is a “tree-child”, i.e.,
u\in\child(v)
with

\indeg(u)=1
.

Def.
5.1

normal

N
is tree-child and shortcut-free.

Def.
5.4

tree-based

there is a base tree
T
of
N
that can be obtained from
N
in a
prescribed manner

Def.
5.11

cluster-lca (CL)

\lca(\CC(v))
is defined for all
v\in V(N)

Def.
6.8

lca-network

\lca(A)
is well-defined,
i.e., if
|\LCA(A)|=1
for all non-empty subsets
A\subseteq X
.

Def.
6.14

strong lca-network

N
is an lca-network and, for every
non-empty subset
A\subseteq X
, there are
x,y\in A
such that

\lca(\{x,y\})=\lca(A)
.

Def.
6.24

galled tree

every non-trivial block in
N
is an
(undirected) cycle.

Def.
8.3

conventional

(i) all leaves have indegree at
most
1
and (ii) every hybrid vertex is contained in a unique
non-trivial block.

Def.
8.17

quasi-binary

\indeg_{N}(w)=2
and
\outdeg_{N}(w)=1
for every hybrid vertex
w\in V(N)

and, additionally,
\outdeg_{N}(\max B)=2

for every non-trivial block
B
in
N
.

Def.
8.30

Table 2:
Properties of clustering systems considered in this paper.

Clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is/satisfies

Ref.

hierarchy

for all
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, it holds

C\cap C^{\prime}\in\{\emptyset,C,C^{\prime}\}
.

Def.
3.5

closed

\mathcal{C}
is closed under intersection, i.e.,

\bigcap_{C\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}C\in\mathscr{C}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\emptyset\}
holds
for all
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
.

Def.
3.31

pre-binary

for every pair
x,y\in X
, there is a unique
inclusion-minimal cluster
C
such that
\{x,y\}\subseteq C

Def.
6.6

binary

pre-binary and, for every

C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a pair of vertices
x,y\in X
such that
C

is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster containing
x
and
y
.

Def.
6.25

weak hierarchy

for all
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
, it holds

C_{1}\cap C_{2}\cap C_{3}\in\{C_{1}\cap C_{2},C_{1}\cap C_{3},C_{2}\cap C_{3},\emptyset\}
.

Def.
6.25

(L)

C_{1}\cap C_{2}=C_{1}\cap C_{3}
for all
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}

where
C_{1}
overlaps both
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
.

Def.
7.15

(N3O)

\mathscr{C}
contains no three distinct pairwise
overlapping clusters.

Def.
8.7

paired hierarchy

every
C\in\mathscr{C}
overlaps with at most one
other
cluster in
\mathscr{C}
.

Def.
8.14

(2-Inc)

for all
clusters
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there are at most two inclusion-maximal
clusters
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
with
A,B\subsetneq C
and at most two
inclusion-minimal clusters
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
with
C\subsetneq A,B
.

Def.
8.22

A network
N
with leaf set
X
is often called a
network on
X
.
Two networks
N_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
and
N_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2})
on
X
are
graph
isomorphic
, in symbols
N_{1}\sim N_{2}
, if there is a
graph
isomorphism
, i.e., a bijection
\varphi\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
such that

(u,v)\in E_{1}
if and only if
(\varphi(u),\varphi(v))\in E_{2}
for all

u,v\in V_{1}
. Moreover, if additionally
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are networks on the
same leaf set
X
, we say that
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are
isomorphic
in
symbols
N_{1}\simeq N_{2}
if
N_{1}\sim N_{2}
(via the graph isomorphism

\varphi
) and
\varphi(x)=x
for all
x\in X
. We say that a network
N

on
X
is
unique
w.r.t. some property (or some set of properties),
if
N\simeq N^{\prime}
for every network
N^{\prime}
that also satisfies the desired
property (or properties).

Many studies into phylogenetic networks require that reticulation events
and speciation events are separated, i.e.,
\outdeg(v)=1
for all hybrid
vertices.

Definition 3.2
.

A network
N
is
separated
if all hybrid vertices have outdegree

1
.

In particular, all leaves have indegree
1
in a separated network (or
indegree zero if the network consists of a single vertex).

The properties
phylogenetic
and
separated
are part of
the definition of networks in many publications in the field, see
e.g.
[
42
,
47
,
59
]
. However, we opted for the more
general definition of networks for several reasons. On the one hand, we aim
to explore which restrictions are actually needed to establish the
relationship of different properties or classes of networks. On the other
hand, separated networks do not include regular networks
[
5
]
,
which are, as we shall see, a class of networks that is intimately linked
with clustering systems.

An even more restrictive class of networks that is often considered are
binary networks
[
24
,
12
,
43
]
:

Definition 3.3
.

A network is
binary
if every tree vertex
v
is either a leaf or
has
\outdeg(v)=2
, and every hybrid vertex
v
satisfies
\indeg(v)=2

and
\outdeg(v)=1
.

By construction, binary networks are always phylogenetic and separated.

Throughout this paper, several other properties and distinct classes of
networks are considered. For convenience, all these types are listed in
Table
1
. More formal definitions or more precise
explanations are given in the remainder of the paper. A further essential
ingredient to our paper are clusters and clustering systems as defined
next.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
N
be a network with vertex set
V
, leaf set
X
and partial order

\preceq_{N}
. Then, for each
v\in V
, the associated
cluster
is

\CC(v)\coloneqq\CC_{N}(v)\coloneqq\{x\in X\mid x\preceq_{N}v\}
.
Furthermore, we write
\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}_{N}\coloneqq\{\CC(v)\mid v\in V\}
.

Note, that
\CC(v)=\CC(w)
may be possible for distinct
v,w\in V
. However,

\mathscr{C}
is considered as a
set
and thus, each cluster appears
only once in
\mathscr{C}_{N}
. The clusters in
\mathscr{C}_{N}
are usually
called the
hardwired clusters
of
N
, see e.g.
[
37
]
.

Definition 3.5
.

[
7
,
48
]

A
clustering system
on
X
is a set
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}

such that (i)
\emptyset\notin\mathscr{C}
, (ii)
X\in\mathscr{C}
, and
(iii)
\{x\}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
x\in X
. A clustering system is a

hierarchy
if it does not contain pairwise overlapping sets.

Figure 1:
Three non-isomorphic (binary) level-1 networks with the
same clustering system
\mathscr{C}=\{\{a\},\{b\},\{a,b\}\}
. While they are indistinguishable in
terms of their clustering systems, they are encoded by their multiset
of clusters, see Thm.
8.33
, i.e., they are
distinguished by the multiplicities of the clusters
\{a\}
,
\{b\}
,
and
\{a,b\}
.

We will mainly focus on clustering systems
\mathscr{C}_{N}
of networks
N

(cf. Lemma
3.29
). As shown in Fig.
1
, the
information conveyed by
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is often insufficient to determine

N
, i.e., there are non-isomorphic networks
N
and
N^{\prime}
for which

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. A natural generalization is to
consider the
multiset of clusters

\mathscr{M}_{N}
, in which each
cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
appears once for every vertex
v\in V(N)
with

\CC(v)=C
. We say that
\mathscr{M}_{N}

encodes

N

within a given class
\mathbb{P}
of networks if

N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}
and
\mathscr{M}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathscr{M}_{N}
implies
N^{\prime}\simeq N
.

As for networks, we will also consider plenty of different types of clustering
systems equipped with certain properties and, for convenience, list them in
Table
2
.

3.2
Arc-Expansion and Arc-Contraction

As mentioned above, often only separated networks are considered,
stipulating that (1) leaves, i.e., vertices
v
with
\outdeg(v)=0
have

\indeg(v)=1
; (2) hybrid vertices
v
have
\indeg(v)\geq 2
and

\outdeg(v)=1
. Such networks are obtained from the ones in
Def.
3.1
by means of a simple refinement operation that replaces
every “offending” vertex by a pair of vertices connected by single
arc. More precisely, we define the following operation on a network
N
,
which is also part of
[
37
, Alg. 6.4.2]
:

\expand(v)

Create a new vertex
v^{\prime}
, replace arcs
(u,v)
by

(u,v^{\prime})
for all
u\in\parent_{N}(v)
, and add the arc
(v^{\prime},v)
.

It will also useful to consider the reversed operation for arcs
(v^{\prime},v)

that are not shortcuts:

\contract(v^{\prime},v)

Replace arcs
(u,v^{\prime})
by
(u,v)
for all

u\in\parent_{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus\parent_{N}(v)
; replace arcs
(v^{\prime},w)
by

(v,w)
for all
w\in\child_{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(v)
; and finally
delete
(v^{\prime},v)
and
v^{\prime}
.

The notation
\contract
is chosen in compliance with the literature where
arc contraction is a commonly used operation. Fur our purpose, however, it
will be useful to have this more formal definition in order to precisely
keep track of the vertex sets upon execution of multiple operations.
Observe that, e.g. since
(u,v^{\prime}),(u,v)\in E
is possible, applying first

\contract(v^{\prime},v)
and then
\expand(v)
does not necessarily yield a
network that is isomorphic to the original network. Furthermore, we remark
that the condition that
(v^{\prime},v)
is not a shortcut cannot be dropped since
otherwise directed cycles are introduced
(cf. Fig.
2
(A)).

Figure 2:
Complications arising in the contraction of arcs. The arcs to
be contracted are highlighted in blue. (A) Contraction of a shortcut

(u,w)
introduced directed cycles. (B) Application of

\contract(u,w)
to a shortcut-free network
N
can result in a network

N^{\prime}
that contains a shortcut (r.h.s., shortcut indicated by the red
arc). (C),(D) Contraction of an arc
(u,w)
in a phylogenetic network

N
can yield a network
N^{\prime}
that is no longer
phylogenetic. Application of
\phylo(N^{\prime})
can resolve this issue.

We are now in the position to define least-resolved networks:

Definition 3.6
.

A network
N
is
least-resolved
(w.r.t. its clustering system

\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}_{N}
) if there is no network
N^{\prime}
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
that can be obtained from
N
by a
non-empty series of shortcut removal and application of
\contract(v^{\prime},v)

for some arc
(v^{\prime},v)
that is not a shortcut.

In many applications, phylogenetic networks are considered. However,

\contract(w^{\prime},w)
applied on a phylogenetic network may result in a
non-phylogenetic network. By way of example, see
Fig.
2
(C), if
u
is a tree vertex with parent
u^{\prime}
and
two children
w
and
w^{\prime}
which are leaves, then
\contract(u,w)
will
“locally” result in a path with arcs
(u^{\prime},w)
and
(w,w^{\prime})
, i.e.,

\indeg(w)=\outdeg(w)=1
. Similarly,
\contract(u,w)
in a block that
contains a shortcut can result in a network
N^{\prime}
that is not phylogenetic,
see Fig.
2
(D). To circumvent this issue, we must
“suppress”
w
to obtain a phylogenetic network. To this end, we define
the following operation to make a network
N
phylogenetic:

\phylo(N)

Repeatedly apply
\contract(u,w)
for an arc
(u,w)
such that

\outdeg(u)=1
and
\indeg(u)\leq 1
until no such operation is possible.

Now contraction of an arcs
(v^{\prime},v)
that is not a shortcut and
“suppression” of superfluous vertices can be combined in:

\contract^{\star}(v^{\prime},v)

Apply
\contract(v^{\prime},v)
to obtain
N^{\prime}
and then
\phylo(N^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
N
and
N^{\prime}
be networks such that
V(N^{\prime})\subseteq V(N)
. Then,
N

and
N^{\prime}
are
(N,N^{\prime},W)
-ancestor-preserving
if for all

v,v^{\prime}\in W\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
, it holds that
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
if and only
if
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. If
N
and
N^{\prime}
are

(N,N^{\prime},V(N^{\prime}))
-ancestor-preserving, then we say for simplicity that
N

and
N^{\prime}
are
(N,N^{\prime})
-ancestor-preserving.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
(u,w)\in E(N)
be a shortcut. Then
removal of
(u,w)
in
N
results in a network
N^{\prime}
with leaf set
X
and

V(N)=V(N^{\prime})
. In particular,
\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all

v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)
and thus,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.
Moreover,
N
and
N^{\prime}
are
(N,N^{\prime})
-ancestor-preserving.

Proof.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
(u,w)
be a shortcut in
N
.
Since
(u,w)
is a shortcut, there is a
w^{\prime}\in\child(u)\setminus\{w\}

such that
w\prec_{N}w^{\prime}
. Hence, there is a
w^{\prime}w
-path
P
in
N
. Since

N
is acyclic and
w^{\prime}\prec_{N}u
,
u
is not a vertex in
P
since
otherwise
u\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
. Therefore,
w
has indegree larger than
1

in
N
. In particular, there is a
uw
-path
P^{\prime}
in
N^{\prime}
formed by the
arc
(u,w^{\prime})
and
w^{\prime}w
-path
P
. Since removal of
(u,w)
only
decreases the indegree of
w
and
\indeg_{N}(w)\geq 2
,
\rho_{N}=\rho_{N^{\prime}}

is still the only vertex with indegree
0
in
N^{\prime}
. Moreover, removal of
arcs clearly preserves acyclicity, and thus
N^{\prime}
is a rooted network.

Now let
v,v^{\prime}\in V(N)=V(N^{\prime})
. If
v\not\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
, then there is no

v^{\prime}v
-path in
N
. Clearly, removal of arcs changes nothing about this
and thus
v\not\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. Suppose now that
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}

and thus let
P_{v^{\prime}v}
be a
v^{\prime}v
-path in
N
. If
P_{v^{\prime}v}
does not
contain the arc
(u,w)
, then
P_{v^{\prime}v}
is still a
v^{\prime}v
-path in
N^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, the path obtained from
P_{v^{\prime}v}
by replacing
(u,w)
by the

uw
-path
P^{\prime}
is a
v^{\prime}v
-path in
N^{\prime}
. Hence,
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
holds
in both cases. In summary, we have
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
if and only if

v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
.

By the latter arguments,
N^{\prime}
is a network with leaf set
X

and we have
x\preceq_{N}v
if and only if
x\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
for all

x\in X
and all
v\in V(N)=V(N^{\prime})
. Therefore,
x\in\CC_{N}(v)
if and only
if
x\in\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
x\in X
and all
v\in V(N)=V(N^{\prime})
, and thus,

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
. Together with
V(N)=V(N^{\prime})
, this implies

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.
∎

Note that deletion of a shortcut from a phylogenetic network does not
necessarily result in a phylogenetic network.

Lemma 3.9
.

If a network
N
is shortcut-free and has no vertex of outdegree
1
,
then for every vertex
w\in V(N)\setminus\{\rho_{N}\}
, there is a vertex

v\in\child_{N}(\parent_{N}(w))
such that
v
and
w
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. In this case,
N
is phylogenetic.

Proof.

Since
N
is shortcut-free, siblings
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)
,
v^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime\prime}

are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Thus there is
v\in\child_{N}(\parent_{N}(w))

that is
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable with
w
if and only if

\parent_{N}(w)\neq\emptyset
and
\outdeg(\parent_{N}(w))>1
. Both conditions are
satisfied by assumption.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
(u,w)\in E(N)
be an arc that is not a
shortcut. Then,
\contract(u,w)
applied on
N
results in a network

N^{\prime}
with leaf set
X
or
X\setminus\{w\}
and

V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
. Moreover, for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V(N^{\prime})
,

1.

v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
implies
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
, and

2.

v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
implies
(i)
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
or
(ii)
w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
and
v\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
for some

w^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
that is
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
with
w
.

In particular,
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
always implies
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
or
v
and

v^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.

Proof.

The proof is rather lengthy and technical and is, therefore, placed to
Section
A.1
in the Appendix.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
(u,w)\in E(N)
such that

\outdeg_{N}(u)=1
. Then
\contract(u,w)
results in a network
N^{\prime}
with
leaf set
X
and
V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
that is

(N,N^{\prime})
-ancestor-preserving.
Moreover,
\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
and,
in particular,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
(u,w)\in E(N)
such that
\outdeg_{N}(u)=1
.
Since an arc
(u,w)\in E(N)
with
\outdeg_{N}(u)=1
cannot be a
shortcut and satisfies
\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}=\emptyset
, and thus
condition (ii) in Lemma
3.10
cannot occur,
N^{\prime}
is a
network with leaf set
X
or
X\setminus\{w\}
and

(N,N^{\prime})
-ancestor-preserving. Moreover, since
w
is the only
out-neighbor of
u
, we do not add any out-neighbors for
w
. Hence,
N^{\prime}

has leaf set
X
.

By the latter argument,
N^{\prime}
is a network with leaf
set
X
and we have
x\preceq_{N}v
if and only if
x\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
for
all
x\in X
and all
v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
. Therefore,

x\in\CC_{N}(v)
if and only if
x\in\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
holds for all
x\in X

and all
v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
. Hence, we have

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
.
Moreover, since
w
is the unique out-neighbor of
u
,
one can easily verify that
\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(w)
(cf. Obs.
3.36
for further arguments) and thus

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w)\in\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. Taken together, we obtain

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.8

and
3.11
, we obtain

Corollary 3.12
.

Every least-resolved network
N
is shortcut-free and does not
contain vertices
v
with
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
N
be a network and
N^{\prime}
be obtained from
N
by applying

\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)
. Then
N^{\prime}
is a network such that
N

and
N^{\prime}
are
(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving.
Moreover,
\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in V(N)\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
and, in particular, it holds
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, if
N
is phylogenetic, then
N^{\prime}
is phylogenetic if and only if

w
is a hybrid vertex and
\outdeg_{N}(w)\neq 1
.

Proof.

Let
N
be a network on
X
. We show first that
N^{\prime}
is a network. By
construction,
w
is the only vertex in
N
whose in-neighborhood changes
and it has the new vertex
u
as its unique in-neighbor in
N^{\prime}
. If
w\neq\rho_{N}
, then
w
has at least one in-neighbor in
N
, which becomes an
in-neighbor of
u
. Hence,
\rho_{N}
is still the only vertex with
indegree
0
in
N^{\prime}
. If
w=\rho_{N}
, then it has no in-neighbors in
N

and thus
u
has no in-neighbors in
N^{\prime}
. Together with the fact that
w

no longer has indegree
0
,
u
is the only vertex with indegree
0
in

N^{\prime}
in this case. Now assume that
N^{\prime}
contains a directed cycle
K

comprising the vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}
,
k\geq 2
, in this order,
i.e.,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
(v_{k},v_{1})
are arcs in

N^{\prime}
. If all arcs in
K
are in
N
, then
K
is a directed cycle in
N
;
a contradiction. If
K
contains an arc that is not in
N
, then
K

must contain the new vertex
u
since all new arcs are incident with

u
. Suppose w.l.o.g. that
u=v_{1}
. Since
u
has a unique out-neighbor

w
and exactly the vertices in
\parent_{N}(w)
as in-neighbors, we must
have
v_{2}=w
and
v_{k}\in\parent_{N}(w)
, respectively. In particular, this
implies
v_{2}\neq v_{k}
and
(v_{k},v_{2})\in E(N)
. Since
u
appears in
K

at most once,
(v_{k},v_{1})
and
(v_{1},v_{2})
are the only arcs in
K
that
are incident with
u
, and thus all other arcs of
K
are also arcs in

N
. In particular, there is a
v_{2}v_{k}
-path in
N
. Together with the
fact that
(v_{k},v_{2})\in E(N)
, this implies that
N
contains a directed
cycle; a contradiction. Therefore,
N^{\prime}
must be acyclic. Since moreover

N^{\prime}
has a unique root, it is a network.

The operation
\expand(w)
on a network
N
creates a network
N^{\prime}
with
an additional vertex
u
such that
w
is the unique out-neighbor of
u

and
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(u)=\parent_{N}(w)
. Therefore,
N
is recovered from

N^{\prime}
by applying
\contract(u,w)
. This observation together with
Lemma.
3.11
implies that
N
and
N^{\prime}
are

(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving.

Suppose now that
N
is phylogenetic. Assume first that
w
is a hybrid
vertex and
\outdeg_{N}(w)\neq 1
. Then, by construction, the newly
created vertex
w
satisfies
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\indeg_{N}(w)\geq 2
and
moreover, we have
\outdeg_{N^{\prime}}(w)=\outdeg_{N}(w)\neq 2
. The only other
vertices whose neighborhoods are affected are the vertices

u\in\parent_{N}(w)
. More precisely, their outneighbor
w
is replaced by
an outneighbor
w^{\prime}
and thus
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(u)=\indeg_{N}(u)
for any

u\in\parent_{N}(w)
. Together with the fact that
N
is phylogenetic, the
latter arguments imply that there is no vertex
v\in V(N^{\prime})
with

\outdeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)=1
and
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)\leq 1
. Hence,
N^{\prime}
is
phylogenetic. Now assume that
w
is a not hybrid vertex or

\outdeg_{N}(w)=1
. If
w
is not a hybrid vertex, then

\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\indeg_{N}(w)\leq 1
. Moreover,
\outdeg_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=1

holds by construction, and thus
N^{\prime}
is not phylogenetic. If

\outdeg_{N}(w)=1
, then
\outdeg_{N^{\prime}}(w)=1
since the outneighborhood
of
w
does not change. In addition,
w^{\prime}
is the unique in-neighbor of

w
in
N^{\prime}
by construction. Hence,
N^{\prime}
is not phylogenetic. In
summary, it holds that
N^{\prime}
is phylogenetic if and only if
w
is a
hybrid vertex and
\outdeg_{N}(w)\neq 1
.

By the latter arguments,
N^{\prime}
is a network with leaf set
X
. The newly
created vertex
w^{\prime}
has a unique child
w
.
The statement “
\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in V(N)\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
and, in particular,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
”
therefore follows immediately from
Lemma
3.11

and the fact that
N
is recovered from
N^{\prime}
by applying
\contract(w^{\prime},w)
.
∎

The following result shows that the expansion operation does not introduce
shortcuts and is an immediate consequence of Lemma
A.1

in Section
A.1
.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
N
be a network and
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)
. Then
N
is shortcut-free if and only
if
N^{\prime}
is shortcut-free.

We remark that an analogue of Cor.
3.14
does not hold
for the contraction operation
\contract(u,w)
.
Fig.
2
(B) shows an example where contraction
introduces a shortcut.

3.3
Blocks

The blocks of
N
will play a key role in the following. We first establish
several technical results that will allow us efficiently reason about the
block structure of a network.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
N
be a network and
u,v\in V(N)
be two
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
vertices. Then
u
and
v
are connected by an undirected path
P
that
contains at least 3 vertices and of which all inner vertices
w
satisfy

u\prec_{N}w
or
v\prec_{N}w
. In addition, we have
w\not\preceq_{N}u

and
w\not\preceq_{N}v
for every such inner vertex
w
.

Proof.

There are directed paths
P_{u}
and
P_{v}
from
\rho_{N}
to both
u
and

v
, respectively. Let
w^{*}
be the
\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex of
P_{u}

that is also a vertex of
P_{v}
, which exists since at least
\rho_{N}
is
contained in both paths. It must hold that
w^{*}\notin\{u,v\}
since
otherwise
u
and
v
would be
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. In particular,

u\prec_{N}w^{*}
and
v\prec_{N}w^{*}
. Let
P^{\prime}_{u}
and
P^{\prime}_{v}
be the
subpaths of
P_{u}
and
P_{v}
from
w^{*}
to
u
and
v
, respectively. By
construction,
P^{\prime}_{u}
and
P^{\prime}_{v}
only have vertex
w^{*}
in common, which
moreover is an outer vertex of both paths. Now consider the path
P

that is the union of the underlying undirected version of
P^{\prime}_{u}
and

P^{\prime}_{v}
. By construction,
P
contains at least the three vertices
u
,

v
, and
w^{*}
and all of its inner vertices
w
satisfy
u\prec_{N}w
or

v\prec_{N}w
. Assume, for contradiction, that
w\preceq_{N}u
for some of
these inner vertices. Since
u\prec_{N}w
is not possible, we must have

v\prec_{N}w
. But then
v\prec_{N}w
and
w\preceq_{N}u
imply that
v

and
u
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable; a contradiction. Hence,

w\not\preceq_{N}u
must hold. One shows analogously that

w\not\preceq_{N}v
.
∎

Paths of the form described in Lemma
3.15
connecting two
leaves
u
and
v
are called “up-down-paths” in
[
13
]
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
B
be a block in a network
N
and
u,v\in V(B)
such that

v\preceq_{N}u
. Then every
uv
-path in
N
is completely contained in

B
.

Proof.

Let
P
be a
uv
-path in
N
, which exists since
v\preceq_{N}u
. The
statement holds trivially if
B
is an isolated vertex,
v=u
, or
B
is
the arc
(u,v)
. Thus suppose
B
is a non-trivial block. Suppose, for
contradiction, there is a vertex
w\in V(P)\setminus\{V(B)\}
. Let

w_{a}
and
w_{d}
be the
\preceq_{N}
-minimal ancestor and the

\preceq_{N}
-maximal descendant, resp., of
w
in
P
(both of which
exist since
u,v\in V(P)
). Consider the subpath
P^{\prime}
of
P
from
w_{a}

to
w_{d}
. By Prop.
2.5
, the subgraph of
N
obtained by
adding
P^{\prime}
to
B
is again biconnected. Together with
w\in V(P^{\prime})\setminus\{V(B)\}
, this contradict that
B
is a block. Hence such
a vertex cannot exist. Therefore and since blocks are always induced
subgraphs, the statement follows.
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Every block
B
in a network
N
has a unique
\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex

\max B
. In particular, for every
v\in V(B)
, there is a directed path
from
\max B
to
v
and every such path is completely contained in
B
.

Proof.

The statement is trivial for a block that consists only of a single
vertex or arc. Otherwise, suppose there are two distinct

\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in
B
. By assumption,

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
must be
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. By
Lemma
3.15
,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are connected by an
undirected path
P
that contains at least 3 vertices and of which all
inner vertices
w
satisfy
u\prec_{N}w
or
v\prec_{N}w
. By

\preceq_{N}
-maximality of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, none of these inner vertices
can be contained in
B
. By Prop.
2.5
, adding
P
to
B

preserves biconnectivity, and thus
B
is not a maximal biconnected
subgraph; a contradiction. In particular, for every
v\in V(B)
, we have

v\preceq_{N}\max B
, i.e., there is a path from
\max B
to
v
and by
Lemma
3.16
, each every such path is completely
contained in
B
.
∎

Corollary 3.18
.

If
B
is a non-trivial block in network
N
, then
\max B
has at least
two out-neighbors in
B
.

Proof.

Since
B
is non-trivial,
\max B
lies on an undirected cycle in
B
and
thus is incident with two distinct vertices in
B
. By

\preceq_{N}
-maximality of
\max B
in
B
, these must be out-neighbors of

\max B
.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
N
be a network and suppose that
v\in V(N)
is contained in the
blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
of
N
. If
v\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
, then

B=B^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume that vertex
v
is contained in the blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
of
N
but

v\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
. By Lemma
3.17
, there
exists a directed path
P
in
B
from
\max B
to
v
. Similarly, there
is a directed path
P^{\prime}
in
B^{\prime}
from
\max B^{\prime}
to
v
. Since

v\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
, both
P
and
P^{\prime}
contain at least one
arc.

Assume first that
P
and
P^{\prime}
share an arc
e
and thus, that
B
and

B^{\prime}
share the arc
e
. In this case, contraposition of Obs.
2.2
implies that
B=B^{\prime}
. Hence, in the
following we assume that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are arc-disjoint.

Consider first the case
\max B^{\prime}\preceq_{N}\max B
. Let
u
be the
unique
\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex in
P
such that

\max B^{\prime}\preceq_{N}u
. Together with
v\prec_{N}\max B^{\prime}
, this implies
that
u\neq v
. Let
P_{u,v}
be the subpath of
P
from
u
to
v
and
note that
P_{u,v}
contains at least one arc. Since

\max B^{\prime}\preceq_{N}u
, we can find a directed path
P_{u,\max B^{\prime}}

(possible only containing a single vertex
u=\max B^{\prime}
) from
u
to

\max B^{\prime}
. The paths
P_{u,v}
and
P_{u,\max B^{\prime}}
only have vertex
u

in common since
u
is the unique
\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex in
P
with

\max B^{\prime}\preceq_{N}u
. Since
N
is acyclic,
P_{u,\max B^{\prime}}
and
P^{\prime}

are arc-disjoint. In summary,
P^{\prime}
,
P_{u,v}
, and
P_{u,\max B^{\prime}}
are
pairwise arc-disjoint. Hence,
\max B^{\prime}
and
v
are connected by two
arc-disjoint undirected paths that correspond to
P^{\prime}
and the union of

P_{u,v}
and
P_{u,\max B^{\prime}}
. Therefore,
\max B^{\prime}
and
v
are
contained in a common block
B^{\prime\prime}
. In particular,
B
and
B^{\prime\prime}
share
all arcs in
P_{u,v}
, and thus at least one arc. Similarly,
B^{\prime}
and

B^{\prime\prime}
share all arcs in
P^{\prime}
, and thus at least one arc. By Obs.
2.2
, it follows that
B=B^{\prime\prime}=B^{\prime}
. Similarly,

\max B\preceq_{N}\max B^{\prime}
implies
B=B^{\prime}
.

Suppose now that
\max B
and
\max B^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.
Recall that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are arc-disjoint and each contain at least one
arc. Let
\breve{P}
be the undirected path corresponding to the union
of
P
and
P^{\prime}
and observe that all of its inner vertices
w
that
satisfy
w\preceq_{N}\max B
or
w\preceq_{N}\max B^{\prime}
. Since
\max B
and

\max B^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, Lemma
3.15

implies that they are connected by an undirected path
\invbreve{P}
that
contains at least 3 vertices and of which all inner vertices
w^{\prime}
satisfy

w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}\max B
and
w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}\max B^{\prime}
. As a
consequence,
\breve{P}
and
\invbreve{P}
only have their endpoints

\max B
and
\max B^{\prime}
in common. Hence,
\max B
and
\max B^{\prime}
are
contained in a common block
B^{\prime\prime}
. In particular,
B
and
B^{\prime\prime}
share
all arcs in
P
, and thus at least one arc. Similarly,
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}

share all arcs in
P^{\prime}
, and thus at least one arc. By Obs.
2.2
, it follows that
B=B^{\prime\prime}=B^{\prime}
.
∎

By definition,
N
is tree if and only if it contains no undirected cycle,
i.e., if all blocks are trivial. Thus
N
is a tree if and only if there
are no hybrid vertices.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
N
be a network and
B
a non-trivial block in
N
with
terminal vertices
\{m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{h}\}
,
h\geq 1
. Then

B^{0}\coloneqq B\setminus\{\max B,m_{1},m_{2},\dots m_{h}\}

(1)

is the
interior
of
B
.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.19
, we have

Observation 3.21
.

Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be two distinct blocks in
N
. Then
B_{1}^{0}\cap B_{2}^{0}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
N
be a network and
w\in V(N)
be a hybrid vertex. Then
w
and all
of its in-neighbors are contained in a non-trivial block
B
.

Proof.

Let
w
be a hybrid vertex, i.e.,
\indeg_{N}(w)\geq 2
, and let
v

and
v^{\prime}
be two distinct in-neighbors of
w
. If
v^{\prime}\prec_{N}v
, then there is
a directed path
P
from
v
to
v^{\prime}
that contains at least one arc.
Moreover,
w
is not a vertex of
P
since otherwise
v^{\prime}\preceq_{N}w
would
contradict
w\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. Therefore,
P
together with
w
and arcs
vw
and

v^{\prime}w
form an undirected cycle. An analogous argument applies if

v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. If
v
and
v^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, then
Lemma
3.15
implies that they are connected by an
undirected path
P
that contains at least 3 vertices and of which all
inner vertices
w^{\prime}
satisfy
w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}v
and
w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
.
Together with
w\prec_{N}v,v^{\prime}
, this implies that
w
is not contained in

P
. Therefore,
P
together with
w
and arcs
vw
and
v^{\prime}w
form an
undirected cycle. In summary, in all cases,
w
is contained in a
non-trivial block
B_{v^{\prime}}
that, in particular, also contains
v
,
v^{\prime}
,
and the arc
vw
. Since
v^{\prime}
was chosen arbitrarily among the
in-neighbors of
w
that are distinct from
v
and the blocks
B_{v^{\prime}}

for all of these vertices share the arc
vw
,
Obs.
2.1
implies that
w
and all of its
in-neighbors are contained in a non-trivial block
B
.
∎

Following
[
37
]
, we say that a hybrid vertex
w
is

properly contained
in a block
B
if all of its in-neighbors are
contained in
B
. As an immediate consequence of
Lemma
3.22
, every hybrid vertex is properly
contained in exactly one block.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
N
be a network,
w
a hybrid vertex in
N
, and
B
be a block of
N
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

1.

w
is properly contained in
B
, i.e.,
w
and all of its parents
are contained in
B
.

2.

w
and one of its parents
u
are contained in
B
.

3.

w\in V(B)\setminus\{\max B\}
.

Proof.

(3)
\implies
(2).

Since
\max B
is the unique
\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex in
B
, we have

w\prec_{N}\max B
. By Lemma
3.17
, there is a directed
path from
\max B
to
w
that is completely contained in
B
. Clearly,
P
contains a
parent of
w
, which is there also contained in
B
.

(2)
\implies
(1).

By Lemma
3.22
,
w
and all of its parents
are contained in a non-trivial block
B^{\prime}
.
Hence,
B
and
B^{\prime}
share the distinct vertices
w
and
u
. By
Obs.
2.1
,
B=B^{\prime}
.

(1)
\implies
(3).

If
w
and all of its (at least two) parents are contained in
B
, then
clearly
w\in V(B)\setminus\{\max B\}
.
∎

As a consequence, if a hybrid vertex
w
is contained in a block
B
but
not properly contained, then it must hold
w=\max B
. This motivates the
following definition of level-
k
networks:

Definition 3.24
.

A network
N
is
level-
k
if each block
B
of
N
contains at
most
k
hybrid vertices distinct from
\max B
.

Equivalently, by Lemma
3.22
,
N
is
level-
k
if each block
B
of
N
properly contains at most
k
hybrid
vertices. In
[
19
]
, level-
k
networks are simply defined by
having no more than
k
hybrid vertices within any given block. We note
that this is equivalent to our definition in a setting where hybrid
vertices are restricted to having outdegree
1
. Def.
3.24

also accommodates the contraction of out-arcs of hybrid vertices
v

with
\outdeg(v)=1
, see Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:
The network
N
contains a hybrid vertex
v
with

\outdeg(v)=1
. Network
N^{\prime}
is obtained from
N
by contraction of the
arc
(v,w)
, i.e., the operation
\contract(v,w)
which preserves
vertex
w
. Vertex
w
is now a hybrid vertex that is contained in two
blocks of
N^{\prime}
. However, only the upper block properly contains it.

The following two lemmas show that neither arc contraction nor expansion
increases the level of a network. Since their proofs are rather lengthy and
technical, they are given in Section
A.1
in the Appendix.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
N
be a network,
(w^{\prime},w)\in E(N)
be an arc that is not a
shortcut, and
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\contract(w^{\prime},w)
. If
N
is level-
k
, then
N^{\prime}
is also level-
k
.

Proof.

See Section
A.1
in the Appendix.
∎

Figure 4:
The level-
3
network
N^{\prime}
is obtained from the level-
6

network by application of
\contract(w^{\prime},w)
. The hybrid vertices are
highlighted in orange.

The converse of Lemma
3.25
, however, is not true as
shown by the example in Fig.
4
. This example
also shows that even mitigated versions “
if
N^{\prime}
is level-
k
, then

N
is level-
(k+1)
” do not hold. As an immediate consequence of the
definition of
\phylo(N)
, Lemma
3.11
and

3.25
, we obtain

Corollary 3.26
.

Let
N
be a level-
k
network. Then the network
N^{\prime}
obtained by
operation
\phylo(N)
is a phylogenetic level-
k
network such that

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
N
be a network and
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)
. Then
N
is level-
k
if and only if

N^{\prime}
is level-
k
.

Proof.

See Section
A.1
in the Appendix.
∎

The definition of
\phylo(N)
and
\contract^{\star}(v^{\prime},v)
and
Lemma
3.25
yield

Corollary 3.28
.

Let
N
be a level-
k
network. If
N^{\prime}
is the the network obtained from

N
by applying
\phylo(N)
or
\contract^{\star}(v^{\prime},v)
for some arc

(v^{\prime},v)\in E(N)
that is not a shortcut, then
N^{\prime}
is phylogenetic and
level-
k
.

3.4
Clusters, Hasse Diagrams, and Regular Networks

In this section we consider general properties of the set of clusters

\mathscr{C}_{N}
of a phylogenetic network as specified in
Def.
3.4
above.

Lemma 3.29
.

For all networks
N
on
X
, the set
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a clustering
system.

Proof.

Every non-leaf vertex
v\in V\setminus X
has at least one out-neighbor
and
N
is acyclic and finite. Thus every directed path in
N
can be
extended to a directed path that eventually ends in a leaf, implying

\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
. Since
\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
for all
v\in V
and since

N
contains at least a root
\rho_{N}
as a vertex, we have

\emptyset\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
and thus Condition (i) holds. Since

v\preceq_{N}\rho_{N}
for all
v\in V
, we have
\CC(\rho_{N})=X
and (ii) is
satisfied. To see that Condition (iii) holds, observe that for all

x\in X
, we have
\outdeg(x)=0
and thus
\CC(x)=\{x\}
.
∎

This simple observation connects phylogenetic networks to a host of
literature on clustering systems, which have been studied with motivations
often unrelated to evolution or phylogenetics

[
41
,
7
,
38
]
.

A particular difficulty in the characterization of certain types of
networks by means of their clustering systems is that even rather simple
clustering systems such as hierarchies can be explained by very
complex networks.

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then, for all
k\in\{0,2,\dots,n\}
, there
is a phylogenetic, shortcut-free level-
k
network
N
on
n
leaves that
is not level-
(k-1)
such that
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

Proof.

If
n=1
, then
k=0
and the single vertex graphs serves as an example
(since a network contains at least one vertex and thus a level-
(-1)

cannot exist by definition).
Let
n\geq 2
. For
k=0
, simply take a tree whose root is adjacent to
the
n
leaves only. Again, this tree is level-
0
but not level-
(-1)
.
We refer to this tree as a star tree. For
k\geq 2
, take a star tree
T
and randomly collect
k
of its leaves

l_{1},\dots l_{k}
. Now add new leaves
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
and edges such that
the induced subgraph
N[\{l_{1},\dots l_{k},x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
is graph
isomorphic to a complete bipartite graphs where one part of the
bipartition contains all
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
and the other part
all
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
(see Fig.
5

for a generic example).
It is easy to verify that
N
is shortcut-free,
phylogenetic, level-
k
but not level-
(k-1)
. In all cases,
\mathscr{C}_{N}

just consist of the clusters
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
,
X
, and the singletons

\{x\}
,
x\in X
and is, therefore, a hierarchy.
∎

Figure 5:
A generic framework that shows that, for every
n\geq 2
and

k\in\{0,2,\dots,n\}
, there is a phylogenetic, shortcut-free level-
k

network
N
on
n
leaves that is not level-
(k-1)
and where

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy. The network shown left is level-0 and its
clustering system is trivially a hierarchy. The clustering system

\mathscr{C}_{N}
of the level-
k
network
N
shown right consists of the
clusters
X^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
(for which the corresponding
vertices are highlighted in orange),

X=X^{\prime}\cup\{l_{k+1},\dots,l_{n}\}
, and the
singletons and, therefore,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

As we shall in Lemma
7.3
there is no
phylogenetic shortcut-free level-1 network
N
(that is not a tree) for
which
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

For a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
and a subset
A\subseteq 2^{X}
,
we define the
closure operator
as the map
\cl\colon 2^{X}\to 2^{X}

defined by

\cl(A)\coloneqq\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathscr{C}\\
A\subseteq C\end{subarray}}C.

(2)

It is well defined, isotonic
[
A\subseteq B\implies\cl(A)\subseteq\cl(B)
], enlarging
[
A\subseteq\cl(A)
], and idempotent
\cl(\cl(A))=\cl(A)
. For
|X|>1
, we have
\cl(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

Definition 3.31
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is

closed
if, for all
non-empty

A\in 2^{X}
, the following
condition holds:
\cl(A)=A\iff A\in\mathscr{C}
.

The following result is well know in the clustering literature.

Lemma 3.32
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is closed if and only if

A,B\in\mathscr{C}
and
A\cap B\neq\emptyset
implies

A\cap B\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

For completeness, a proof is provided in Section
A.2
in
the Appendix.
∎

We continue with three simple observations concerning the clusters of
networks.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
N
be a network. Then
v\preceq_{N}w
implies
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(w)
.

Proof.

By construction,
x\in\CC(v)
if and only if
x\in X
and
v
lies on a
directed path from the root
\rho_{N}
to
x
. Furthermore,
v\preceq_{N}w

implies that
w
lies on a directed path from
\rho_{N}
to
v
. By (N1)
and since
N
is a DAG, there is directed path from
\rho_{N}
to
x
that
contains
w
, and thus
x\preceq_{N}w
, i.e.,
x\in\CC(w)
.
∎

We note in passing that the converse of Lemma
3.33
is not
always satisfied (even in level-
1
networks): If
v
is a hybrid vertex
with unique child
w
, we have
\CC(v)=\CC(w)
and thus

\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(w)
, but
v\not\preceq_{N}w
, (cf. the network
N
in
Fig.
3
). A result similar to Lemma
3.15

ensures the existence of a path
P
connecting
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
vertices
u,v\in V(N)
that contains only vertices that are below
u
or

v
. However, it requires that
u
and
v
have at least one descendant
leaf in common, i.e., that
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
:

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
N
be a network and
u,v\in V(N)
be two
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
vertices such that
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
. Then, for every

x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
,
u
and
v
are connected by an undirected path

P=(w_{1}\coloneqq u,\dots,w_{h},\dots,w_{k}\coloneqq v)
,
1<h<k
, such
that

(i)

(w_{i},w_{i+1})\in E(N)
for all
1\leq i<h
,

(w_{i+1},w_{i})\in E(N)
for all
h\leq i<k
, and
w_{h}
is a hybrid
vertex satisfying
w_{h}\prec_{N}u
and
w_{h}\prec_{N}v
.

(ii)

x\in\CC(w_{h})
,

In particular,
k\geq 3
,
all inner vertices
w_{i}
of
P
satisfy
w_{i}\prec_{N}u
or
w_{i}\prec_{N}v
,
and
P
is a subgraph of a non-trivial block
B
.

Proof.

There are directed paths
P_{u}
and
P_{v}
from
u
and
v
, respectively,
to the leaf
x
. Let
w^{*}
be the
\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex of
P_{u}

that is also a vertex of
P_{v}
, which exists since at least
x
is
contained in both paths. It must hold that
w^{*}\notin\{u,v\}
since
otherwise
u
and
v
would be
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. In particular,

w^{*}\prec_{N}u
and
w^{*}\prec_{N}v
. Let
P^{\prime}_{u}
and
P^{\prime}_{v}
be the subpaths
of
P_{u}
and
P_{v}
from
u
and
v
, respectively, to
w^{*}
. By
construction,
w^{*}
must be a hybrid vertex,
x\in\CC(w^{*})
, and
P^{\prime}_{u}

and
P^{\prime}_{v}
only have vertex
w^{*}
in common, which moreover is an outer
vertex of both paths. Now consider the path

P=(w_{1}\coloneqq u,\dots,w_{h}\coloneqq w^{*},\dots,w_{k}\coloneqq v)
, that
is the union of the underlying undirected version of
P^{\prime}_{u}
and
P^{\prime}_{v}
.
It is now easy to verify that
P
satisfies all of the desired
properties. Two see that
P
is a subgraph of a non-trivial block
B
,
observe that, by Lemma
3.15
, the two

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable vertices
u
and
v
are connected by an
undirected path
\invbreve{P}
that contains at least 3 vertices and of
which all inner vertices
w^{\prime}
satisfy
w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}u
and

w^{\prime}\not\preceq_{N}v
. Hence,
P
and
\invbreve{P}
cannot have any inner
vertices in common. Therefore,
u
and
v
are connected by two distinct
paths that both have at least 3 vertices and that only have the endpoints

u
and
v
in common. Hence,
u
and
v
lie on a cycle
K
and thus in
a common block
B
of
N
. In particular,
P
is a subgraph of
K
and
thus of
B
.
∎

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
N
be a network and
u,v\in V(N)
. If
\CC_{N}(u)
and
\CC_{N}(v)

overlap, then
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable and

u,v\in B^{0}
for a non-trivial block
B
of
N
.

Proof.

If two clusters
\CC_{N}(u)
and
\CC_{N}(v)
overlap, then
Lemma
3.33
implies that
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Lemma
3.34
implies
that
u
and
v
are contained in
a common non-trivial block
B
of
N
.
Since
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(\max B)
for all
u\in B
,
\CC(\max B)

does not overlap any cluster
\CC(v)
with
v\in B
, and thus
u,v\neq\max B
. Now suppose
u
is a terminal vertex of
B
and
v\in B
such
that
\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)
overlap. By Lemma
3.34
there
is
w\in B
in
x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
with
w\prec_{N}u
, contradicting
that
u
is terminal. Therefore
u,v\in B^{0}
.
∎

Clusters of outdegree-1 vertices
w
are redundant in the sense that every
directed path from
w
to one of its descendant leaves necessarily passes
through the unique child
v
of
w
. Thus we have
\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(v)
.
Moreover,
v\prec_{N}w
and Lemma
3.33
imply
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(w)
, and thus,
\CC(v)=\CC(w)
. Hence, we have

Observation 3.36
.

Let
N
be a network. If
v
is the unique child of
w
in
N
, then

\CC(v)=\CC(w)
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
N
be a network,
B
a block in
N
and
u,v\in V(B)
. Moreover, let

H
be the set of hybrid vertices
h
that are properly contained in
B

and satisfy
h\preceq_{N}u,v
. Then it holds

\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\in\{\CC(u),\CC(v),\bigcup_{h\in H}\CC(h)\}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\notin\{\CC(u),\CC(v)\}
implies

\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\bigcup_{h\in H}\CC(h)\eqqcolon C
. Hence, suppose

\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\notin\{\CC(u),\CC(v)\}
. Then Lemma
3.33

implies that
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. If
x\in C
,
then
x\in\CC(h)
for some
h\in H
. Since
h\preceq_{N}u,v
,
Lemma
3.33
implies
\CC(h)\subseteq\CC(u)
and

\CC(h)\subseteq\CC(v)
and thus,
x\in\CC(h)\subseteq\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
. Now
suppose
x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
. By Lemma
3.34
, the

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable vertices
u
and
v
are connected by an
undirected path
P
which contains a hybrid vertex
h\prec_{N}u,v
with

x\in\CC(h)
and is a subgraph of a non-trivial block
B^{\prime}
of
N
. Since

B
and
B^{\prime}
share the two distinct vertices
u
and
v
,
Obs.
2.1
implies
B=B^{\prime}
. In particular,

u,v,h\in V(B)
and
h\prec_{N}u,v
imply
h\neq\max B
, and thus,
h

must be properly contained in
B
by
Lemma
3.22
. Hence, we have
h\in H
and
thus
x\in\CC(h)\subseteq C
. In summary, we have
x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)

if and only if
x\in C
, and thus
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=C
.
∎

Note that
H=\emptyset
and thus,
C=\bigcup_{h\in H}\CC(h)=\emptyset

in Lemma
3.37
is possible.

The
Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
of

\mathscr{C}
w.r.t. to set inclusion is a DAG whose vertices are the
clusters in
\mathscr{C}
. There is a directed arc
(C,C^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{H}
if

C^{\prime}\subsetneq C
and there is no
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
with
C^{\prime}\subsetneq C^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq C
. Since
X\in\mathscr{C}
, the Hasse diagram is connected
and has
X
as its unique root. The singletons
\{x\}
,
x\in X
, are
exactly the inclusion-minimal vertices in
\mathscr{C}
and thus, they have
outdegree
0
but not necessarily indegree
1
in
\mathfrak{H}
. Another simple
property of
\mathfrak{H}
is the following:

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system on
X
. Then every non-singleton set

C\in\mathscr{C}
satisfies
\outdeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(C)\geq 2
in the Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}
of
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
C\in\mathscr{C}
be a non-singleton set, i.e.,
|C|\geq 2
.
Therefore and since
\{x\}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
x\in X
, there is a
directed path in
\mathfrak{H}
from
C
to some singleton set

\{x^{\prime}\}\in\mathscr{C}
. In particular, this path contains at least the two
distinct clusters
C
and
\{x^{\prime}\}
, and thus,
C
has a child
C^{\prime}
in

\mathfrak{H}
with
\{x^{\prime}\}\subseteq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C
.
Now pick an element
x^{\prime\prime}\in C\setminus C^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Since

\{x^{\prime\prime}\}\in\mathscr{C}
and
\{x^{\prime\prime}\}\subseteq C
, we can argue similarly
as before to conclude that
C
has a child
C^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathfrak{H}
with

\{x^{\prime\prime}\}\subseteq C^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq C
.
Since
x^{\prime\prime}\notin C^{\prime}
, we have
C^{\prime}\neq C^{\prime\prime}
and thus,
C
satisfies

\outdeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(C)\geq 2
.
∎

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system on
X
with corresponding Hasse
diagram
\mathfrak{H}
. Then
\mathfrak{H}
is a phylogenetic network with leaf set

X_{\mathfrak{H}}\coloneqq\{\{x\}\mid x\in X\}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathfrak{H}
is a DAG. Since
X\in\mathscr{C}
and
C\subseteq X

for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
,
X
is the unique cluster in
\mathscr{C}

with indegree 0, i.e.,
X
is the root in
\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathfrak{H}
satisfies
(N1). By definition of clustering systems, we have

X_{\mathfrak{H}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
. Now consider a cluster

\{x\}\in X_{\mathfrak{H}}
. Since
\emptyset\notin\mathscr{C}
,
\{x\}
has
outdegree zero in
\mathfrak{H}
. Lemma
3.38
implies

\outdeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(C)\geq 2
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
with
|C|>1
,
i.e.,
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}\setminus X_{\mathfrak{H}}
. Taken together, the
latter arguments imply that the elements in
X_{\mathfrak{H}}
are exactly the
leaves of
\mathfrak{H}
and that (N2) is satisfied.
∎

For a given a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
and a cluster

C\in\mathscr{C}
, we will moreover make use of the subsets

\mathcal{D}(C)\coloneqq\{D\in\mathscr{C}\mid D\subsetneq C\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)\coloneqq\{D\in\mathscr{C}\mid D\not\subseteq C\}.

(3)

Note that, by definition, we have

\mathcal{D}(C)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{C\}=\mathscr{C}

for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
,
\mathcal{D}(C)=\emptyset
if and only if
C
is a
singleton, and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\emptyset
if and only if
C=X
.

Lemma 3.40
.

Let
\mathfrak{H}
be the Hasse diagram of a clustering system
\mathscr{C}

and
C\in\mathscr{C}
such that
C
does not overlap any other set. Then,
there is no undirected cycle in
\mathfrak{H}
that intersects both

\mathcal{D}(C)
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
. In particular, if

C\neq X
and
|C|>1
, then
C
is a cut vertex in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C
does not overlap with any other cluster. If
C=X
then,

\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\emptyset
and if
|C|=1
then

\mathcal{D}(C)=\emptyset
and thus, for any cycle
K
in
\mathfrak{H}
we
have
K\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\emptyset
or
K\cap{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\emptyset
. Hence,
K
cannot intersect both. Now,
assume that
C\neq X
and
|C|>1
. Since
C
is neither a singleton
nor

X
, both
\mathcal{D}(C)
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
are
non-empty. Furthermore,
\mathcal{D}(C)\cup\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C\}
. Let
C_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(C)
and

C_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
. By assumption, we have
C_{1}\subsetneq C
and either (i)
C_{2}\cap C=\emptyset
or (ii)
C\subsetneq C_{2}
. In
case (i), we have
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\emptyset
and in case (ii), it holds

C_{1}\subsetneq C\subsetneq C_{2}
. Therefore and since

C_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(C)
and
C_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
were chosen
arbitrarily,
\mathfrak{H}
contains no arc connecting a cluster in

\mathcal{D}(C)
and a cluster in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
. Together
with

\mathcal{D}(C)\cup\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)=\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C\}
,
this implies that the subgraph of
\mathfrak{H}
obtained by removing
C
is
disconnected and thus,
C
is a cut vertex. In particular, every
undirected path connecting a cluster in
\mathcal{D}(C)
and a cluster in

\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
has to pass through
C
and thus the second
statement of the lemma follows as an immediate consequence.
∎

Figure 6:
Both the rooted
K_{2,3}
(A) and
K_{3,3}
(B) are a
phylogenetic networks that have only two leaves, denoted
x
and
y

here. The clustering system therefore consists only of
X=\{x,y\}
and
the two singletons
\{x\}
and
\{y\}
. The same clustering system can
be represented by a rooted tree with a single root that is adjacent to
the two leaves
x
and
y
. In particular, both networks do not satisfy
(PCC) since the highlighted vertices are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable but
share the cluster
\{x,y\}
. (C) A network showing that

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(u)
is also possible for two

\preceq
-incomparable vertices
u
and
v
.

Every phylogenetic tree
T
is isomorphic to the Hasse diagram of its
clustering systems
\mathscr{C}
by virtue of the map

\varphi\colon V(T)\to\mathscr{C},\,v\mapsto\CC(v)
, see e.g.
[
48
]
. Fig.
6
shows that this is not the
case for phylogenetic networks in general. The rooted networks that share
this property with phylogenetic trees have been introduced and studied in

[
5
,
6
,
56
]
.

Definition 3.41
.

[
5
]

A network
N=(V,E)
is
regular
if the map

\varphi\colon V\to V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N}))\colon v\mapsto\CC(v)
is a
graph isomorphism between
N
and
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
.

The graph isomorphism in Def.
3.41
is quite constrained. In
particular, it is not obvious that an arbitrary graph isomorphism
\varphi

between the two networks
N
and
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
implies that
N

is regular, as
\varphi(v)\neq\CC(v)
may be possible. As we shall see in
Cor.
4.11
, however,
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
if and only
if
N
is regular. Even more, as noted without proof in
[
5
]
,
a rooted network
N
with leaf set
X
is regular if and only if it is
graph isomorphic to the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
for some
clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
. This result will be an
immediate consequence of the results established below and will be
summarized and proven in Prop.
4.10
.

Proposition 3.42
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
, there is a unique regular
network
N
with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system on
X
. By
Lemma
3.39
,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a network with
leaf set
X_{\mathfrak{H}}\coloneqq\{\{x\}\mid x\in X\}
.
Replacing all leaves
\{x\}
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
with the single
element
x
that they contain clearly yields a network
N
such that

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
and
\varphi\colon V(N)\to V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}))\colon v\mapsto\CC_{N}(v)
is an isomorphism
between
N
and
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. By definition,
N
is regular.

Now let
N^{\prime}
be a regular network with
\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathscr{C}
,
i.e, there is an isomorphism

\varphi^{\prime}\colon V(N^{\prime})\to V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}))\colon v\mapsto\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)

between
N^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.
In particular, we have
\varphi(x)=\varphi^{\prime}(x)=\{x\}
for all
x\in X
and
thus
\varphi^{\prime-1}(\varphi(x))=x
.
Hence,
\varphi^{\prime-1}\circ\varphi
is an isomorphism between
N
and
N

that is the identity on
X
. Hence,
N
is the unique regular network with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
.
∎

Remark 1
.

By a slight abuse of notation, we also write
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
for the
unique regular network of a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
since it is
obtained from the Hasse diagram by relabeling all leaves
\{x\}
with
x
.

The following characterization is a slight rephrasing of Prop. 4.1 in

[
5
]
:

Proposition 3.43
.

A network
N
is regular if and only if

(i)

\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)\iff u\preceq_{N}v
for all
u,v\in V
,
and

(ii)

N
is shortcut-free.

Proof.

Prop. 4.1 in
[
5
]
states that
N
is regular if and only if
the following three conditions hold: (a)
u\neq v
implies
\CC(u)\neq\CC(v)
, i.e.,
\CC(u)=\CC(v)\implies u=v
; (b) if
\CC(u)\subsetneq\CC(v)
, then there is a directed path from
v
to
u
, i.e.,
u\prec_{N}v
; and (c) if there are two distinct directed paths connecting
u
and

v
, then neither path consists of a single arc, i.e.,
(u,v)
is not a
shortcut. Clearly, conditions (ii) and (c) are equivalent. It therefore
suffices to show that condition (i) holds if and only if conditions (a)
and (b) are satisfied. Together, (a), (b) and Lemma
3.33

obviously imply (i). Now suppose (i) is satisfied. Then
\CC(u)=\CC(v)

implies
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
and
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
and thus we
have both
u\preceq_{N}v
and
v\preceq_{N}u
, and hence
u=v
, i.e., (a)
holds. Assuming
\CC(u)\subsetneq\CC(v)
, i.e,
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)

and
\CC(u)\neq\CC(v)
implies
u\preceq_{N}v
by (i) and
u\neq v
by (a),
and thus
u\prec_{N}v
, i.e., (b) holds as well.
∎

Prop.
3.42
and
3.43
imply

Corollary 3.44
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
there is a network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
such that
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)\iff u\preceq_{N}v
for all
u,v\in V
.

Corollary 3.45
.

Every regular network is least-resolved.

Proof.

Suppose, for contradiction, that the regular network
N
is not
least-resolved, i.e., there is a network
N^{\prime}
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
that can be obtained from
N
by a non-empty
sequence of shortcut removals and application of
\contract(v^{\prime},v)
.
By Prop.
3.43
,
N
is shortcut-free and, therefore,
the operation that is applied first must be a contraction.
Therefore and since no new vertices are introduced, it must hold

|V(N^{\prime})|<|V(N)|
. Since
N
is regular, it holds
|V(N)|=|\mathscr{C}_{N}|
.
Hence, we have
|V(N^{\prime})|<|\mathscr{C}_{N}|=|\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}|
; a contradiction.
∎

The converse of Cor.
3.45
, however, is not
satisfied, see Fig.
7
. The network
N
is
shortcut-free and satisfies
|V(N)|=|\mathscr{C}_{N}|
.
Hence,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is
least-resolved. The unique regular network
N^{\prime}
for
\mathscr{C}_{N}
on the
r.h.s. of Fig.
7
is not isomorphic to
N
. Hence, we
obtain

Observation 3.46
.

Not every least-resolved network is regular.

Nevertheless, for level-1 networks the terms least-resolved and regular
coincide as shown in Cor.
7.2
.

Figure 7:
The network
N
is least-resolved but
not isomorphic to
N^{\prime}\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
. Consequently,

N
is not regular.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
, by definition, is a hierarchy if and
only if, for all
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
holds
C\cap C^{\prime}\in\{\emptyset,C,C^{\prime}\}
.

Corollary 3.47
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
is a hierarchy if and only if

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a phylogenetic tree. Moreover,
N
is a
phylogenetic tree if and only if
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})\sim N
and

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

Proof.

The 1-to-1 correspondence of hierarchies and phylogenetic trees is well
known, see e.g.
[
48
, Thm. 3.5.2]
.
∎

4
Semi-Regular Networks

4.1
Path-Cluster Comparability

Regularity as characterized in Prop.
3.43
is a bit too
restrictive for our purposes. We therefore consider a slightly weaker
condition, which we will call
semi-regularity
. More precisely, we
relax condition (i) in Prop.
3.43
:

Definition 4.1
.

A network
N
has the
path-cluster-comparability (PCC)
property if
it satisfies, for all
u,v\in V(N)
,

(PCC)

u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable if and only if

\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
or
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
.

Definition 4.2
.

A network is
semi-regular
if it is shortcut-free and satisfies
(PCC).

We introduce the term “semi-regular” because, as we shall see in
Thm.
4.8
, it is a moderate generalization of regularity
that preserves many of useful properties of regular networks. We
emphasize that (PCC) is a quite restrictive property. For instance, the
rooted
K_{3,3}
in Fig.
6
(B) violates (PCC). The example
in Fig.
6
(C) shows that even
\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(u)
is
possible for two
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable vertices
u
and
v
.

Observation 4.3
.

Let
N
be a network satisfying (PCC) and
u,v\in V(N)
. Then

\CC(u)\subsetneq\CC(v)
implies
u\prec_{N}v
.

Proof.

Suppose
\CC(u)\subsetneq\CC(v)
. Then (PCC) implies that
u
and
v

are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. If
v\preceq_{N}u
, then
Lemma
3.33
implies
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
; a
contradiction. Hence, only
u\prec_{N}v
is possible.
∎

Property (PCC) still allows that distinct vertices are associated with the
same clusters. It requires, however, that such vertices lie along a common
directed path.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
N
be a network. Then
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
and
u\prec_{N}v
imply that there
is
w\in\child_{N}(v)
such that
u\preceq_{N}w\prec_{N}v
and
\CC(w)=\CC(v)
.

Proof.

Since
u\prec_{N}v
, there is a
vw
-path which passes through some child

w\in\child_{N}(v)
. Hence, we have
u\preceq_{N}w\prec_{N}v
. Lemma
3.33
implies
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(v)
. Together with
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
, this yields
\CC(v)=\CC(w)=\CC(u)
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
N
be a semi-regular network and let
v\in V(N)
. Then, there is a
vertex
u\in V(N)
with
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
and
u\prec_{N}v
if and only if
