\outdeg(v)=1
. If, moreover,
N
is phylogenetic, then
u
is the unique
child of
v
in this case.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\outdeg(v)=1
and thus let
u
be the unique child of

v
. Thus, it holds
u\prec_{N}v
and, by Obs.
3.36
,
we have
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
. Conversely, suppose that
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
and

u\prec_{N}v
. Lemma
4.4
implies that there is

w\in\child(v)
with
u\preceq_{N}w\prec_{N}v
and
\CC(w)=\CC(v)
. Suppose
there is another child
w^{\prime}\in\child(v)
with
w^{\prime}\neq w
. By
Lemma
3.33
, we have
\CC(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC(v)=\CC(w)
. Hence,
(PCC) implies that
w
and
w^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. But then
Obs.
2.6
and
N
being shortcut-free imply
w=w^{\prime}
; a
contradiction. Hence,
w
is the unique child of
v
.

Now suppose, in addition, that
N
is phylogenetic and assume, for
contradiction, that
u\neq w
and thus
u\prec_{N}w
. We can apply similar
arguments as before to conclude that
w
has a unique child. Therefore
and since
N
is phylogenetic, there must be a vertex

v^{\prime}\in\parent_{N}(w)\setminus\{v\}
since otherwise

\indeg(w)=\outdeg(w)=1
. By Lemma
3.33
, we have

\CC(v)=\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
. This together with (PCC) implies that

v
and
v^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. But then
Obs.
2.6
and
N
being shortcut-free imply
v=v^{\prime}
; a
contradiction. Therefore,
u=w
is the unique child of
v
.
∎

As a consequence of Lemmas
3.33
and
4.5
, we
have

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
N
be a semi-regular network and let
v\in V^{0}
. Then

\CC(u)\subsetneq\CC(v)
for all
u\in\child_{N}(v)
if and only if

\outdeg(v)\geq 2
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
N
be a semi-regular,
u\in V(N)
, and let
Q(u)\coloneqq\{u^{\prime}\in V(N)\mid\CC(u^{\prime})=\CC(u)\}
. Then the vertices of
Q(u)
are pairwise

\preceq_{N}
-comparable and lie consecutively along an induced directed
path in
N
. Moreover, if
|Q(u)|>1
, either
Q(u)
is contained
in a non-trivial block, or adjacent pairs of vertices in
Q(u)
form a
trivial block.

Proof.

By (PCC),
\CC(v)=\CC(w)
and
v\neq w
implies
v\prec_{N}w
or
w\prec_{N}v
for
v,w\in Q(u)
, i.e., the vertices in
Q(u)
are pairwise

\preceq_{N}
-comparable and thus linearly ordered w.r.t.
\preceq_{N}
.
Using Lemma
4.4
, one easily verifies that
Q(u)
forms a
directed path in
N
and there are unique
\preceq_{N}
-minimal and

\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertices
\min Q(u)
and
\max Q(u)
. In
particular, since
N
is acyclic and shortcut-free, this path must be an
induced subgraph.

Now suppose
u
is a hybrid vertex and suppose there is
v\in Q(u)
with

(v,u)\in E(N)
, i.e.,
u
has a parent in
Q(u)
. Then there is

v^{\prime}\in\parent(u)\setminus\{v\}
. By Lemma
3.33
,

\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
, and thus by (PCC),
v
and
v^{\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable. However, since
N
is shortcut-free,
v
and
v^{\prime}

are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable by Obs.
2.6
; a contradiction.
Thus only
\max Q(u)
can be a hybrid vertex in
Q(u)
. By similar
argument, only
\min Q(u)
can have outdegree greater that one. Hence,
all inner vertices in the directed path
P
formed by
Q(u)
have degree

2
. Therefore, if one arc in
P
is contained in an undirected cycle,
then all arcs in
P
are contained in this cycle, in which case
Q(u)

is contained in a non-trivial block. Otherwise
Q(u)
consists of a
sequence of consecutive cut-arcs.
∎

The examples in Fig.
6
(A) and (B) show that (PCC) is
necessary in Lemma
4.7
. We note, moreover, that the
cardinality
|Q(u)|
equals the multiplicity of the cluster

\CC(u)
in
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.

Theorem 4.8
.

A network is regular if and only if it is semi-regular and there is no
vertex with outdegree
1
.

Proof.

If
N
is regular, it is in particular also semi-regular, and thus
satisfies (PCC). Furthermore, then
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
implies
u=v
and thus
there are no two vertices
u,v
satisfying
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
and
u\prec_{N}v
. Lemma
4.5
thus implies that there is no vertex with
outdegree
1
. Conversely, assume that
N
is semi-regular (and thus
shortcut-free) and suppose there is not vertex with outdegree
1
. Then
Lemma
4.5
implies that there is no pair of vertices
u,v

with
\CC(u)=\CC(v)
and
u\prec_{N}v
or
v\prec_{N}u
, i.e.,

\CC(u)=\CC(v)
implies
u=v
. Therefore and by
Lemma
3.33
, we have
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)\iff u\preceq_{N}v
. By Prop.
3.43
, therefore,
N
is regular.
∎

By Thm.
4.8
, every regular network is semi-regular and thus,
satisfies (PCC). This together with Cor.
3.44

and Def.
4.1
implies

Corollary 4.9
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
there is a network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
that that satisfies (PCC).

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
N
be a network on
X
. Then,
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
for some
clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
if and only if
N
is
regular.

Proof.

Assume first that
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. In this case,
N
is
shortcut-free, satisfies (PCC), and has no vertex with outdegree
1

(since
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
has these properties). By
Thm.
4.8
,
N
is regular. Assume now that
N
is
regular. By Def.
3.41
,
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C_{N}})
. Thus, we can put
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}

and obtain
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.
∎

It should be noted that
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
does not necessarily
imply that
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
. By way of example, consider a
binary phylogenetic rooted tree
T
on
X
with
\mathscr{C}_{T}=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{1,2\},\{1,2,3\}\}
and the clustering system
\mathscr{C}=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{2,3\},\{1,2,3\}\}
. It can easily be verified
that
T\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
although
\mathscr{C}_{T}\neq\mathscr{C}
.
Nevertheless, Prop.
4.10
together with
Def.
3.41
immediately implies

Corollary 4.11
.

N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
if and only if
N
is regular.

A crucial link between a network and its clustering systems is the ability
to identify the non-trivial blocks. The following result shows that, at
least in semi-regular networks, key information is provided by the overlaps
of clusters.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
B
be a non-trivial block in a semi-regular network
N
. Then for
every
u\in B^{0}
there is a
v\in B^{0}
such that
\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)

overlap.

Proof.

Suppose
u\in B^{0}
and consider the two disjoint sets
A\coloneqq\{w\in V(B)\mid u\prec_{N}w\}
and
D\coloneqq\{w\in V(B)\mid w\prec_{N}u\}
, i.e., the ancestors and descendants, resp., of
u
in
B
. Note
that both sets are non-empty since
u\in B^{0}
. There is no arc
connecting a vertex in
A
with a vertex in
D
. To see this,
consider
a\in A
and
d\in D
. Since
d\prec_{N}u\prec_{N}a
and
N

is acyclic, there is a directed path
P
from
a
to
d
passing through

u
. Thus an arc
(a,d)
would be a shortcut, contradicting that
N
is
semi-regular, and an arc
(d,a)
would imply
a\prec_{N}d

contradicting
d\prec_{N}u\prec_{N}a
. Since
a,d\in V(B)
and
B
is
a non-trivial block,
a
and
d
lie on an undirected cycle
K
in
B
.
In particular, they are connected by two undirected paths that do not
share any inner vertices. Thus there is an undirected path
P=(d=v_{1},v_{2},\dots,a=v_{k})
that does not contain
u
. Let
v_{i}
be the unique
vertex in
P
such that
v_{i}\in D
and there is no vertex
v_{j}\in D
with

i<j\leq k
. Such a vertex exists since
v_{1}\in D
. Moreover,

v_{k}=a\in A
implies
i<k
. Thus consider the vertex
v\coloneqq v_{i+1}
. We have
v\notin D
by construction and
v\notin A
since

v_{i}\in D
is not adjacent to any vertex in
A
. Since
P
does not contain

u
and
v\in V(B)\setminus(A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}D)
, we see that
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Since
v_{i}\in D
, we
have
v_{i}\prec_{N}u
. Hence,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
cannot be an arc in
N

since otherwise
v_{i+1}\prec_{N}v_{i}\prec_{N}u
would imply
v_{i+1}\in D
; a contradiction. Therefore, it have
(v,v_{i})=(v_{i+1},v_{i})\in E(N)

and thus
v_{i}\prec_{N}u,v
. By Lemma
3.33
, this implies

\emptyset\neq\CC_{N}(v_{i})\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)\cap\CC_{N}(v)
. Together
with (PCC) and the fact that
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable,
this yields that
\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)
overlap. In particular,
v\neq\max B
since
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable and
v
is not a
terminal vertex since
v_{i}\prec_{N}v
. Therefore, we have
v\in B^{0}
.
∎

We note that semi-regularity cannot be omitted in
Lemma
4.12
, since the statement is not true for
the rooted
K_{3,3}
of Fig.
6
. Lemma
3.35

and Lemma
4.12
together show that in semi-regular
networks all vertices in the interior of non-trivial blocks are identified
by the fact their clusters overlap. It remains an open question, however,
whether the information of cluster overlaps is sufficient to identify the
non-trivial blocks.

We continue by showing that, whenever (PCC) is satisfied, least-resolved
networks are precisely the regular network, To this end, we consider first
the implications given by the operations
\expand
and
\contract
, and by
the removal of short-cuts, respectively.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
N
be a network,
w\in V(N)
, and
N^{\prime}
the network obtained from
N

by applying
\expand(w)
. Then
N
satisfies (PCC) if and only if
N^{\prime}

satisfies (PCC).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.13
,
N
and
N^{\prime}
are
(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving,
i.e.,
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
if and only if
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
holds for all

v,v^{\prime}\in V(N)
. By Lemma
3.13
,

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in V(N)\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
. Let
w^{\prime}
be
the unique vertex in
V(N^{\prime})\setminus V(N)
. By construction,
w
is the
unique child of
w^{\prime}
in
N^{\prime}
and thus, by
Obs.
3.36
,
\CC_{N}(w)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})
.

Suppose first
N^{\prime}
satisfies (PCC), i.e., for all
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})
, it
holds that
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable if and only if

\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
or
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)
.
To see that
N
also satisfies (PCC), consider
u,v\in V(N)\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
. Suppose
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. Hence,
u
and

v
are also
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable, and thus

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)=\CC_{N}(v)
or

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC(v)_{N^{\prime}}\subseteq C_{N^{\prime}}(u)=\CC_{N}(u)
. Conversely, if

\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
or
\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)
,
then also
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
or
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)
. Hence,
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable and thus
also
\preceq_{N}
-comparable.

Now suppose
N
satisfies (PCC). By similar argument as above, it holds,
for all
u,v\in V(N)=V(N^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
that
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable if and only if
\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
or

\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)
. To show that
N^{\prime}
satisfies (PCC), it
therefore only remains to consider
w^{\prime}
and some vertex
v\in V(N)
. It
holds that
v
and
w^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable if and only if
v

and
w
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable. To see this, suppose first
v

and
w^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable. If
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
, then

v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}w
since
w
is the unique child of
w^{\prime}
. If
w^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
, then
w\prec_{N^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
implies
w\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
. Now suppose
v
and

w
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable. If
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}w
, then

w\prec_{N^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
implies
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
. If
w\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
, then

w^{\prime}\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
(and thus
w^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
) since
w^{\prime}
is the unique
parent of
w
in
N^{\prime}
. Taken together and since
v,w\in V(N)
, the
arguments so far imply that
v
and
w^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable if
and only if
v
and
w
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable if and only if

\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})
or

\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(w)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
In summary, therefore,

N^{\prime}
satisfies (PCC).
∎

Cor.
3.14
and Lemma
4.13
imply that
semi-regularity is preserved by
\expand
applied on arbitrary vertices and

\contract(u,w)
applied to arcs
(u,w)
where
\outdeg(u)=1
.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
N
be a network,
w\in V(N)
, and
N^{\prime}
the network obtained from
N

by applying
\expand(w)
.
Then
N
is semi-regular if and only if
N^{\prime}
is semi-regular.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
N
be a network satisfying (PCC). The unique regular network

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
is obtained from
N
by repeatedly executing one of
the following operations until neither of them is possible:

(1)

remove a shortcut
(u,w)
, and

(2)

apply
\contract(u,w)
for an arc
(u,w)
with

\outdeg(u)=1
.

Proof.

Let
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained by applying one of the following
operations until neither of them is possible. By construction, we have

V(N^{\prime})\subseteq V(N)
. We can repeatedly apply
Lemma
3.8
and
3.11

to conclude that
N^{\prime}
is a network with leaf set
X
and that, for all

v,v^{\prime}\in V(N^{\prime})
, it holds
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
if and only if
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. Similarly, Lemma
3.8
and

3.11
imply that
\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)

holds for all
v\in V(N^{\prime})
and, in particular,

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.

By assumption,
N
satisfies (PCC), i.e., for all
u,v\in V(N)
, it
holds that
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable if and only if

\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
or
\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)
. To see
that
N^{\prime}
also satisfies (PCC), consider
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})
. Suppose
u

and
v
are
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable. Hence,
u
and
v
are also

\preceq_{N}
-comparable, and thus
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)=\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)
or
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)=\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)
. Conversely, if
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)

or
\CC_{N^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N^{\prime}}(u)
, then also
\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
or
\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)
. Hence,
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable and thus also
\preceq_{N^{\prime}}
-comparable.
Therefore,
N^{\prime}
satisfies (PCC). Since moreover
N^{\prime}
is shortcut-free by
construction,
N^{\prime}
is semi-regular. This together with
Thm.
4.8
and the fact that, by construction, there is
no vertex
v
with
\outdeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)=1
implies that
N^{\prime}
is regular.
This, together with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
, implies that
N^{\prime}

is the unique regular network
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
N
be a least-resolved network satisfying (PCC). Then
N
is the
unique regular network for
\mathscr{C}_{N}
.

Proof.

By Cor.
3.12
,
N
is shortcut-free (and thus,
semi-regular) and contains no vertex with outdegree
1
.
By Thm.
4.8
, therefore,
N
is regular.
∎

As a consequence of
Cor.
3.45
, Thm.
4.8
, and
Prop.
4.16
, we obtain

Theorem 4.17
.

A network
N
is regular if and only if
N
is least-resolved and satisfies
(PCC).

4.2
Separated Networks and Cluster Networks

Recall that a network
N
is
separated
if all hybrid vertices have
outdegree
1
.
We have already seen above that the Hasse diagrams of the clustering
systems cannot produce separated networks with hybrid vertices
because
\outdeg(v)=1
implies that
\CC(v)=\CC(u)
whenever
u
is
the only child of
v
. By Thm.
4.8
, furthermore, a
regular network does not have any vertex with outdegree
1
. Therefore, a
regular network cannot be separated whenever it contains at least one
hybrid vertex and
vice versa
.

The
Cluster-popping
algorithm
[
26
]
constructs a separated
network for a given clustering system
\mathscr{C}
by first constructing the
Hasse diagram, and thus the unique regular network
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
, and
then applying
\expand(w)
to all hybrid vertices
w\in V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}))
. In particular, the resulting network is a so-called

cluster network

[
26
,
37
]
:

Definition 4.18
.

A network
N
is a
cluster network
if (i) it satisfies (PCC), and,
for all
u,v\in V(N)
, it holds

(ii)

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
if and only if
u=v
or
v
is a
hybrid vertex and parent of
u
or vice versa,

(iii)

if
u
is a child of
v
, then there exists no node
w

with
\CC_{N}(u)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(w)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(v)
, and

(iv)

every hybrid vertex
v
has exactly one child, which is a
tree node.

We note that the definition of cluster networks usually is expressed using
the following condition instead of (PCC):

(i’)

\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
if and only if

u\preceq_{N}v
for all

u,v\in V(N)

[
26
,
59
]
.

However, this contradicts the existence of hybrid vertices
v
, which are
required to have exactly one child
u
by (iv). To see this, observe that

u\prec_{N}v
and, by Obs.
3.36
, we have

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
. The latter means that
\CC_{N}(v)\subseteq_{N}\CC_{N}(u)
is satisfied and thus (i’) implies
v\preceq_{N}u
; a
contradiction.

We can rephrase conditions (i)-(iv), and thus the definition of cluster
networks, as follows:

Theorem 4.19
.

A network
N
is a cluster network if and only if it is semi-regular,
separated, and phylogenetic.

Proof.

Suppose first that
N
is a cluster network, i.e., it satisfies
conditions (i)-(iv) in Def.
4.18
. By condition (i)
and (ii), resp.,
N
satisfies (PCC) and is separated. Hence, it remains
to show that
N
is shortcut-free and phylogenetic. Suppose, for
contradiction, that
(v,u)
is a shortcut in
N
. Then there is
w\in\child_{N}(v)\setminus\{u\}
and
w^{\prime}\in\parent_{N}(u)\setminus\{v\}

such that
u\prec_{N}w^{\prime}\preceq_{N}w
. By Lemma
3.33
,

\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC_{N}(w)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
. If
\CC_{N}(w)=\CC_{N}(v)
, then conditions (ii) and (iv)
imply that
v
is a hybrid vertex with exactly one child; a
contradiction. Therefore,
\CC_{N}(w)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(v)
must hold. If

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(w^{\prime})
, then conditions (ii) and (iv) imply that
w^{\prime}

is a hybrid vertex and its unique child
u
is a tree node, contradicting
that
w^{\prime},v\in\parent_{N}(u)
. Hence, we have
\CC_{N}(u)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC_{N}(w)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(v)
, which
contradicts (iii). Therefore,
N
must be shortcut-free and thus
semi-regular. Suppose, for contradiction, that
N
is not
phylogenetic. Hence, there is a vertex
v
with exactly one child
u
and

\indeg_{N}(v)\leq 1
. By Obs.
3.36
, we have

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
and thus
v
must be a hybrid vertex by (ii); a
contradiction.

Conversely, suppose
N
is semi-regular, separated, and phylogenetic.
Hence,
N
satisfies (PCC), i.e., condition (i). Condition (iii) must be
satisfied since an arc
(v,u)
with
\CC_{N}(u)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(w)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(v)
for some
w\in V(N)
would be a shortcut by
Obs.
4.3
. We continue with
showing (ii). Suppose
\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
and
u\neq v
. By (PCC),
it holds
u\prec_{N}v
or
v\prec_{N}u
. Suppose w.l.o.g. that

u\prec_{N}v
. Then Lemma
4.5
implies that

\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
and
u
is the unique child of
v
. Since
N
is
phylogenetic,
v
must be a hybrid vertex. Conversely, a hybrid vertex

v
in a separated network has exactly one child
u
implying

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
by Obs.
3.36
. Since
N

is separated, it remains to show that the unique child
u
of a hybrid
vertex
v
is a tree node. Suppose for contradiction that
u
is a
hybrid node. Then
u
again has a unique child
w
. Hence, we have

w\prec_{N}u\prec_{N}v
and, by Obs.
3.36
, it
holds
\CC_{N}(w)=\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
. By (ii), this implies that

(v,w)
is an arc in
N
and, in particular, a shortcut; a
contradiction. Therefore, condition (iv) is also satisfied.
∎

We shall see in Thm.
4.25
in the following
section that cluster networks are uniquely determined by their cluster
sets. To obtain this result it will be convenient to make use of the fact
the semi-regular networks are encoded by their multisets of clusters.

4.3
Cluster Multisets of Semi-Regular Networks

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
N
be a semi-regular phylogenetic network. Then, the multiplicity
of each cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}
in the cluster multiset
\mathscr{M}_{N}

is either one or two. In the latter case, the two distinct vertices

u,v\in V(N)
with
\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)=C
are adjacent.

Proof.

Recall that a semi-regular network
N
satisfies (PCC) and is
shortcut-free. Let
C\in\mathscr{C}
. Thus, there is at least one vertex

v\in V(N)
with
\CC_{N}(v)=C
. Now suppose there is
u\in V(N)\setminus\{v\}
with
\CC_{N}(u)=C
. By (PCC), it holds
u\prec_{N}v
or
v\prec_{N}u
. Suppose that
u\prec_{N}v
. Then
Lemma
4.5
implies that
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
and
u
is the
unique child of
v
. Suppose, for contradiction, there is a third vertex

w\in V(N)\setminus\{u,v\}
. By (PCC), it holds
w\prec_{N}v
or

v\prec_{N}w
. If
w\prec_{N}v
, then Lemma
4.5
implies
that
w
is the unique child of
v
; a contradiction. If
v\prec_{N}w
,
then we have also
u\prec_{N}v\prec_{N}w
. By Lemma
4.5
,
therefore,
v
and
u
are both the unique child of
w
, which is not
possible since
u\neq v
. One argues similarly if
v\prec_{N}u
. In
particular,
u
and
v
are adjacent in both cases.
∎

As we shall see in Theorem
4.23
, the property of being
phylogenetic, however, is not necessary for semi-regular networks to be
identified by their cluster multisets. In order to prove this, the
following map will be of useful.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
N
and
\tilde{N}
be two networks satisfying (PCC) and

\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
.
Then, the map
\varphi_{PCC}\colon V(N)\to V(\tilde{N})
is given by the
following steps for all

C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}
:

(i)

sort the
k\geq 1
vertices in
N
with cluster
C
such that

v_{1}\prec_{N}\dots\prec_{N}v_{k}
,

(ii)

sort the
k
vertices in
\tilde{N}
with cluster
C
such
that
\tilde{v}_{1}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\dots\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}_{k}
, and

(iii)

set
\varphi(v_{i})=\tilde{v}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

In other words, we map the
\preceq_{N}
-larger vertices with cluster
C

in
N
to
\preceq_{\tilde{N}}
-larger vertices with cluster
C
in

\tilde{N}
, which is possible since, by (PCC), these vertices are totally
ordered w.r.t.
\preceq_{N}
and
\preceq_{\tilde{N}}
, respectively.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
N
and
\tilde{N}
be two networks satisfying (PCC) and

\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
.
Then
\varphi_{PCC}
is a bijection between
V(N)
and
V(\tilde{N})
that
is the identity on the common leaf set
X
.
Writing
\tilde{v}\coloneqq\varphi_{PCC}(v)
, it moreover holds

1.

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{v})
for all
v\in V(N)
,

2.

v
is a leaf if and only if
\tilde{v}
is a leaf for all

v\in V(N)
, and

3.

u\prec_{N}v
if and only if
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
for all
u,v\in V(N)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
, the multiplicity of every
cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}

is equal in
\mathscr{M}_{N}
and
\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
, i.e., there
are
k\geq 1
vertices with cluster
C
in
N
and
k
vertices with
cluster
C
in
\tilde{N}
. One easily verifies that, by construction,

\varphi_{PCC}
is a bijection between
V(N)
and
V(\tilde{N})
that is
the identity on the common leaf set
X
and satisfies

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{v})
for all
v\in V(N)
. To see that

u\prec_{N}v
if and only if
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
,
suppose
u\prec_{N}v
. By Lemma
3.33
, this implies

\CC_{N}(u)\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
. If
\CC_{N}(u)\subsetneq\CC_{N}(v)
(and
thus
\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{u})\subsetneq\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{v})
),
then Obs.
4.3
implies

\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
. If, on the other hand,

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)
, then
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
holds
by construction of
\varphi
. Analogously,
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
implies
u\prec_{N}v
. In particular, this implies that, for
every
v\in V(N)
,
v
is a leaf if and only if
\tilde{v}
is a leaf.
∎

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
N
be a semi-regular network. Then
N
is the unique semi-regular
network whose cluster multiset is
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.

Proof.

Suppose
N
and
\tilde{N}
are semi-regular networks with

\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
. By assumption, both
N
and

\tilde{N}
are shortcut-free and satisfy (PCC). We continue with
showing that
\varphi_{PCC}\colon V(N)\to V(\tilde{N})
is a graph
isomorphism. By Lemma
4.22
,
\varphi_{PCC}
is a bijection
that is the identity on the common leaf set
X
. In the following, we
write
\tilde{v}\coloneqq\varphi_{PCC}(v)
for all
v\in V(N)
. Suppose
that
(v,u)\in E(N)
. Thus, we have
u\prec_{N}v
which implies

\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
by Lemma
4.22
(3).
Assume, for contradiction, that
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\notin E(\tilde{N})
. Then there must be
\tilde{z}\in V(\tilde{N})
such that

\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
. By
Lemma
4.22
(3), we have
u\prec_{N}z\prec_{N}v
. Hence, the
arc
(v,u)
must be a shortcut in
N
; a contradiction. Therefore,

(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\in E(\tilde{N})
. By analogous arguments,

(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\in E(\tilde{N})
implies
(v,u)\in E(N)
. Hence,

\varphi_{PCC}
is a graph isomorphism that is the identity on
X
and
thus
N\simeq\tilde{N}
. Therefore,
N
is the unique semi-regular
network whose cluster multiset is
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.
∎

We emphasize that none of the two conditions (PCC) and shortcut-free that
define semi-regular networks can be omitted in
Theorem
4.23
as shown by the examples in
Fig.
8
.

Figure 8:
Two pairs of non-isomorphic (phylogenetic) networks
N
and
N^{\prime}

for which
\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{N^{\prime}}
. (A)
N
and
N^{\prime}

satisfy (PCC) but are not shortcut-free. (B)
N
and
N^{\prime}
are
shortcut-free but do not satisfy (PCC).

It is an easy task to verify that semi-regular networks
N
(as any other
network for which the property of being phylogenetic has been left out) are
not determined by their clustering systems
\mathscr{C}_{N}
. The network

N
in Fig.
9
(A), for example, is not phylogenetic (but
semi-regular). Suppression of any vertex with in- and outdegree
1
yields
a network
N^{\prime}
with
N\not\simeq N^{\prime}
but
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. We will see in the following that there is a 1-to-1
correspondence between clustering systems and cluster networks. To show
this, we will need the following technical result that relates the
occurrence of vertices with equal clusters in such networks to the
structure of the Hasse diagram.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
N
be a cluster network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
. Then,
for every cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is either exactly one vertex

v\in V(N)
with
\CC_{N}(v)=C
or there are exactly two vertices
u,v\in V(N)
with
\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)=C
. The latter case occurs if and only
if
C
has indegree at least
2
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Moreover, in
this case,
u
and
v
are adjacent in
N
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.19
,
N
is phylogenetic,
separated, and semi-regular, i.e., it satisfies (PCC) and is
shortcut-free. By Lemma
4.20
, it only
remains to show that there are two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(N)
with

\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)=C
if and only if
C
has indegree at least
2
in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.

Suppose
C=\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{N}(u)
for two distinct vertices
v,u\in V(N)

and assume w.l.o.g. that
u\prec_{N}v
. By Lemma
4.5
,
u

must be the unique child of
v
. Since
N
is phylogenetic, this implies
that
\indeg_{N}(v)\geq 2
. Thus let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be two distinct
parents of
v
. Since
N
is shortcut-free and by
Obs.
2.6
,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are

\prec_{N}
-incomparable. Using (PCC), we conclude that
C_{1}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(v_{1})
and
C_{2}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(v_{2})
are distinct and none of
them is contained in the other. Moreover, we have
C\subseteq C_{1}\cap C_{2}
by Lemma
3.33
, and thus, and
C\subsetneq C_{1},C_{2}
.
Suppose there is
C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
such that
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C_{1}
. Let
v^{\prime}\in V(N)
be a vertex with
\CC_{N}(v)=C^{\prime}
. By
Obs.
4.3
, we have
u\prec_{N}v^{\prime}\prec_{N}v
. Therefore,
(v,u)\in V(N)
must be a shortcut; a
contradiction. Hence, there is no
C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
with
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C_{1}
and
C_{1}
must be a parent of
C
in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. By similar arguments,
C_{2}
is a parent of
C
in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
, which together with
C_{1}\neq C_{2}
implies that
C

has indegree at least
2
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.

Conversely, suppose
C
has at least two distinct parents
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Hence, it holds
C\subsetneq C_{1}
and

C\subsetneq C_{2}
and none of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
is contained in the other.
Let
v,v_{1},v_{2}\in V(N)
be vertices with
C=\CC_{N}(v)
,
C_{1}=\CC_{N}(v_{1})
, and
C_{2}=\CC_{N}(v_{2})
. Clearly,
v
,
v_{1}
, and
v_{2}

are pairwise distinct. By Obs.
4.3
, we
have
v\prec_{N}v_{1}
and
v\prec_{N}v_{2}
, i.e., there are a
v_{1}v
-path
P_{1}
and a
v_{2}v
-path
P_{2}
in
N
. Let
v^{\prime}
be the

\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex in
P_{1}
that is also a vertex in
P_{2}
. We
distinguish the two cases
v^{\prime}=v
and
v^{\prime}\neq v
. If
v^{\prime}=v
, then
v

has a parent in each of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
which are distinct by
construction. Thus
v
is a hybrid vertex. Since moreover
N
is
separated,
v
has a unique child
u
. By
Obs.
3.36
, this implies
\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)=C
.
Now suppose
v^{\prime}\neq v
. Lemma
3.33
and
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}\preceq_{N}v_{1},v_{2}
imply
C\subseteq C^{\prime}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(v^{\prime})
,

C^{\prime}\subseteq C_{1}
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq C_{2}
. Since none of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
is
contained in the other, we must have
C\subseteq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C_{1}
and

C\subseteq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C_{2}
. Since
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are parents of
C

in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
, the latter is only possible if
C=C^{\prime}
. Hence,
in both cases, there are two distinct vertices in
N
with cluster
C
.
∎

Theorem 4.25
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
, there is a unique cluster
network
N
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
. It is obtained from the
unique regular network
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
of
\mathscr{C}
by applying

\expand(v)
to all hybrid vertices. In particular,
N
is the unique
semi-regular separated phylogenetic network with clustering system

\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

By Thm.
4.8
, the unique regular network

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is shortcut-free, satisfies (PCC), and has no
vertex with outdegree
1
. In particular,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is
phylogenetic. Now let
N
be the network obtained from
N
by repeatedly
applying
\expand(w)
to some hybrid vertex whose outdegree is not
1

until no such vertex exists. Clearly this is achieved by applying

\expand(w)
to all hybrid vertices
w\in V(N)
since they all satisfy

\outdeg_{\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})}(w)\neq 1
and, moreover, no expansion step
introduces new such vertices but reduces their number by
1
. We can
repeatedly (i.e., in each expansion step) apply Lemma
3.13
to
conclude that the resulting digraph
N
is a phylogenetic network,
Lemma
3.13
to conclude that
N
satisfies

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})}=\mathscr{C}
, and
Cor.
4.14
to conclude that
N
is semi-regular.
In particular, by construction, all hybrid vertices in
N
have outdegree

1
, i.e.,
N
is separated. By Theorem
4.19
,

N
is a cluster network.

It remains to show that
N
is the unique cluster network with clustering
system
\mathscr{C}
. To this end, let
\tilde{N}
be a cluster network
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}
. By
Theorem
4.19
, both
N
and
\tilde{N}
are
semi-regular, shortcut-free, and phylogenetic. Moreover, by
Lemma
4.24
, for every cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}
,
C

has multiplicity
2
in
\mathscr{M}_{N}
if and only if
C
has indegree
at least
2
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
if and only if
C
has multiplicity

2
in
\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
; and multiplicity
1
in both

\mathscr{M}_{N}
and
\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
otherwise. Hence, we have

\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
. By
Theorem
4.23
, we conclude that
N\simeq\tilde{N}
,
and thus,
N
is the unique cluster network. In particular, by
Theorem
4.19
,
N
is the unique semi-regular
separated phylogenetic network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
.
∎

The uniqueness of cluster networks for a given clustering system

\mathscr{C}
has been proved in the framework of
reticulate
networks
in
[
3
, Thm. 3.9]
, using alternative arguments. A
network
N
is
reticulate
in the sense of
[
3
, Def. 2.3]

if (a) every hybrid vertex has exactly one child which, moreover, must
be a tree vertex, and (b) if a vertex
v
has
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
and

\indeg_{N}(v)\leq 1
then
v
has a unique child and parent, both of which
are hybrid vertices. We observe that the additional condition that
N

is reticulate does not affect cluster networks, and thus
Thm.
4.25
and
[
3
, Thm. 3.9]
are
equivalent.

Proposition 4.26
.

If
N
is a cluster network, then
N
is reticulate.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.19
,
N
is semi-regular,
phylogenetic, and separated. Since
N
is phylogenetic, it does not
contain a vertex satisfying condition (b), and hence (b) is satisfied
trivially. Since
N
is separated, every hybrid vertex
v
has exactly
one child
u
. By Obs
3.36
, we have

\CC(v)=\CC(u)
. Suppose that
u
is also a hybrid vertex, i.e., there is

v^{\prime}\in\parent_{N}(u)\setminus\{v\}
. Since
N
is shortcut-free,
v
and

v^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. However, by
Lemma
3.33
, we have
\CC(v)=\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
and
thus (PCC) implies that
v
and
v^{\prime}
must be
\preceq_{N}
-comparable; a
contradiction. Hence,
N
also satisfies condition (a). is also
satisfied for any cluster network.
∎

5
Tree-Child, Normal, and Tree-Based Networks

Definition 5.1
.

[
15
]

A network
N
has the
tree-child
property if, for every

v\in V^{0}
, there is a “tree-child”, i.e.,
u\in\child(v)
with

\indeg(u)=1
.

Tree-child networks are not necessarily phylogenetic, see
Fig.
9
(A) for an example. As a shown in

[
15
, Lemma 2]
,
N
is a tree-child network if and only if
every vertex
v\in V
has a strict descendant, i.e., a leaf
x\in X
such
that every directed path from the root
\rho_{N}
to
x
contains
v
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
N
is tree-child and
u
and
v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.
Then there is a vertex
x\in\CC(v)
such that
x\notin\CC(u)
and
y\in\CC(u)
such
that
y\notin\CC(v)
.

Proof.

Since
N
is tree-child, there is a strict descendant
x
of
v
. It
satisfies
x\in\CC(v)
and every path from the root
\rho_{N}
to
x
runs
through
v
. Now suppose
x\in\CC(u)
. Then there is a directed path
from
\rho_{N}
to
x
that contains
u
. Since any such path also contains

v
, the vertices
u
and
v
must be
\preceq_{N}
-comparable; a
contradiction. Thus
x\notin\CC(u)
. The same argument shows that there
is
y\in\CC(v)
with
y\notin\CC(u)
.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

Every tree-child network satisfies (PCC).

Proof.

Let
N
be a tree-child network and
u,v\in V(N)
. By
Lemma
3.33
,
u\preceq_{N}v
implies

\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
. On the other hand, if
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, then Lemma
5.2
implies
that either
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\emptyset
or
\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)

overlap and thus neither
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
nor

\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
is satisfied.
∎

The converse is not true. Fig.
9
(B) shows an example of a
network that satisfies (PCC) but does not have the tree-child property.

Figure 9:
(A) Example of a network that is semi-regular and
tree-child but
not phylogenetic. (B) Example of a cluster network, thus satisfying
(PCC), that is not tree-child. The central node marked in red does not
have a tree-child. One easily checks that it satisfies (PCC) since
vertices associated with overlapping pairs of clusters are
incomparable.

Definition 5.4
.

A network is
normal
if it is tree-child and shortcut-free.

Willson
[
57
]
studies normal networks in a somewhat different
setting, in which
X
comprises not only the leaves in our sense but also
the root and all vertices with outdegree
1
. Under this assumption,

[
57
, Thm. 3.4]
states that “
N
is regular whenever it is
normal”. The absence of vertices with outdegree
1
can be included as an
extra condition. The analog of Willson’s result in our setting follows
immediately from Cor.
5.3
, Thm.
4.8
,
and the absence of shortcuts:

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
N
be a network. If
N
is normal, then
N
is semi-regular.
If, in addition, there are no vertices with outdegree
1
, then
N
is
regular.

The converse is not true, there are (semi-)regular networks that are not
normal, see Fig. 1 of
[
57
]
and also Fig.
9
(B)
for a semi-regular example. Hence, we have

Remark 2
.

Not every semi-regular network, and in particular not every cluster
network, is normal.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system. If there is a phylogenetic,
separated, normal network
N
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
, then
N

is unique w.r.t. these properties. In particular,
N
is the unique
cluster network with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
.

Proof.

Suppose
N
is phylogenetic, separated, and normal and satisfies

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
. By Cor
5.5
,
N
is
semi-regular and thus, by Theorem
4.19
, a
cluster network. By Thm.
4.25
,
N
is
unique.
∎

From Prop.
5.6
and the definition of binary networks,
we immediately obtain

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
N
be a binary normal network. Then
N
is the unique binary normal
network whose cluster set is
\mathscr{C}_{N}
. In particular,
N
is a
cluster network.

The following result appears to be well known, see e.g.
[
44
]
. An argument for binary tree-child and level-1
networks can be found
[
34
]
. A direct proof for our
more general setting is included here for completeness.

Proposition 5.8
.

Every phylogenetic level-1 network is tree-child.

Proof.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic level-
1
network. If
u
is a hybrid vertex,
then, by Lemma
3.22
, there is a
non-trivial block
B_{v}
that contains
v
and all its parents. Suppose,
for contradiction, there is a non-leaf vertex
v
whose children are all
hybrid vertices. Suppose first that
\outdeg(v)=1
. Since
N
is
phylogenetic, this implies that
v
is hybrid vertex. Let
u
be the
unique child of
v
, which is a hybrid vertex by assumption. The hybrid
vertices
u
and
v
are contained in a common non-trivial block
B_{u}
.
In particular,
u\prec_{N}v
implies
u\neq\max B_{u}
. Additionally,

v\neq\max B_{u}
since
\outdeg(v)=1
but
\outdeg(\max B_{u})>1
by
Lemma
3.18
; contradicting that
N
is level-1. Now
suppose that
\outdeg(v)\geq 2
. Since all
u_{i}\in\child(v)
are hybrid
vertices,
v
and
u_{i}
are contained in blocks
B_{i}\coloneqq B_{u_{i}}
.
Note that
u_{i}\neq\max B_{i}
. If there are two distinct

u_{i},u_{j}\in\child(v)
such that
v\notin\{\max B_{i},\max B_{j}\}
, then
Lemma
3.19
implies
B_{i}=B_{j}
and thus
B_{i}
contains
the hybrid vertices
u_{i},u_{j}\neq\max B_{i}
; this contradicts that
N
is
level-1. Otherwise, there is at least one
u_{i}\in\child(v)
such that

v=\max B_{i}
. Since
u_{i}
is a hybrid vertex, there is
w_{i}\in\parent_{N}(u_{i})\setminus\{v\}
, which is also contained in

B_{i}
. Hence, we have
u_{i}\prec_{N}w_{i}\prec_{N}\max B_{i}=v
. Therefore,
and because
N
is acyclic, there is
u_{j}\in\child_{N}(v)
such that

w_{i}\preceq_{N}u_{j}\prec_{N}v
. By assumption,
u_{j}
is a hybrid vertex
and moreover
u_{j}\notin\{u_{i},v=\max B_{i}\}
. By
Lemma
3.16
,
w_{i}\preceq_{N}u_{j}\prec_{N}v
and

w_{i},v\in V(B_{i})
imply
u_{j}\in V(B_{i})
. Hence,
B_{i}
contains two
distinct hybrid vertices
u_{i},u_{j}\neq\max B_{i}
, contradicting that
N
is
level-1.
∎

Note that “phylogenetic” cannot be omitted in
Prop.
5.8
: Consider a tree-vertex
v
with a hybrid
child
u\in\child(v)
. Subdivision of the arc
(v,u)
creates a new tree
vertex
u^{\prime}\in\child(v)
with the hybrid
u
as its only child. The
modified network is still level-
1
but no longer tree-child.

Next we consider the overlapping clusters in tree-child networks in
some more detail:

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
N
be a tree-child network and suppose
u
and
v
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Then either
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\CC(h)
for some
hybrid vertex
h\in V(N)
or
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
.

Proof.

By Cor.
5.3
,
N
satisfies (PCC) and thus either

\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\emptyset
, in which case the assertion is obviously
true, or
\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)
overlap. In the latter case,
Lemma
3.34
implies that
u,v
are contained in a common
non-trivial block
B
. Set
A\coloneqq\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
and assume

A\neq\CC(h)
for any hybrid vertex
h\in V(N)
. By
Lemma
7.8
, this implies that
N
is level-
k
for some
k\geq 2
. Lemma
3.37
implies that
A=\bigcup_{h\in H}\CC(h)
for some set
H
of
|H|\geq 2
hybrid vertices.
Furthermore, we have
\CC(h)\subsetneq A
for all
h\in H
. Now suppose,
for contradiction, that there is a non-hybrid vertex
w\in V(N)
such
that
\CC(w)=\bigcup_{h\in H}\CC(h)
. Then, for all
h\in H
, we have

\CC(h)\subsetneq\CC(w)
, which together with
Cor.
4.3
implies
h\prec_{N}w
. Moreover,
all elements in
\CC(w)
are descendants of one of the hybrid vertices in

H
. Since
N
is tree-child, there is a leaf
x
that is reachable from

w
along a path consisting entirely of tree vertices and thus cannot be
a descendant of any hybrid vertex
h\prec_{N}w
. That is, there is
x\in\CC(w)
and
x\notin\CC(h)
for all
h\in H
; a contradiction. Therefore

A\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
.
∎

In particular, the case
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
in
Lemma
5.9
can indeed occur even if

\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
as the example in Fig.
10

shows. Hence, the clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
of a tree-child
network
N
is not necessarily closed.

Figure 10:
A tree-child network whose clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is
not closed since
\{w,x,y\}\cap\{x,y,z\}=\{x,y\}\notin\mathscr{C}
.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
N
be a tree-child network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
and let

C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
be a pair of overlapping clusters. Then
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
if and only if there is a hybrid vertex
h\in V(N)
such
that
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\CC(h)
.

Another class of networks that has received considerable attention in the
last decade are tree-based networks

[
23
,
58
,
42
,
47
]
. They capture the idea
that networks can be obtained from (the subdivision of) a tree by inserting
additional arcs:

Definition 5.11
.

A network
N
is called
tree-based
with base tree
T
if
N
can be
obtained from
T
by (a) subdividing the arcs of
T
by introducing
vertices with in- and outdegree
1
(called
attachment points
),
and (b) adding arcs (called
linking arcs
) between pairs of
vertices, so that
N
remains acyclic.

This definition further generalizes the original one for non-binary
tree-based networks in
[
42
]
in the sense that we do not require
the two properties phylogenetic and separated and that we allow to have
additional arcs between non-attachment points and attachment points may
have in- and outdegree greater than one. This generalization ensures that
all trees, i.e., in particular non-phylogenetic trees remain tree-based.

Equivalently, a network
N
on
X
is tree-based if and only if there is a
rooted (not necessarily phylogenetic) spanning tree
T
with leaf set
X
,
i.e., there are no
dummy leaves
in
T
that correspond to inner
vertices in
N
. Clearly, the unique incoming arc
(u,v)
of a tree vertex

v
must be contained in every rooted spanning tree
T
of a network
N

and thus
u
cannot be a dummy leaf in
T
. As an immediate consequence,
the well-known fact that tree-child network are always tree-based

[
47
]
, remains also true in our generalized setting: and does in
particular, not require the properties phylogenetic and separated:

Observation 5.12
.

Every tree-child network is tree-based.

Figure 11:
(A) A network that is tree-based (with a possible base tree
indicated by the solid-line arcs) but that does not satisfy
(PCC). (B) A cluster network (thus satisfying (PCC)) that is not
tree-based, see details in the text.

Fig.
11
(A) shows a network
N
that is tree-based since
removal of the dashed arcs results in a rooted spanning tree whose leaves
are exactly the leaves of
N
. However,
N
does not satisfy (PCC) since
the two vertices highlighted in orange correspond to the same cluster but
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Conversely, the example in
Fig.
11
(B) shows that (PCC), or even semi-regularity,
does not imply that a network is tree-based. In particular, the network

N^{\prime}
is the cluster network for
\mathscr{C}=\{\{x\},\{y\},\{z\},\{v\},\{x,y\},\{x,z\},\{x,v\},\{y,z\},\{y,v\},\{x,y,z,v\}\}
. To
see that
N^{\prime}
is not tree-based, consider the set
U
of all inner vertices
whose children are all hybrid vertices, called
omnians
in

[
42
]
. These vertices are highlighted in orange. In any rooted
spanning tree, each of the four hybrid vertices (highlighted in cyan) has
exactly one parent. Since, in addition, none of the five omnians has a
child that is not one of the four hybrid vertices, one easily verifies that
at least one omnian must be a dummy leaf in every spanning tree. Therefore,

N^{\prime}
is not tree-based. The latter observation is in line with Cor. 3.6 in

[
42
]
, which holds for a more restricted definition of
tree-based and states that a (phylogenetic, separated) network is
tree-based if and only if, for all
S\subseteq U
, the number of different
children of the vertices in
S
is greater than or equal to
|S|
.
Clearly, the latter is not satisfied for
S=U
in the example. We
summarize the latter findings in

Observation 5.13
.

Not every tree-based network satisfies (PCC). Moreover, there are cluster
networks and thus, phylogenetic separated networks that do not satisfy
(PCC).

6
Least Common Ancestors and Lca-Networks

6.1
Basics

Definition 6.1
.

[
8
]

A
least common ancestor
(LCA) of a subset
Y\subseteq V
in a DAG

N
is a
\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex of
V
that is an ancestor of all
vertices in
Y
.

In general DAGs
N
, an LCA does not necessarily exist for a given vertex
set. Moreover, the LCA is not unique in general. We write
\LCA(Y)
for the
(possibly) empty set of
\preceq_{N}
-minimal ancestors of the elements in
Y
.
In a (phylogenetic) network
N
, the root
\rho_{N}
is an ancestor of all
vertices in
V
, and thus a least common ancestor exists for all
Y\subseteq V
. The LCA sets retain key information on the partial order
\preceq_{N}
:

Observation 6.2
.

Let
N
be a network on
X
and
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq V
. Then for
every
y\in\LCA(Y)
there is
z\in\LCA(Z)
such that
z\preceq_{N}y
.

Proof.

Consider
y\in\LCA(Y)
. Then
y
is also an ancestor of all vertices in

Z
and thus there is a
\preceq_{N}
minimal descendant
z\preceq_{N}y

that is an ancestor of all vertices in
Z
, i.e.,
z\in\LCA(Z)
.
∎

If
\LCA(Y)=\{u\}
consists of a single element
u
only, we write

\lca(Y)=u
. In other words,
\lca(Y)=u
always implies that the

\preceq_{N}
-minimal ancestor of the elements in
Y
exists and is
uniquely determined. We leave
\lca(Y)
undefined for all
Y
with

|\LCA(Y)|\neq 1
.

In
[
33
]
, least common ancestors
u
are defined in terms of the fact
that no child of
u
is an ancestor of all vertices in
Y
. These
definitions are equivalent:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
N
be a network and
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq V
. Then
u\in V
is
a least common ancestor of
Y
if and only if
u
is an ancestor of all
vertices in
Y
but there is no
v\in\child_{N}(u)
that is an ancestor of
all vertices in
Y
.

Proof.

By definition,
u\in V
is a least common ancestor of
Y
if it is
ancestor of all vertices in
Y
and
\preceq_{N}
-minimal w.r.t. this
property. Thus the
only if
-part of the statement follows
immediately. Conversely, suppose
u
is an ancestor of all vertices in

Y
but there is no
v\in\child_{N}(u)
with this property. Writing
D_{x}

for the set of descendants of a vertex
x\in V
, suppose conversely that

u
is an ancestor of all vertices in
Y
but
Y\not\subseteq D_{v}
for
each
v\in\child_{N}(u)
. For every vertex
w\in V(N)
with
w\prec_{N}u
,
there is a directed path passing through some child
v\in\child_{N}(u)
,
i.e.,
w\preceq_{N}v
. Hence, we have
D_{w}\subseteq D_{v}
. Together with

Y\not\subseteq D_{v}
, this implies
Y\not\subseteq D_{w}
. Hence,
u
is a
least common ancestor of
Y
.
∎

We will in particular be concerned here with LCAs of leaves, i.e., the sets

\LCA(A)
for non-empty subsets
A\subseteq X
. We can then express LCAs in
terms of clusters:

Observation 6.4
.

v\in\LCA(A)
if and only if
A\subseteq\CC(v)
and there is no
vertex
u\prec_{N}v
such that
A\subseteq\CC(u)
.

Suppose
\lca(A)\eqqcolon q
is defined for some non-empty
A\subseteq X
.
Then, by assumption, every vertex
v
with
A\subseteq\CC(v)
satisfies

q\preceq_{N}v
and thus
\CC(q)\subseteq\CC(v)
by
Lemma
3.33
. Since every vertex
v^{\prime}
for which

\CC(q)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
in particular also satisfies
A\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
,
we conclude that
\lca(\CC(q))=q
. Thus we have

Observation 6.5
.

Let
N
be a network,
\emptyset\neq A\subseteq X
, and suppose
\lca(A)

is defined. Then the following is satisfied:

(i)

\lca(A)\preceq_{N}v
for all
v
with
A\subseteq\CC(v)
.

(ii)

\CC(\lca(A))
is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster in

\mathscr{C}_{N}
containing
A
.

(iii)

\lca(\CC(\lca(A)))=\lca(A)
.

In much of the literature on least common ancestors in DAGs, only pairwise
LCAs are considered. Networks with unique pairwise LCAs are of interest
because of a close connection with so-called binary clustering systems

[
7
]
and monotone transit functions

[
16
,
17
]
.

Definition 6.6
.

[
7
]

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
is
pre-binary
if, for every
pair
x,y\in X
, there is a unique inclusion-minimal cluster
C
such that

\{x,y\}\subseteq C
.

From Obs.
6.5
(ii), we immediately obtain

Observation 6.7
.

If
N
is a network on
X
such that
\lca(\{x,y\})
is defined
for all
x,y\in X
, then
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is pre-binary.

Figure 12:
(A) A regular network that is not an lca-network even though all

\lca(\{x,y\})
is defined for all pairs. For
A=\{x,y,z\}
both
children of the root are contained in
\LCA(A)
. Here,
Y
is not a
cluster. (B) The existence of pairwise LCAs is also insufficient to
ensure that property (CL) is satisfied, i.e., that
\lca(\CC(v))
is
defined for all
v\in V(N)
since
A
is a cluster in this example.

The first example in Fig.
12
shows, however, that
unique pairwise LCAs are not sufficient to ensure that
\lca(A)
is also
defined for larger sets. The second example shows that unique pairwise
LCAs also do not ensure that all clusters
\CC(v)
have a unique LCA.

Willson
[
57
, Thm. 3.3]
showed that
\lca(\CC(v))
(there
called “mrca”) is well defined for all vertices of a normal network.
However,
[
57
]
uses a different definition of
X
as the
“base set” comprising the root, leaves, and all vertices with outdegree

1
. We therefore adapt Thm. 3.3 of
[
57
]
to our setting
and include a proof for completeness.

Definition 6.8
.

A network
N
has the
cluster-lca
property (CL) if

(CL)

For every
v\in V(N)
,
\lca(\CC(v))
is defined.

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose
N
has property (CL). Then for all
v\in V(N)
holds

\lca(\CC(v))\preceq_{N}v
and
\CC(\lca(\CC(v)))=\CC(v)
.

Proof.

If
v\notin\LCA(v)
then there is a descendant
v^{\prime}\preceq v
such that

\CC(v)=\CC(v^{\prime})
, and every
\preceq_{N}
-minimal descendant
v^{\prime}
with this
property satisfies
v^{\prime}\in\LCA(\CC(v))
. By property (CL),
\LCA(\CC(v))

contains only a single vertex
\lca(\CC(v))
, which therefore must
coincide either with
v
or one of its descendants. The second statement
now follows directly from the definition.
∎

In Lemma
4.7
, we saw that
Q(u)\coloneqq\{u^{\prime}\in V(N)\mid\CC(u^{\prime})=\CC(u)\}
forms an induced path in semi-regular
networks. Property (CL) imposes a weaker structure.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
N
be a network satisfying (CL). Then (i)
Q(u)
has a unique

\preceq_{N}
-minimal element, namely
\lca(\CC(u))=\min Q(u)
, and
(ii) if
u,v\in Q(u)
and
w
is contained in a directed path from

u
to
v
, then
w\in Q(u)
.

Proof.

The second statement in Lemma
6.9
implies that

q\coloneqq\lca(\CC(u))\in Q(u)
. By definition
q
is the unique

\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex that has all leaves in
\CC(q)
as its
descendants and thus
q\preceq u^{\prime}
for all
u^{\prime}\in Q(u)
, establishing
statement (i). Statement (ii) is a direct consequence of
Lemma
3.33
.
∎

Figure 13:
The network
N
satisfies (CL) but not (PCC). In addition to the
singletons, only
\{x,y\}
and
A=\{x,y,z\}
appear as clusters. Both
have unique last common ancestors, hence (CL) is satisfied. In
particular,
\lca_{N}(A)=w
. However, we have
A=\CC_{N}(u)=\CC_{N}(v)

for the two
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable vertices
u
and
v
. Hence,
N

does not satisfy (PCC).

Lemma 6.11
.

If a network
N
satisfies (PCC), then it satisfies (CL).

Proof.

Suppose that
N
satisfies (PCC). Given a cluster
\CC(u)
, we consider
the non-empty set
W\coloneqq\{w\in V\mid\CC(u)=\CC(w)\}\subseteq V
.
(PCC) implies that the elements of
W
are pairwise

\preceq_{N}
-comparable, and thus there is a unique
\preceq_{N}
-minimal
element
w\in W
. Furthermore,
\CC(u)=\CC(w)\subsetneq\CC(v)
implies

w\prec_{N}v
by Obs.
4.3
. Therefore,

\lca(\CC(u))=w
. Since
u\in W
by construction, we have
w\preceq_{N}u
,
and thus
\lca(\CC(u))\preceq_{N}u
.
∎

Fig.
13
shows that the converse is not true, i.e., (CL)
does not imply (PCC). Cor.
4.9
and
Lemma
6.11
imply

Proposition 6.12
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
there is a network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
that satisfies (CL).

Since a normal network is tree-child, it satisfies (PCC) by
Cor.
5.3
. This together with Lemma
6.11
implies

Corollary 6.13
.

Every normal network
N
satisfies (CL).

Property (CL) however does not imply that
\lca
is well-defined for all
subsets
A\subseteq X
.

6.2
Lca-Networks

Definition 6.14
.

A network
N
is an
lca-network
if
\lca(A)
is well-defined,
i.e., if
|\LCA(A)|=1
for all non-empty subsets
A\subseteq X
.

From Obs.
6.2
and uniqueness of the least common ancestors,
we immediately obtain

Observation 6.15
.

Let
N
be an lca-network and
A\subseteq B\subseteq X
. Then

\lca(A)\preceq_{N}\lca(B)
.

Corollary 6.16
.

Let
N
be an lca-network on
X
and
\emptyset\neq A\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)

for some
v\in V(N)
. Then, it holds
\lca_{N}(A)\preceq_{N}v
.

Proof.

Since
Y\subseteq\CC_{N}(v)
, there clearly exists some vertex

u\preceq_{N}v
such that
A\subseteq\CC_{N}(u)
but
A\not\subseteq\CC(w)
for all vertices
w\in V(N)
with
w\prec_{N}u
. By
Lemma
7.9
,
u
must be the unique vertex
\lca_{N}(A)
.
∎

Lemma 6.17
.

If
N
is a network with a closed clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}

and
N
satisfies (PCC), then it is an lca-network.

Proof.

Assume, for contradiction, that there is some set
A
with two distinct
vertices
u,v\in\LCA(A)
. Then
u,v
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable and

A\subseteq\CC(u)
and
A\subseteq\CC(v)
. If
\CC(u)\subseteq\CC(v)
,
then (PCC) implies
u\preceq_{N}v
or
v\preceq_{N}u
; a
contradiction. Similarly,
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(u)
is not possible. Thus

\CC(u)
and
\CC(v)
overlap. Since
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed, there is
a cluster
\CC(w)=\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
for some
w\in V
. Since

A\subseteq\CC(w)\subsetneq\CC(u)
and
A\subseteq\CC(w)\subsetneq\CC(v)
, Obs.
4.3
implies
w\prec_{N}u
and

w\prec_{N}v
, contradicting
u,v\in\LCA(A)
.
∎

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
N
be a network satisfying (PCC). Then
N
is an lca-network if and
only if its clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed.

Proof.

Let
N
be an lca-network satisfying (PCC). We show that
\mathscr{C}_{N}

is closed, i.e., for all non-empty
A\in 2^{X}
, it holds
\cl(A)=A\iff A\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
. From the definitions of
\LCA
, clusters, and the
closure operator we obtain

A\subseteq\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V\\
A\subseteq\CC(v)\end{subarray}}\CC(v)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}\\
A\subseteq C\end{subarray}}C=\cl(A)\,.

If
A\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
, then clearly
\cl(A)=A
. Now suppose

A\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
and assume, for contradiction, that

\cl(A)=A
. Thus, we have
\cl(A)\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
. Then there are at
least two distinct inclusion-minimal clusters
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
such that

A\subsetneq C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
. By Lemma
4.7
, there are unique

\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertices
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
with
C^{\prime}=\CC(u^{\prime})
and

C^{\prime\prime}=\CC(u^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore and by Lemma
3.33
, we obtain,
for all
v\prec_{N}u^{\prime}
, that
\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(u^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
and thus

A\not\subseteq\CC(v)
by inclusion-minimality of
C^{\prime}
. Hence,
u^{\prime}
is a
last common ancestor of
A
. By analogous arguments,
u^{\prime\prime}
is a last
common ancestor of
A
. Since
C^{\prime}\neq C^{\prime\prime}
,
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
are
distinct. Together with
\{u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\}\subseteq\LCA(A)
, this contradicts
that
N
is an lca-network. Therefore,
A\notin\mathscr{C}_{N}
implies

A\subsetneq\cl(A)
. Isotony of the closure function together with
the contraposition of the latter statement shows that
A=\cl(A)\implies A\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
. In summary, therefore,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed.
Conversely, by Lemma
6.17
, a network satisfying (PCC)
with a closed clustering system is an lca-network.
∎

The example in Fig.
13
shows that (PCC) cannot be omitted
in Theorem
6.18
. A trivial consequence of
Theorem
6.18
is

Corollary 6.19
.

A semi-regular networks
N
is an lca-network if and only if its clustering
system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed.

Since every tree-child network satisfies (PCC) we also obtain

Corollary 6.20
.

A tree-child network
N
is an lca-network if and only if its clustering
system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed.

Moreover, we have

Proposition 6.21
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is closed if and only if it
is the clustering system of some lca-network. In this case, the unique
regular network and the unique cluster network of
\mathscr{C}
are
lca-networks.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the clustering system. By
Prop.
3.42
and Thm.
4.25
,
there is a unique regular network
N
and a unique cluster network
N^{\prime}

with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. By
Thm.
4.8
and
4.19
,
resp., both networks are semi-regular and thus satisfy (PCC). Now apply
Thm.
6.18
.
∎

As in the case of trees, there is a simple connection of the LCA with the
closure operator:

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
N
be an lca-network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
.
Then the following identity holds:

\CC(\lca(Y))=\cl(Y)\quad\text{ for all }\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X.

(4)

Furthermore, we have

\CC(\lca(C))=\cl(C)=C\quad\text{ for all }C\in\mathscr{C}.

(5)

Proof.

Let
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X
. By Prop.
6.21
,

\mathscr{C}
is closed and thus
\cl(Y)\in\mathscr{C}
. In
particular,
\cl(Y)
is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster in

\mathscr{C}
containing
Y
. Let
u
be a vertex in
N
such that

\CC(u)=\cl(Y)
. Since
Y\subseteq\cl(Y)=\CC(u)
,
Cor.
6.16
yields
\lca(Y)\preceq_{N}u
. By
Lemma
3.33
, we have
Y\subseteq\CC(\lca(Y))\subseteq\CC(u)=\cl(Y)
. Since
\cl(Y)
is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster
in
\mathscr{C}
containing
Y
, this implies
\CC(\lca(Y))=\cl(Y)
.
For every cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}
, we have
C=\cl(C)
by
Equ. (
2
) and thus Equ. (
5
) follows
immediately.
∎

Next we show that two set sets have the same LCA whenever their LCAs are
associated with the same cluster.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
N
be an lca-network on
X
and let
Y,Y^{\prime}\subseteq X
. Then (i)

\lca(\CC(\lca(Y)))=\lca(Y)
and (ii)
\CC(\lca(Y))=\CC(\lca(Y^{\prime}))

implies
\lca(Y)=\lca(Y^{\prime})

Proof.

Since
\cl
is enlarging, i.e.,
Y\subseteq\cl(Y)
, we have

Y\subseteq\CC(\lca(Y))
by Lemma
6.22
. Thus
Obs.
6.15
implies
\lca(Y)\preceq_{N}\lca(\CC(\lca(Y)))
. On
the other hand, all leaves in
\CC(\lca(Y))
are descendants of

\lca(Y)
, and thus
\lca(\CC(\lca(Y)))\preceq_{N}\lca(Y)
. Thus statement
(i) holds. Now suppose
\CC(\lca(Y))=\CC(\lca(Y^{\prime}))
. Uniqueness of the
LCA implies
\lca(\CC(\lca(Y)))=\lca(\CC(\lca(Y^{\prime})))
and thus statement
(i) implies
\lca(Y)=\lca(Y^{\prime})
.
∎

6.3
Strong Lca-Networks and Weak Hierarchies

In this section, we consider an interesting subclass of lca-networks.

Definition 6.24
.

A network
N
on
X
is a
strong lca-network
if it is an
lca-network and, for every non-empty subset
A\subseteq X
, there are

x,y\in A
such that
\lca(\{x,y\})=\lca(A)
.

Fig.
14
shows an lca-network that is not a strong
lca-network.

Figure 14:
An lca-network with a subset of leaves
A\coloneqq\{x,y,z\}\subseteq X
in which there are no
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in A
such that

\lca(\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\})=\lca(A)
.

We shall see below that strong lca-networks are intimately connected with
well-studied types of clustering systems.

Definition 6.25
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
is

a
weak hierarchy
if
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\cap C_{3}\in\{C_{1}\cap C_{2},C_{1}\cap C_{3},C_{2}\cap C_{3},\emptyset\}
for all

C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
; and

binary
if it is pre-binary and, for every

C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a pair of vertices
x,y\in X
such that
C

is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster containing
x
and
y
.

Weak hierarchies have been studied in detail in
[
4
]
. Binary
clustering system are considered systematically in
[
7
]
.
We first consider the lca-networks with binary clustering systems:

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
N
be an lca-network. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

For all
v\in N
, there is
x,y\in\CC(v)
such that

\lca(\{x,y\})=\lca(\CC(v))
.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is binary.

Proof.

Since
N
is an lca-network, it is in particular pre-binary (cf. Obs.
6.7
) and satisfies (CL). Property (i) and
Lemma
6.9
imply

\CC(v)=\CC(\lca(\CC(v)))=\CC(\lca(\{x,y\}))
for two vertices

x,y\in\CC(v)
. By Obs.
6.5
(ii),
\CC(v)
is the unique
inclusion-minimal cluster containing
x
and
y
, i.e.,
\mathscr{C}_{N}

is binary. Conversely, suppose
N
is an lca-network with a binary
clustering system. Then for every
v\in N
, there is
x,y\in X
such that

\CC(v)
is the unique inclusion-minimal cluster that contains
x
and

y
. By Obs.
6.5
(ii), this implies

\CC(v)=\CC(\lca(\{x,y\}))
. Hence, we have

\lca(\CC(v))=\lca(\CC(\lca(\{x,y\})))
, and thus, by
Obs.
6.5
(iii),

\lca(\CC(v))=\lca(\CC(\lca(\{x,y\})))=\lca(\{x,y\})
, i.e., property (i)
holds.
∎

In particular, therefore, strong lca-networks give rise to binary
clustering systems:

Corollary 6.27
.

The clustering system of a strong lca-network is binary.

The converse is not true in general, since condition (i) in
Lemma
6.26
requires only that the LCAs of clusters but not
necessarily the LCAs of all sets are determined by the LCA of a leaf
pair. The latter, stronger condition, is related to weak hierarchies. To
investigate this connection, we recall

Proposition 6.28
.

[
4
, Lemma 1]

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
is a weak hierarchy if and only if
for every nonempty subset
A\subseteq X
there exist
x,y\in A
such that

\cl(A)=\cl(\{x,y\})
.

Proposition 6.29
.

Let
N
be an lca-network on
N
. Then
N
is a strong lca-network if and
only of
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a weak hierarchy.

Proof.

Def.
6.24
and Equ. (
4
) imply that for every

\emptyset\neq A\subseteq X
there is
x,y\in A
such that

\cl(A)=\CC(\lca(A))=\CC(\lca(\{x,y\}))=\cl(\{x,y\})
, and thus

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a weak hierarchy by Prop.
6.28
. Conversely,
if
N
is an lca-network such that
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a weak hierarchy,
then for all
\emptyset\neq A\subseteq X
there is
x,y\in A
such that

\CC(\lca(A))=\CC(\lca(\{x,y\}))
. By Obs.
6.5
we have

\lca(A)=\lca(\CC(\lca(A)))=\lca(\CC(\lca(\{x,y\})))=\lca(\{x,y\})
, and
thus
N
is a strong lca-network.
∎

From Prop.
6.21
and Prop.
6.29
, we obtain

Corollary 6.30
.

Let
N
be a network satisfying (PCC). Then
N
is a strong lca-network
if and only if
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a closed weak hierarchy.

Furthermore, we can use the same arguments in the proof of
Prop.
6.21
together with
Cor.
6.30
to derive the final result of this
section:

Proposition 6.31
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is a closed weak hierarchy if and only
if it is the clustering system of a strong lca-network. In this case,
the unique regular network and the unique cluster network of

\mathscr{C}
are strong lca-networks.

7
Level-1 Networks

7.1
Basic Properties

We start by showing that all phylogenetic level-1 networks have the
path-cluster-comparability property (PCC).

Lemma 7.1
.

Every phylogenetic level-1 network satisfies (PCC).

Proof.

If
N
is a phylogenetic level-1 network, then it is tree-child by
Prop.
5.8
, and in turn every phylogenetic tree-child
network satisfies (PCC) by Cor.
5.3
.
∎

We note that “phylogenetic” cannot be dropped in
Lemma
7.1
. To see this, consider the level-
1
network
N

in Fig.
16
(B) where the two parents of the hybrid
vertex both correspond to cluster
\{a\}
but they are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable; a violation of (PCC).

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network. Then,
N
is least-resolved if and only if

N
is regular.

Proof.

By Cor.
3.12
and Thm.
4.8
, resp.,
least-resolved and regular networks do not contain vertices with
outdegree
1
, and thus they are phylogenetic. The statement now follows
immediately from Lemma
7.1
and
Thm.
4.17
.
∎

We emphasize, however, that there can exist least-resolved networks
N
for
a given clustering system
\mathscr{C}
that are not regular, as the
example in Fig.
7
shows. In this example, the
regular network
N^{\prime}
is level-
1
. Next we show that Lemma

3.30
does not hold for level-1 networks:

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then, there is no phylogenetic,
shortcut-free level-
1
network
N
on
n
leaves that is not a tree and
where
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

Proof.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic, shortcut-free level-
1
that is not a tree. By
Lemma
7.1
,
N
satisfies (PCC). Since, in addition,
N
is
shortcut-free,
N
is semi-regular. Since
N
is not a tree, it must
contain a non-trivial block
B
. By Lemma
4.12
,
there are at least two vertices
u
and
v
such that
\CC(u)
and

\CC(v)
overlap. Hence,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is not a hierarchy.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Thm.
4.8
and
Lemma
7.1
, we also obtain the following

Proposition 7.4
.

A phylogenetic level-1 network is semi-regular if and only if it is
shortcut-free. Furthermore, a level-1 network is regular if and only if
it is shortcut-free and has no vertex with outdegree
1
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic level-1 network and
v
be a hybrid-vertex of

N
. Then
\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(u)
for every
u\in V(N)
with
v\prec_{N}u
.

Proof.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic level-1 network and
v
be a hybrid vertex of

N
. By Lemma
3.22
,
v
and all of its
(at least two) parents are contained in a common non-trivial block

B
. Hence, consider first one of the parents
w_{1}
of
v
such that

w_{1}\neq\max B
. By Lemma
3.33
,
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(w_{1})
. Assume, for contradiction, that
\CC(v)=\CC(w_{1})
. Since

v
and
w_{1}
are contained in the same non-trivial block
B
and
N
is
level-1,
w_{1}
cannot be a hybrid vertex and thus, since
N
is
phylogenetic, we have
\outdeg_{N}(w_{1})\geq 2
. Let
w^{\prime}\neq v
be another
child of
w_{1}
. Again, by Lemma
3.33
,
\CC(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC(w_{1})
and thus,
\CC(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC(v)
. Lemma
7.1

implies that
N
satisfies (PCC), and thus,
v
and
w^{\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable. Hence, we distinguish the two cases (a)

w^{\prime}\prec_{N}v
and (b)
v\prec_{N}w^{\prime}
.

In Case (a), the arc
(w_{1},w^{\prime})
must be a shortcut since
w^{\prime}\prec_{N}v

and
v\in\child_{N}(w_{1})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
. In particular,
w^{\prime}
must be a
hybrid vertex and there is a directed path from
w_{1}
to
w^{\prime}
passing
through
v
, which together with the arc
(w_{1},w^{\prime})
forms an undirected
cycle. Hence,
w_{1}
,
v
, and
w^{\prime}
are contained in a common block that
shares the arc
(w_{1},v)
with
B
and thus equals
B
. But then
B

contains two hybrid vertices
v
and
w^{\prime}
that are distinct from
\max B
; a contradiction.

Now consider Case (b), i.e.,
v\prec_{N}w^{\prime}\prec_{N}w_{1}
. In this
case,
v
has a parent
w_{2}
such that
v\prec_{N}w_{2}\prec_{N}w_{1}\prec_{N}\max B
. In particular,
w_{2}
lies in
B
and
Lemma
3.33
implies
\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(w_{2})\subseteq\CC(w_{1})
and thus
\CC(v)=\CC(w_{2})
. Now we can repeat the latter
arguments for parent
w_{2}
and eventually encounter a contradiction as in
Case (a) or, if we never obtain such a contradiction, we end in an
infinite chain of vertices
w_{1}\succ_{N}w_{2}\succ_{N}\dots
; a
contradiction to
V(N)
being finite. The latter together with the fact
that
w_{1}\neq\max B
was chosen arbitrarily implies that

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)
for every parent
w
of
v
that is distinct
from
\max B
. Now suppose that
w=\max B
is a parent of
v
. Since
v

is a hybrid vertex, it has another parent
w^{\prime}
, which is also contained
in
B
and satisfies
v\prec_{N}w^{\prime}\prec_{N}w
. Lemma
3.33
and the arguments above thus yield

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w^{\prime})\subseteq\CC(w)
.

Finally note that
v\prec_{N}u
if and only if
v\prec_{N}w\preceq_{N}u

where
w
is a parent of
v
. This together with Lemma

3.33
implies that
\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(u)
for all
u\in V(N)
with
v\prec_{N}w\preceq_{N}u
and where
w
is
a parent of
v
.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network. Then every block
B
has a unique

\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex
\min B
and a unique
\preceq_{N}
-maximal
vertex
\max B
. In case
B
is not a single vertex or arc,

\min B
is the unique properly contained hybrid vertex in
B
and

\max B
is the unique root of
B
.

Proof.

The statement is trivial for a block that consists only of a single
vertex or arc. Uniqueness of the
\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex in
B

follows from Lemma
3.17
. Otherwise, every
v\in V(B)

lies on an undirected cycle. Since
B
is acyclic, a
\preceq_{N}
-minimal
vertex
u
in
B
does not have an out-neighbor along the cycle, and
therefore,
u
has at least two in-neighbors that are contained in
B
.
Thus
u
is a hybrid vertex and, by
Lemma
3.22
,
u
is properly contained in

B
. By definition of level-1, there is at most one such vertex in
B
.
∎

As an immediate consequence, we have

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network and
B
a block of
N
. For every
v\in B
,
it holds
\min B\preceq_{N}v\preceq_{N}\max B
and

\CC(\min B)\subseteq\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(\max B)
.

The fact that every block in a level-1 contains at most one hybrid vertex,
implies that
B^{0}=B\setminus\{\min B,\max B\}
for every block
B
(cf. Def.
3.20
).
Recall that a block is non-trivial if it is not a single vertex or a single
arc. Hence, a block
B
is non-trivial precisely if
B^{0}\neq\emptyset
. In
the absence of shortcuts and in case
B
is non-trivial, the subnetwork
induced by
B^{0}
is a forest consisting of at least two non-empty trees.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
N=(V,E)
be a level-1 network and suppose
u,v\in V
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Then
u
and
v
are located in a common block

B
of
N
if and only if
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
. In particular,
they share exactly the descendants of the
\preceq_{N}
-minimal element

\min B
of
B
, i.e., in this case we have
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\CC(\min B)
.

Proof.

If
u
and
v
are both located in block
B
, then
Cor.
7.7
implies
\emptyset\neq\CC(\min B)\subseteq\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
. Conversely, if
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\neq\emptyset
, then
Lemma
3.34
implies that, for every
x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
,
u
and
v
are contained in a common block
B
, and
B

contains, in addition, a hybrid vertex
w
such that
w\prec_{N}u,v
and

x\in\CC(w)
. Since
N
is level-
1
and
w\neq\max B
, we have
w=\min B
. Moreover, for all
x\in\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)
, the corresponding blocks

B
share
u
and
v
and are therefore identical by
Obs.
2.1
. Hence, we obtain
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)\subseteq\CC(\min B)
and thus
\CC(u)\cap\CC(v)=\CC(\min B)
.
∎

7.2
Clusters and Least Common Ancestors

An important property of level-1 networks that is not true in general
phylogenetic networks is the following.

Lemma 7.9
.

Every level-
1
network is an lca-network.

Proof.

Let
N
be a level-1 network on
X
and
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X
.
Suppose for contradiction that there are two distinct such vertices
u

and
u^{\prime}
for which
Y\subseteq\CC(u),\CC(u^{\prime})
and such that

Y\not\subseteq\CC(v)
whenever
v\prec_{N}u,u^{\prime}
. Clearly,
u
and
u^{\prime}

must be
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. From Lemma
7.8
and

\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq\CC(u)\cap\CC(u^{\prime})
, we obtain that
u
and
u^{\prime}

are located in the same block
B
and
\CC(u)\cap\CC(u^{\prime})=\CC(\min B)
.
In particular, therefore,
Y\subseteq\CC(\min B)
. Since
\min B\preceq_{T}u,u^{\prime}
by Cor.
7.7
and
u
and
u^{\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, we have
u\neq\min B
and
u^{\prime}\neq\min B
, and
thus
\min B\prec_{N}u,u^{\prime}
; a contradiction to
Y\not\subseteq\CC(v)
for
all
v\prec_{N}u,u^{\prime}
. Therefore, the least common ancestor is unique and

\lca_{N}(u)
is well-defined for all
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X
.
∎

By Lemma
7.9
, every leaf set in a level-1 network
N
has
a unique LCA. As a further consequence of Lemmas
6.3

and
7.9
, the following result, which was stated without
proof in
[
33
]
for binary level-1 networks, also holds in our more
general setting:

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network on
X
and
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X
. Then
there is a unique vertex
u
such that
Y\subseteq\CC(u)
but

Y\not\subseteq\CC(v)
for all
v\in\child(u)
. In this case,
u=\lca_{N}(Y)
.

Prop. 1 of
[
33
]
also states the following result (without proof) for
binary level-1 networks:

Lemma 7.11
.

Every level-1 network is a strong lca-network.

Proof.

Let
N
be a level-1 network. By Lemma
7.9
,
N
is an
lca-network. Thus, it remains to show that, for every
\emptyset\neq Y\subseteq X
, there are leaves
x,y\in X
such that

\lca(Y)=\lca(\{x,y\})
. The statement holds trivially if
Y=\{x\}
,
since then
\lca(\{x,x\})=\lca(Y)
. Hence, suppose now that
|Y|\geq 2
and thus that
v\coloneqq\lca(Y)
is not a leaf. By
Cor.
7.10
, every child
v^{\prime}\in\child(v)
satisfies

Y\not\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime})
and
Y\subseteq\CC(v)=\bigcup_{v^{\prime}\in\child(v)}\CC(v^{\prime})
, there are two distinct children

v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\child(v)
such that there is
x\in Y\cap\CC(v^{\prime})\setminus\CC(v^{\prime\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
y\in Y\cap\CC(v^{\prime\prime})\setminus\CC(v^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
. Since
\{x,y\}\subseteq Y
, we have

\lca(\{x,y\})\preceq_{N}v
by Lemma
7.9
and
Obs.
6.15
. Suppose for contradiction that

\lca(\{x,y\})\prec_{N}v
. Contraposition of Cor.
7.10

and
\{x,y\}\subseteq\CC(v)
implies that there is a child

v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\child(v)
with
\{x,y\}\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime\prime\prime})
. By the choice of

v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
we have
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}
.

We continue by showing that
v
,
v^{\prime}
, and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are located in a common
block
B
of
N
. Consider first the case that
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable. Then Lemma
3.33
,
y\in\CC(v^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
and
y\notin\CC(v^{\prime})
imply
v^{\prime}\prec_{N}v^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Hence, the three vertices

v,v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
lie on an undirected circle formed by the arcs

(v,v^{\prime})
and
(v,v^{\prime\prime\prime})
and a directed path from
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
. By Obs.
2.1
, the vertices
v
,
v^{\prime}
, and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are part of
a common block
B
. Assume now that
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, then
x\in\CC(v^{\prime})\cap\CC(v^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
Lemma
7.8
implies that
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are contained in
common non-trivial block
B
. If
v
is not contained in
B
, then
v

and arcs
(v,v^{\prime})
and
(v,v^{\prime\prime\prime})
can be added to
B
without losing
biconnectivity; contradicting that
B
is a maximal biconnected
subgraph. Hence,
v
is also contained in
B
. Similarly, one shows that

v
,
v^{\prime\prime}
, and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are located in a common block
B^{\prime}
of
N
. Since

B
and
B^{\prime}
share the arc
(v,v^{\prime\prime\prime})
, Obs.
2.2
implies
B=B^{\prime}
. In summary,
v
,
v^{\prime}
,

v^{\prime\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are all located in a common block
B
of
N
.

Now suppose again that
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-comparable. As
argued above, we have
v^{\prime}\prec_{N}v^{\prime\prime\prime}
and thus there is a directed path

P
from
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
. Since
N
is acyclic and
(v,v^{\prime\prime\prime})\in E(N)
, all
vertices
w
in
P
satisfy
w\prec_{N}v
. Together with
(v,v^{\prime})\in E(N)
,
this implies that
v^{\prime}
has at least indegree 2 and thus,
v^{\prime}
is a
hybrid-vertex of
B
. Since the hybrid vertex in each block of a level-1
network is unique, we have
v^{\prime}=\min B
. But then we have

x\in\CC(v^{\prime})=\CC(\min B)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime\prime})
by Cor.
7.7
; a
contradiction. Hence,
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
must be
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
and we can apply Lemma
7.8
to conclude that

\CC(v^{\prime})\cap\CC(v^{\prime\prime\prime})=\CC(\min B)
and thus
x\in\CC(\min B)
.
Cor.
7.7
therefore implies

x\in\CC(\min B)\subseteq\CC(v^{\prime\prime})
; a contradiction. In summary, the case

\lca(\{x,y\})\prec_{N}v
is not possible and hence we must have

\lca(\{x,y\})=v
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
7.11
(or alternatively
Lemmas
3.33
and
7.8
) and Prop.
6.31
,
we have:

Corollary 7.12
.

The clustering system of a level-1 network is a closed weak hierarchy.

This result also generalizes
[
24
, Prop. 1]
, who showed
that
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a weak hierarchy for binary level-1 networks.

In many applications, vertex- or arc-labeled networks are considered as a
scaffold to explain genomic sequence data

[
31
,
32
,
33
,
30
,
28
,
27
,
29
,
14
]
.
In this context, it is of considerable interest to understand the structure
of least-resolved networks that still explain the same data and
are obtained from the original network by shortcut removal and contraction
of arcs (cf. Def.
3.6
). Hence, it is important to
keep track of
\lca
’s after arcs have been contracted. To this end, we
provide the following

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network with leaf set
X
and
(v^{\prime},v)
be an arc
such that
v
is neither a hybrid vertex nor a leaf of
N
. Moreover, let

N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by application of

\contract(v^{\prime},v)
. Then, for all
x,y\in X
, we have
\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)=\lca_{N}(x,y)
whenever
\lca_{N}(x,y)\neq v^{\prime}
and, otherwise,

\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)=v
.

Proof.

Since
v
is not a hybrid vertex,
e=(v^{\prime},v)
is not a shortcut. Thus,

\contract(v^{\prime},v)
is well-defined. Let
x,y\in X
. If
x=y
, then

\lca_{N}(x,y)=x=\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)
. Hence, assume that
x\neq y
. Note,
by Lemma
3.25
,
N^{\prime}
remains a level-1 network. By
Cor.
7.10
, therefore,
\lca_{N}(x,y)
and
\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)

are well defined and, in particular, correspond to unique vertices in
N

and
N^{\prime}
, respectively.

Assume first that
\lca_{N}(x,y)=v^{\prime}
. Hence,
x,y\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
and there must
be children
c,c^{\prime}
of
v^{\prime}
such that
x\preceq_{N}c
and
y\preceq_{N}c^{\prime}
. By construction, each of
c
and
c^{\prime}
either equals
v
or becomes a
child of
v
in
N^{\prime}
. This and Lemma
3.10
(1) implies
that
x\preceq_{N^{\prime}}c\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
and
y\preceq_{N^{\prime}}c^{\prime}\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
. Cor.
6.16
and
x,y\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
imply

z\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
. Assume, for contradiction, that
z\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
Since

x,y\preceq_{N^{\prime}}z
, for
x
and
z
(resp.
y
and
z
) one of the
Cases (i) or (ii) as specified in Lemma
3.10
(2) must
hold.

Assume that Case (i) holds for both
x
and
z
as well as
y
and
z
,
i.e., we have
x,y\preceq_{N}z
. Note that Lemma
3.10
(2)
must hold for
z
and
v
as well. Hence, we have (i)
z\preceq_{N}v

or (ii)
z\preceq w^{\prime}
for some child
w^{\prime}\neq v
of
v^{\prime}
in
N
. For both
cases, we have
x,y\preceq_{N}z\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
; a contradiction to
v^{\prime}=\lca_{N}(x,y)
.

Assume now that Case (ii) is satisfied for
x
and
z
. In this case,

x\preceq_{N^{\prime}}z
implies, in particular, that
v\preceq_{N}z
. This and
Lemma
3.10
(1) implies that
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}z
. This
together with
