z\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
implies
z=v
; a contradiction. By
similar arguments, Case (ii) cannot hold for
y
and
z
. Hence, neither
of the Cases (i) or (ii) as specified in Lemma
3.10
(2)
hold for
x
and
z
(resp.
y
and
z
); a contradiction. Therefore,

\lca_{N}(x,y)=v^{\prime}
implies that
\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)=v
.

Assume now that
\lca_{N}(x,y)\neq v^{\prime}
. Let
z\coloneqq\lca_{N}(x,y)
and

z^{\prime}\coloneqq\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)
. Since
z\neq v^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\in V(N^{\prime})
, we can
conclude that
z,z^{\prime}\in V(N)\cap V(N^{\prime})
. Lemma
3.10
(1)
together with
x,y\prec_{N}z
implies
x,y\prec_{N^{\prime}}z
. Assume, for
contradiction, that
z\neq z^{\prime}
. We distinguish Cases (a)
z\prec_{N}z^{\prime}
, (b)
z^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
, and (c)
z
and
z^{\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.

In Case (a),
x,y\prec_{N}z\prec_{N}z^{\prime}
and
Lemma
3.10
(1) imply
x,y\prec_{N^{\prime}}z\prec_{N^{\prime}}z^{\prime}
; a
contradiction to
z^{\prime}=\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)
.

In Case (b), suppose first, for contradiction, that
x\not\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
. Together with
x\prec_{N^{\prime}}z^{\prime}
, this implies that Case (ii) in
Lemma
3.10
(2) must hold, i.e.,
v\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
and

x\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
for some
w^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus\{v\}
. In
particular, we have
x\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. Since
v^{\prime}
is the
only parent of
v
in
N
, the case
v\prec_{N}z^{\prime}
is not possible as it
would imply
v^{\prime}\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
and thus
x\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}\prec_{N}v^{\prime}\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
; a contradiction. Hence, we have
v=z^{\prime}
. Since

z^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
,
v^{\prime}\neq z
, and
v^{\prime}
is the only parent of
v=z^{\prime}
in
N
,
we must have
v^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
. Now consider
y\prec_{N^{\prime}}z^{\prime}(\prec_{N}v^{\prime})
which, by Lemma
3.10
(2), implies (i)
y\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
or (ii)
y\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
for some
w^{\prime\prime}\in\child_{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus\{v\}
. In any of the two cases, it holds
y\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. Hence, we have

x,y\prec_{N}v^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
; a contradiction to
z=\lca_{N}(x,y)
.
Therefore, it must hold
x\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
. By analogous arguments, it
holds
y\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
. Hence, we have
x,y\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
; a contradiction to
z=\lca_{N}(x,y)
.

In Case (c),
z
and
z^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. If

x,y\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
, then Cor.
6.16
implies

z=\lca_{N}(x,y)\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
; a contradiction. Hence, suppose
w.l.o.g. that
x\not\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}
. Re-using the arguments from
Case (b), this implies
v=z^{\prime}
and
x,y\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. The latter together
with Cor.
6.16
and
z\neq v^{\prime}
implies

z=\lca_{N}(x,y)\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. Hence, there is some child
c\in\child_{N}(v^{\prime})
with
z\preceq_{N}c
. Since
z
and
z^{\prime}=v
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, it holds
c\in\child_{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus\{v\}
.
By construction, therefore,
c
becomes a child of
v
in
N^{\prime}
, and thus,

c\prec_{N^{\prime}}v
. Together with Lemma
3.10
(1), the
latter arguments imply
x,y\prec_{N^{\prime}}z\preceq_{N^{\prime}}c\prec_{N^{\prime}}v=z^{\prime}
; a
contradiction to
z^{\prime}=\lca_{N^{\prime}}(x,y)
.

In summary, neither of Cases (a), (b), and (c) is possible. Therefore,

z=z^{\prime}
must hold.
∎

7.3
Property (L)

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
,
suppose
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
overlap, i.e.,

C_{1}\cap C_{2}\notin\{C_{1},C_{2},\emptyset\}
. Then,

C_{1}\cap C_{3}\in\{C_{1},C_{3},\emptyset,C_{1}\cap C_{2}\}
for all

C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in V(N)
be vertices such that
C_{1}=\CC(u_{2})
and

C_{2}=\CC(u_{2})
. Since
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
overlap, Lemma
3.33

implies that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, and thus, by
Lemma
7.8
,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are located in the same block
B
,
and
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\CC(\min B)
. In particular, we have
u_{1}\neq\max B

since otherwise
u_{2}\preceq_{N}u_{1}
(cf. Cor.
7.7
).
Now consider a vertex
w
with
C_{3}=\CC(w)
. If
w
and
u_{1}
are

\preceq_{N}
-comparable, then
C_{3}\subseteq C_{1}
or
C_{1}\subseteq C_{3}
by
Lemma
3.33
, and thus
C_{1}\cap C_{3}\in\{C_{1},C_{3}\}
. Now
consider the case where
w
and
u
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. If

C_{1}\cap C_{3}=\emptyset
, there is nothing to show. Otherwise, by
Lemma
7.8
,
u_{1}
and
w
are located in a common block
B^{\prime}

and
C_{1}\cap C_{3}=\CC(\min B^{\prime})
. We have
u_{1}\neq\max B^{\prime}
since
otherwise
w\preceq_{N}u_{1}
. Hence, we have
u_{1}\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
and thus
B=B^{\prime}
by Lemma
3.19
. Therefore

C_{1}\cap C_{3}=\CC(\min B)=C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.
∎

Inspection of the proof of Lemma
7.14
shows that there
are overlapping clusters only if
N
contains a non-trivial block and
thus a hybrid vertex. In particular, therefore, if
N
is a rooted tree,
then
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is a hierarchy.

Lemma
7.14
can be rephrased in a more concise form
with help of the following

Definition 7.15

(Property (L))
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
satisfies property (L) if

C_{1}\cap C_{2}=C_{1}\cap C_{3}
for all
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
where
C_{1}

overlaps both
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
.

For later reference we record an equivalent way of expressing property (L):

Corollary 7.16
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
satisfies property (L) if and only
if
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\in\{\emptyset,C_{1},C_{2},C_{1}\cap C\}

for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
that overlap with
C_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be chosen arbitrarily from
\mathscr{C}
.
If
C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}
,
C_{2}\subseteq C_{1}
or
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\emptyset
,
then
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\in\{\emptyset,C_{1},C_{2}\}
and there is nothing to show.
If neither of the latter cases is
satisfied, then
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
overlap.
Let
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
be the subset of cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}
that overlap with
C_{1}
.
By construction,
C_{1}
overlaps with all elements in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}

and
C_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
. Hence, Property (L) holds if and only if

C_{1}\cap C_{2}=C_{1}\cap C
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 7.17
.

The clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
of every level-1 network
N

satisfies property (L).

Proof.

Let
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
such that
C_{1}
overlaps both
C_{2}
and

C_{3}
. Hence,
C_{1}\cap C_{3}\notin\{C_{1},C_{3},\emptyset\}
. This together
with Lemma
7.14
implies that

C_{1}\cap C_{3}\in\{C_{1}\cap C_{2}\}
.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
satisfying Property (L) is a
weak hierarchy.

Proof.

If one of the three sets
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
is contained in another
one, or if one of three pairwise intersections is empty, then the
assertion follows immediately. If
C_{1}
overlaps both
C_{2}
and
C_{3}

then (L) implies
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=C_{1}\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}\cap C_{3}
.
∎

In order to show that closed clustering systems with property (L) define
level-1 networks, we first demonstrate that one can identify the
non-trivial blocks directly in a clustering system provided it satisfies
(L). We start by introducing subsets of clusters with a given overlap: For
a given clustering system
\mathscr{C}
and a set
C\in\mathscr{C}
, define

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\coloneqq\{C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C\}\mid\ \text{there is a }C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C\}\text{
s.t. }C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}=C\}\,.

(6)

Note that the clusters
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
appearing Eq.(
6
) are
different from
C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}
and thus, must overlap. Furthermore, we observe
that
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
and
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
if and only
if
C
is not the intersection of two overlapping clusters. In particular,
we have

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system. Then,
\mathscr{C}
is closed
and
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
if and only
if there is a phylogenetic tree
T
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{T}
.

Proof.

First, let
T
be phylogenetic tree with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{T}
.
By Cor.
7.12
,
\mathscr{C}
must be
closed. Moreover, since
T
is a tree, we have

C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}\in\{C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime},\emptyset\}
and thus,

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
since
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
must satisfy
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\neq C
.

Assume now that
\mathscr{C}
is closed and
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset

for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is closed, Lemma

3.32
implies that
C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
for all

C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
whenever
C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore, we
have
C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}\in\{C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime},\emptyset\}
for all

C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
since otherwise
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlap and we
obtain
C^{\prime},C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(D)\neq\emptyset
for

D=C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
; a contradiction. Thus,
\mathscr{C}
is a
hierarchy and, by
[
48
, Thm. 3.5.2]
, there is a 1-to-1
correspondence between hierarchies and phylogenetic trees
T
such that

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{T}
.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L), and

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then every
cluster
D\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
satisfies ones the
the following alternatives: (i)
D\subseteq C
, (ii)
D\cap C=\emptyset
,
or (iii)
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
.

Proof.

Consider a cluster
D\in\mathscr{C}
with
D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. By
contraposition, assume that none of the alternatives (i), (ii) and (iii)
are satisfied. Hence, suppose
D\not\subseteq C
, i.e.,

D\setminus C\neq\emptyset
, and
D\cap C\neq\emptyset
, and that there is
some set
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
such that
C^{\prime}\setminus D\neq\emptyset
. The case
C^{\prime}=D
cannot occur since

D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. By definition, there is

C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
such that
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlap with

C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}=C
. In particular, we have
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
and

C\subseteq C^{\prime\prime}
, and thus
D\cap C^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and

D\cap C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
. From
D\cap C^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and

C^{\prime}\setminus D\neq\emptyset
we infer that either
D\subseteq C^{\prime}
or
C^{\prime}

and
D
overlap. If
C^{\prime}
and
D
overlap, then (L) implies

C^{\prime}\cap D=C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}=C
, and thus
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
; a
contradiction. Thus we have
D\subseteq C^{\prime}
and hence

C^{\prime\prime}\setminus D\neq\emptyset
. Moreover,
D\subseteq C^{\prime}
,

C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}=C
, and
D\setminus C\neq\emptyset
imply that

D\setminus C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Together with
D\cap C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we
obtain that
C^{\prime\prime}
overlaps with both
C^{\prime}
and
D
. Now (L) implies

C^{\prime\prime}\cap D=C^{\prime\prime}\cap C^{\prime}=C
, and thus
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
.
∎

Corollary 7.21
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L), and

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then
C
does not
overlap with any cluster in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

If
D\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
, then Lemma
7.20

implies that (i)
D\subseteq C
, (ii)
D\cap C=\emptyset
, or (iii)

C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Hence,
D
does not overlap with
C
in any of the three
cases. If, on the other hand,
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
, then
C\subsetneq D
, and thus,
D
and
C
also do not overlap.
∎

Alternative (iii) in Lemma
7.20
can be expressed equivalently with
the help of the set

U(C)\coloneqq\bigcup_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)}C^{\prime}\,.

(7)

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L) and let

C\in\mathscr{C}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then,

D\in\mathscr{C}
satisfies
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
for all

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
if and only if
U(C)\subseteq D
.

Proof.

The “only if statement” follows directly from the definition of
U(C)
.
To see the “if” direction, note first that
U(C)\subseteq D
implies

D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
since otherwise there would be a

C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
overlapping
D
, which is impossible because

C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq U(C)\subseteq D
. Furthermore, we have
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
for
all
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
since
U(C)
, and thus also
D
, contains
at least one set overlapping
C^{\prime}
.
∎

As a consequence we can use the condition
U(C)\subseteq D
instead of
alternative (iii) in Lemma
7.20
. If the clustering system

\mathscr{C}
is closed,
U(C)\subseteq D
is equivalent to requiring

\cl(U(C))\subseteq\cl(D)=D
. Therefore we define

\Top(C)\coloneqq\cl\left(U(C)\right)

(8)

Alternative (iii) in Lemma
7.20
can now be expressed as

\Top(C)\subseteq D
.

Figure 15:
U(C)
is not necessarily a cluster. In both networks

\mathcal{B}(C)
for
C=\CC\{x\}=\{x\}
is the only non-trivial block.
The vertices with overlapping clusters, i.e., the set

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
is highlighted in cyan. In both networks

U(C)=\{w,x,y\}
. L.h.s.:
U(C)\subsetneq\Top(C)
. The additional
red vertex r.h.s., ensures that
U(C)=\Top(C)
is a cluster.

In general,
U(C)\notin\mathscr{C}
, see Fig.
15
. Nevertheless,

\Top(C)\in\mathscr{C}
for all
C
of a closed clustering system

\mathscr{C}
that satisfy
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system. Then,

\Top(C)\in\mathscr{C}
and
C\subseteq\Top(C)
for all

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system on
X
. Since

\mathscr{C}
is closed we have, by definition,
\cl(A)=A
if and only if

A\in\mathscr{C}
for all non-empty sets
A\subseteq 2^{X}
. Let

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Hence,

U(C)\neq\emptyset
. Since
\cl
is enlarging, we have

U(C)\subseteq\cl(U(C))=\Top(C)
and, therefore,
\Top(C)\neq\emptyset
. In
particular,
\Top(C)\subseteq 2^{X}
. Since
\cl
is idempotent, we have

\cl(\Top(C))=\cl(\cl(U(C)))=\cl(U(C))=\Top(C)
. Taking the latter
arguments together, we obtain
\Top(C)\in\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
, then it is straightforward to verify
that
C\subseteq U(C)\subseteq\Top(C)
.
∎

Although
U(C)\notin\mathscr{C}
, it has an interesting property:

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L), and

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then
U(C)
does
not overlap any cluster
D\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
D\in\mathscr{C}
. If
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
, then

D\subseteq U\coloneqq U(C)
by construction. If

D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
, we consider the three alternatives of
Lemma
7.20
. In case (i) we have
D\subseteq C\subseteq U
and in
case (iii) we have
U\subseteq\Top(C)\subseteq D
. In case (ii) we have

D\cap C=\emptyset
. If
D\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
for all

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
, then
D\cap U=\emptyset
. Otherwise

C^{\prime}\cap D\neq\emptyset
for some
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Since

C\subseteq C^{\prime}
and
D\cap C=\emptyset
, we have
C^{\prime}\not\subseteq D
. However,
C^{\prime}
and
D
cannot overlap since in this case (L) implies

C^{\prime}\cap D=C
and thus,
D\in{B}^{0}(C)
, a contradiction. Therefore,

D\subseteq C^{\prime}\setminus C
, which implies
D\subsetneq U
. Thus
D
does
not overlap
U
.
∎

So far,
\Top(C)
is defined in terms of non-empty sets
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
.
We extend this notion to all clusters of a closed clustering system as
follows. Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system. We set

\Top(X)\coloneqq X
. For the remaining clusters
C\in\mathscr{C}
, i.e.,
those that satisfy
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
and
C\neq X
, we define

\Top(C)
as the unique inclusion-minimal cluster
C^{\prime}\neq C
that contains
C
.
To see that
\Top(C)
is well defined in this case, recall first that

X\in\mathscr{C}
and hence there is a cluster properly containing

C\neq X
. For uniqueness, suppose there are two distinct inclusion-minimal
clusters
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\neq C
that contain
C
. Clearly, these two supersets
overlap with
C\subseteq C^{*}\coloneqq C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}
. If
C=C^{*}
, then

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
; a contradiction. If
C\subsetneq C^{*}
, we
have
C^{*}\in\mathscr{C}
since
\mathscr{C}
is closed and thus

C\subsetneq C^{*}\subsetneq C,C^{\prime}
contradicts inclusion-minimality of
C

and
C^{\prime}
. Now set

\mathcal{B}(C)\coloneqq\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cup\{C,\Top(C)\}\text{ for all }C\in\mathscr{C}.

(9)

Corollary 7.25
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is a closed clustering system, then

\mathcal{B}(C)\subseteq\mathscr{C}
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
, we have by construction,

\mathcal{B}(C)\coloneqq\emptyset\cup\{C,C^{\prime}\}=\{C,C^{\prime}\}
, where
C^{\prime}
is
the unique inclusion-minimal element in
\mathscr{C}
that contains
C
.
Hence,
\mathcal{B}(C)\subseteq\mathscr{C}
. The latter covers in
particular also the case
C=X
. Otherwise, if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
, then Lemma
7.23

implies that
\Top(C)\in\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, by definition,

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\subseteq\mathscr{C}
. Taken the latter together with

C\in\mathscr{C}
implies
\mathcal{B}(C)\subseteq\mathscr{C}
.
∎

Lemma
7.20
then implies the following characterization of

\mathcal{B}(C)
:

Corollary 7.26
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Then, for
all
C,D\in\mathscr{C}
it holds that
D\in\mathcal{B}(C)
if and only if

C\subseteq D\subseteq\Top(C)
.

Proof.

Let
C,D\in\mathscr{C}
such that

D\in\mathcal{B}(C)=\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cup\{C,\Top(C)\}
. If

D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
or
D=C
, then
C\subseteq D
and, by
construction,
D\subseteq\Top(C)
. If
D=\Top(C)
, we can apply
Lemma
7.23
to conclude that

C\subseteq\Top(C)=D
. Now, let
C\subseteq D\subseteq\Top(C)
and
assume, for contradiction, that
D\notin\mathcal{B}(C)
. Thus,

C\subsetneq D\subsetneq\Top(C)
and

D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. From
D\subsetneq\Top(C)
, we obtain

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Hence, we can apply Lemma
7.20
to
conclude that the cluster
D
satisfies one of the alternatives: (i)

D\subseteq C
, (ii)
D\cap C=\emptyset
, or (iii)
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
for all

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Based on the latter arguments only case (iii) can
occur, and hence Lemma
7.22
and Eq. (
8
) yield

\Top(C)\subseteq D
; a contradiction.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Cor.
7.26
we have

Corollary 7.27
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Then, for
all clusters
C\in\mathscr{C}
, the Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is an induced subgraph of the Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.

Furthermore, the sets
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
are pairwise disjoint:

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L) and let

C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
. Then
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
implies

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Furthermore, if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then
C=C^{\prime}
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
for all
distinct
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Then there is a set

C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
such that
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlap and
C=C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}
. Assume for contradiction that
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
,
i.e., there are two overlapping clusters
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
such that

C^{\prime}=D\cap D^{\prime}
. Since
C^{\prime\prime}
overlaps
C^{\prime}
it cannot be contained in both

D
and
D^{\prime}
since otherwise
C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq D\cap D^{\prime}=C^{\prime}
and hence,
C^{\prime}

and
C^{\prime\prime}
would not overlap. Thus, at least one of
C^{\prime\prime}\setminus D
and

C^{\prime\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
is non-empty, say
C^{\prime\prime}\setminus D\neq\emptyset
. Moreover,
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D
and
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlapping
each other imply that
D\setminus C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
and

D\cap C^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Hence,
C^{\prime\prime}
and
D
overlap. By (L),

C=C^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}\cap D=D^{\prime}\cap D=C^{\prime}
and thus

C^{\prime}=C\subseteq C^{\prime\prime}
; a contradiction to the assumption that
C^{\prime}
and

C^{\prime\prime}
overlap. Hence,
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
as claimed.
Now assume that
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
,
i.e., there is a cluster
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})

and clusters
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
and
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})
, both
of which overlap with
C^{\prime\prime}
, such that
C=C^{\prime\prime}\cap D
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}\cap D^{\prime}
. By (L), this implies
C=C^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 7.29
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Then

C\in\mathcal{C}
has indegree greater than one in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
if and only if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. In this case, all in-neighbors of
C
in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
are contained in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
.

Proof.

Suppose
C\in\mathcal{C}
has indegree greater than one in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Thus, let
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
be two
distinct in-neighbors of
C
. Hence,
C\subseteq D\cap D^{\prime}
and thus, by
closedness of
\mathscr{C}
and definition of

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
,
C=D\cap D^{\prime}
. In particular,
D
and
D^{\prime}

overlap. Therefore,
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. In
particular, since the in-neighbors
D
and
D^{\prime}
were chosen arbitrarily,
all in-neighbors of
C
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
are contained in

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Now suppose
C\in\mathcal{C}
has indegree zero or
one in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Clearly,
C
has indegree zero if
and only if
C=X
, in which case
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
. Suppose

C
has exactly one in-neighbor
C^{\prime}
and, for contradiction, that

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then there are two overlapping sets

D,D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
such that
C=D\cap D^{\prime}
, and thus directed
paths both from
D
and
D^{\prime}
to
C
. Both of these paths must pass
through
C^{\prime}
and thus,
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subseteq D\cap D^{\prime}
; a
contradiction. Hence, the
if
-direction must also hold.
∎

Lemmas
7.28
and
7.29
imply

Corollary 7.30
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L) and with
Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}
. For every
C\in\mathscr{C}
, the elements

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
have a unique in-neighbor in
\mathfrak{H}
. In
particular, this unique in-neighbor of
C^{\prime}
is always contained in

\mathcal{B}(C)
.

Proof.

The statement is trivially true for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
with

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
. Thus consider a set
C\in\mathscr{C}
with

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Note that
C\neq X
must hold. Consider
a cluster
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. It overlaps with some

C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
and thus
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq\Top(C)
. Therefore,
there is a directed path from
\Top(C)
to
C^{\prime}
and thus,
C^{\prime}
has an
in-neighbor
C^{*}
that satisfies

C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subsetneq C^{*}\subseteq\Top(C)
. By Cor.
7.26
,
we have
C^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(C)
. Lemma
7.28
and

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
imply
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Hence,
C^{\prime}

has indegree
1
by Lemma
7.29
, i.e.,

C^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(C)
is the unique in-neighbor of
C
.
∎

Lemma 7.31
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Let

C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. Then the induced
subgraph
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
of
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is
biconnected. In particular,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is a DAG with unique
source
\Top(C)
and unique sink
C
.

Proof.

By Cor.
7.27
,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is an induced
subgraph of
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. By Lemma
7.30
, all
clusters in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
have a unique in-neighbor in

\mathcal{B}(C)
. By Cor.
7.26
,
C^{\prime}\subseteq\Top(C)
holds
for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Therefore,
\Top(C)
has indegree
0

in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
and, moreover, there exists a directed path
from
\Top(C)
to every cluster
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. In particular,
by Cor.
7.26
, of the clusters in such paths are again
contained in
\mathcal{B}(C)
. Taken together, these arguments imply
that the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{C\})
is a tree
with root
\Top(C)
. Note that this tree is not necessarily
phylogenetic, i.e, there may exist clusters with outdegree
1
. However,
the outdegree of the root
\Top(C)
in

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{C\})
is at least two. To see this, let

C^{\prime}
be a cluster in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. As argued above,
there is a directed path from
\Top(C)
to
C^{\prime}
and this path only
contains clusters in
\mathcal{B}(C)
. Therefore and since

C^{\prime}\neq\Top(C)
,
\Top(C)
has a child
D^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{H}
with

C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subseteq D^{\prime}\subsetneq\Top(C)
. By
Cor.
7.26
, we have
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(C)
, and thus,

D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Hence, there is
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
such
that
D^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlap. By similar argument as before, there is a
child
D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
of
\Top(C)
such that

C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq D^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq\Top(C)
. Now
C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq D^{\prime\prime}
and the
fact that
D^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
overlap imply that
D^{\prime}\neq D^{\prime\prime}
. Hence,
\Top(C)

has at least two children in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{C\})
.
Using Cor.
7.26
, we see that each leaf of the tree induced
by
\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{C\}
is an in-neighbor of
C
. It is now
easy to verify the graph obtained from (i) a rooted tree whose root has
at least two children and (ii) connecting its leaves to an additional
vertex is biconnected. Hence,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is
biconnected. In particular,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
features at least
two internally vertex disjoint directed path connecting
\Top(C)
and

C
, and any two vertices lie along a common “undirected” cycle (which
necessarily passes through
C
).
∎

Lemma 7.32
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Let

D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
for some
C\in\mathscr{C}
and let

D^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{B}(C)
be adjacent to
D
in the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}

of
\mathscr{C}
. Then

(i)

D
is the unique in-neighbor of
D^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{H}
and thus

D^{\prime}\subsetneq D
,

(ii)

D^{\prime}\cap C=\emptyset
, and

(iii)

if
D^{\prime}
overlaps with
some
D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, then there is
C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
such that

D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})
and
\Top(C^{\prime})=D
.

Proof.

We start with showing Property (i). By Cor.
7.30
, the
unique in-neighbor of
D
is contained in
\mathcal{B}(C)
. Thus,
D^{\prime}

must be an out-neighbor of
D
, i.e.,
D^{\prime}\subsetneq D
. If

\mathcal{B}^{0}(D^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then
Lemma
7.29
implies
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(D^{\prime})
.
Lemma
7.28
and
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(D^{\prime})
imply
D^{\prime}=C\in\mathcal{B}(C)
; a contradiction.
Hence,
\mathcal{B}^{0}(D^{\prime})=\emptyset
and in particular, by
Lemma
7.29
,
D^{\prime}
has indegree
1
, and thus

D
is its unique in-neighbor.

We continue with showing Property (ii). Since

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
and
D^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
,
Lemma
7.20
implies that (a)
D^{\prime}\subseteq C
, (b)

D^{\prime}\cap C=\emptyset
, or (c)
C^{\prime}\subsetneq D^{\prime}
for all

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. In Case (a),
D^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{B}(C)
implies

D^{\prime}\subsetneq C
. Since moreover
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
and thus

C\subsetneq D
, we have
D^{\prime}\subsetneq C\subsetneq D
, contradicting that

D^{\prime}
and
D
are adjacent in
\mathfrak{H}
. In Case (c), we obtain

D\subsetneq D^{\prime}
since
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
; contradicting

D^{\prime}\subsetneq D
. Hence, only Case (b)
D^{\prime}\cap C=\emptyset
can hold.

Finally, we show Property (iii). Suppose that
D^{\prime}
overlaps
D^{\prime\prime}
and set

C^{\prime}=D^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime\prime}
and thus,
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})
. Since

D^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(C)
it must hold that

C^{\prime}\neq C
and thus
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
by
Lemma
7.28
. Since
D
is the unique in-neighbor of
D^{\prime}

in
\mathfrak{H}
, Cor.
7.30
implies
D\in\mathcal{B}(C^{\prime})

and thus
D^{\prime}\subsetneq D\subseteq\Top(C^{\prime})
. On the other hand,

D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
implies
D\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})
and hence

D\not\subsetneq\Top(C^{\prime})
; Therefore
D=\Top(C^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 7.33
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Then each
subgraph
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
is
a non-trivial block of the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}
of
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.31
,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is
biconnected. Therefore and since
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
, the set

\mathcal{B}(C)
contains at least four clusters, i.e.,
C
,
\Top(C)
,
and at least two overlapping clusters in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Thus, it
only remains to show that
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is a maximal
biconnected subgraph of
\mathfrak{H}
. Since moreover, by
Cor.
7.27
,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is an induced
subgraph of
\mathfrak{H}
,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is a maximal if and only
if there is no undirected cycle in
\mathfrak{H}
that contains an arc of

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
and a vertex not contained in
\mathcal{B}(C)

(cf. Obs.
2.1
). Assume, for contradiction, that
such a cycle
K
exists. Since
K
contains at least one arc of

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
, we can find a maximal subpath
P
of
K
on at
least two vertices and where all vertices of
P
are contained in

\mathcal{B}(C)
. In particular, the two distinct endpoints of
P
are
both incident with one cluster in
\mathcal{B}(C)
and one cluster that
is not in
\mathcal{B}(C)
. Clearly, at least one of the endpoints of
P

must be distinct from
\Top(C)
. Hence, we can pick an endpoint
D\in\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{\Top(C)\}
of
P
that is adjacent to

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(C)
and
D^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{B}(C)
, where both
C^{\prime}
and

D^{\prime}
are vertices in
K
. Therefore, it suffices to consider the two
mutually exclusive cases (a)
D=C
and (b)
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
:

(a)
D=C
. Hence,
C^{\prime}\neq C
and thus, by Cor.
7.26
,

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(C)\setminus\{C\}
implies
D=C\subsetneq C^{\prime}
and thus,

C^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
(cf. Equ. (
3
)). Suppose, for
contradiction, that
C
overlaps with some cluster

D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
. Then, since
\mathscr{C}
is closed, we have
C\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
for
E=C\cap C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
. However, this together
with Lemma
7.28
implies
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
; a
contradiction. Hence,
C
does not overlap any cluster. Furthermore, by
Lemma
7.29
, all in-neighbors of
C
are
contained in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\subsetneq\mathcal{B}(C)
. Therefore,

D^{\prime}
must be an out-neighbor of
C
and thus
D^{\prime}\subsetneq C
, which
implies
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(C)
. Hence, we can apply
Lemma
3.40
to conclude that there is no cycle
K

containing
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(C)
and
C^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
; a
contradiction.

(b)
D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. By Lemma
7.32
,
D
is the unique
in-neighbor of
D^{\prime}
. However, since
D^{\prime}
is located on the cycle
K
, it
must be adjacent to another vertex
D^{\prime\prime}\neq D
in
K
. Since
D
is the
unique in-neighbor of
D^{\prime}
it follows that
D^{\prime\prime}
must be an out-neighbor
of
D^{\prime}
and thus,
D^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq D^{\prime}
. By construction,

D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(D^{\prime})
and
D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(D^{\prime})
. If
D^{\prime}

does not overlap any cluster in
\mathscr{C}
, then we can apply
Lemma
3.40
to conclude that there is no cycle
K
in

\mathfrak{H}
containing
D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(D^{\prime})
and

D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(D^{\prime})
; a contradiction. Hence,
D^{\prime}
must
overlap with some cluster in
\mathscr{C}
. Then Lemma
7.32
(iii)
implies that there is
E\in\mathscr{C}
such that
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)

and
D=\Top(E)
. In particular, since
D\neq\Top(C)
, we have
D\neq E
.
Moreover, by Lemma
7.32
(ii), we have
D_{1}\cap C=\emptyset
for
all children
D_{1}
of
\Top(E)=D\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
with

D_{1}\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. In particular,
C\subsetneq D
, and thus we
have
U\coloneqq U(E)=\bigcup_{D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)}D_{1}\subsetneq\Top(E)=D
. On the other hand, we have
D_{1}\subsetneq U
for each of the
children of
\Top(E)
. Since
C\subsetneq D
,
D
has at least one child

F
such that
C\subseteq F
.
We continue with showing that
C\cap D_{1}=\emptyset
for all

D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
. Hence, let
D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
. By
Cor.
7.21
,
C
does not overlap with any cluster in

\mathscr{C}
. In particular, this yield
C\neq D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)

and
C
and
D_{1}
do not overlap. The case
C\subsetneq D_{1}
is not
possible since otherwise
C\subsetneq D_{1}\subsetneq\Top(E)=D\subsetneq\Top(C)
and Cor.
7.26
would imply that

D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Together with Lemma
7.28
, this
would imply
C=E
; a contradiction. Now suppose
D_{1}\subsetneq C
. Thus,
we have
E\subsetneq D_{1}\subsetneq C\subsetneq D=\Top(E)
. By
Cor.
7.26
, this implies
C\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
. However,
this is not possible because
C
does not overlap with any other cluster.
Hence,
C\cap D_{1}=\emptyset
must hold for all
D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
.
Therefore, we obtain
U\cap C=\emptyset
. Together with
U\subseteq D

and
C\subsetneq D
, this implies
U\subsetneq D=\Top(E)
. To summarize,
since
\mathscr{C}
is closed, it holds by definition that
\cl(U)=U\iff U\in\mathscr{C}
. The latter arguments taken together with

\Top(E)=\cl(U)
imply
U\notin\mathscr{C}
. consider

\mathscr{C}^{*}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}\cup\{U\}
. Clearly, the Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}^{*}
of
\mathscr{C}^{*}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{H}
by inserting a
extra vertex
U
as child of
D=\Top(E)
and re-attaching the children

D_{1}
of
\Top(E)
with
D_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
in
\mathfrak{H}
as children
of
U
in
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
, while the children
D_{2}
of
\Top(C)
with

D_{2}\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)
remain attached to
D
. In
\mathscr{C}^{*}

we therefore have
\Top(E)=U
. Since
U
does not overlap any set in

\mathscr{C}
by Lemma
7.24
,
\mathscr{C}^{*}
is again
a closed clustering system and satisfies (L). Moreover, since

U\subsetneq D
we have
U\neq X
and since
\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)\neq\emptyset
we have
|U|>1
. Hence, we can apply
Lemma
3.40

to conclude that
U
is a cut vertex in
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
and that there is no
cycle in
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
containing both a vertex in
\mathcal{D}(U)
and in

\overline{\mathcal{D}}(U)
. Since
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(C)
, we have

C\subseteq C^{\prime}
, which together with
U\cap C=\emptyset
implies that

C^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(U)
. Furthermore,
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(E)

implies that
D\subsetneq U
and thus,
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(U)
. Taking
the latter arguments together, there is no cycle in
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
that
contains both
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
. Since
\mathfrak{H}
is recovered from
\mathfrak{H}^{*}

by “contracting” the arc
UD
, there is no cycle in
\mathfrak{H}
that
contains both
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
; a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 7.34
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system on
X
satisfying (L). If

C\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{X\}
,
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
, and

C\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})
for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, then the arc

(\Top(C),C)
is a block in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
.

Proof.

We show that the arc
(\Top(C),C)
is not contained in any cycle in

\mathfrak{H}
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is closed and
C\notin\mathcal{B}^{0}(C^{\prime})

for all
C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, we know that
C
does not overlap any
cluster. By Lemma
3.40
, there is no cycle that intersects
both
\mathcal{D}(C)
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
. Since

C\subsetneq\Top(C)
, we have
\Top(C)\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
.
Furthermore, Lemma
7.29
and

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
imply that
\Top(C)
is the only in-neighbor
of
C
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Therefore, any cycle that contains

(\Top(C),C)
must contain some child
C^{\prime}
of
C
. Clearly,
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(C)
and thus such a cycle cannot exist as it would intersect
both
\mathcal{D}(C)
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}(C)
. Hence,

(\Top(C),C)
is a cut arc, and thus a block.
∎

We summarize Lemmas
7.33

and
7.34
in

Proposition 7.35
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system on
X
satisfying (L) and
with Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}
. Then,
B
is a block of
\mathfrak{H}
if and only
if
|X|=1
or
|X|>1
and
B=\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
for
some
C\in\mathscr{C}
that satisfies either (i)
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
or
(ii)
C\neq X
does not overlap any cluster and

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
. If
|X|=1
or in Case (ii)
B
is a
trivial block and, otherwise, in Case (i) a non-trivial one.

Proof.

If
|X|=1
, then
B=\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))=\mathfrak{H}
consists a
single
vertex only and is, therefore, a block of
\mathfrak{H}
. Assume that
|X|>1
.
By Lemma
7.33
and
Lemma
7.34
, each subgraph

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
with
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
is a
non-trivial block and
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
for which
C\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{X\}
does not overlap any cluster and

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
is a trivial block of the Hasse diagram

\mathfrak{H}
.

For the converse, suppose first that
B
is a trivial block of
\mathfrak{H}
,
i.e., it only consists of the single vertex
C
or the single arc

(C^{\prime},C)
. In the first case, we have
|X|=1
. Otherwise,

\mathfrak{H}
consists of
(C^{\prime},C)
and hence
|X|>1
. Moreover, we
have
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}\subseteq X
and thus
C\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{X\}
. If
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
, then, by
Lemma
7.29
,
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\subsetneq\mathcal{B}(C)
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is a non-trivial
block of
\mathfrak{H}
by Lemma
7.33
. In
particular, the arc
(C^{\prime},C)
is contained in this block, contradicting
that
(C^{\prime},C)
forms a trivial block. Hence, we have
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
. Assume, for contradiction, that
C
overlaps with some
cluster
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
. Then, by closedness of
\mathscr{C}
,

C\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(D)
for some
D\in\mathscr{C}
. Then, by
Cor.
7.30
,
C^{\prime}
is the unique in-neighbor of

C\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(D)
in
\mathfrak{H}
and
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(D)
. Hence,
C

and
C^{\prime}
are contained in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(D))
, which is non-trivial
as a consequence of
C\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(D)
and
Lemma
7.33
. This again contradicts that

(C^{\prime},C)
forms a trivial block. In summary, we have
C\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{X\}
,
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)=\emptyset
, and
C
does
not overlap any cluster. Suppose now that
B
is a non-trivial block of

\mathfrak{H}
. Hence,
|X|>1
and
B
contains an undirected cycle

K
on at least
3
clusters. Since
\mathfrak{H}
is a DAG,
K
contains at
least one cluster
C
with two in-neighbors
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
in
K
(and
thus in
\mathfrak{H}
). By Lemma
7.29
, we have

C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)
. Therefore,
Lemma
7.33
implies that

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
is a non-trivial block of
\mathfrak{H}
. In
particular,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
contains the arcs
C^{\prime}C
and
C^{\prime\prime}C
,
which are also arcs in
B
. By Obs.
2.2
, we
therefore obtain
B=\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{B}(C))
.
∎

7.4
Characterization of Clustering Systems of Level-1 Networks

We start with showing that a regular network is level-1 provided that its
clustering is closed and satisfied (L).

Proposition 7.36
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system on
X
satisfying (L).
Then the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}
of
\mathscr{C}
is a phylogenetic
level-1 network with leaf set
X_{\mathfrak{H}}\coloneqq\{\{x\}\mid x\in X\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.39
,
\mathfrak{H}
is a phylogenetic network
with leaf set
X_{\mathfrak{H}}\coloneqq\{\{x\}\mid x\in X\}
. To show
that
\mathfrak{H}
is level-1, we have to demonstrate that each block
B
of

\mathfrak{H}
contains at most one hybrid vertex that is distinct from the
unique maximum
\max B
. This holds trivially if
B
is a trivial block
consisting of a single arc or, if
|X|=1
, an isolated vertex.
Now suppose that
B
is a non-trivial block, and thus, by
Prop.
7.35
, it contains exactly the clusters in

\mathcal{B}(C)
for some
C\in\mathscr{C}
with

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
7.29
,

C
is a hybrid vertex. From Cor.
7.26
and the
construction of the Hasse diagram, we conclude that
\Top(C)=\max B
. By
Cor.
7.30
, none of the clusters in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)

is a hybrid vertex. Hence,
C
is the only hybrid vertex in

\mathcal{B}(C)=\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\cup\{C,\Top(C)\}
that is distinct from

\Top(C)=\max B
.
∎

Corollary 7.37
.

For every closed clustering system
\mathscr{C}
on
X
that satisfies
(L), there is a level-1 phylogenetic network
N
such that

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
.
In particular, the unique regular network with clustering system

\mathscr{C}
is level-1 and phylogenetic in this case.

Proof.

By Prop.
7.36
, the Hasse diagram
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})

is a phylogenetic level-1 network. Since
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is graph
isomorphic to the regular network
N
for
\mathscr{C}
,
N
is also a
level-1 phylogenetic network.
∎

We summarize Cor.
7.12
, Cor.
7.17
,
and Cor.
7.37
in the following characterization
of clustering systems that can be derived from level-1 phylogenetic
networks.

Theorem 7.38
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system. Then there is a level-1 network

N
such that
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
if and only if
\mathscr{C}
is
closed and satisfies (L).

We emphasize, however, that there is no 1-to-1 correspondence between
level-1 networks and clustering systems. Recall that a network
N
is
regular if
\varphi\colon V\to V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N}))\colon v\mapsto\CC(v)
is a graph isomorphism. In contrast to the unique regular network

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
, a level-1 network might have shortcuts and thus,
could even be not semi-regular and, therefore, not regular
(cf. Prop.
7.4
and
Thm.
4.8
). Nevertheless, a level-1 network
N
can easily
be edited into a level-1 network
N^{\prime}
that is isomorphic to

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
using two simple operations as specified in
Prop.
4.15
.

Proposition 7.39
.

For every level-1 network
N
, the regular network
N^{\prime}
with clustering
system
\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
is level-
1
and can be
obtained from
N
by repeatedly removing shortcuts and contracting arcs

(u,w)
with
\outdeg(u)=1
. In particular,
N^{\prime}
is the unique
least-resolved network w.r.t.
\mathscr{C}_{N}
that can be obtained from

N
in this way.

Proof.

Let
N
be a level-1 network. By Thm.
7.38
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is
closed and satisfies (L). By Cor.
7.37
,
therefore, the regular network with clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is
level-
1
. Now let
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by repeatedly
(1) removing a shortcut and (2) applying
\contract(u,w)
for an arc

(u,w)
with
\outdeg(u)=1
until neither operation is possible. By
construction,
N^{\prime}
is phylogenetic, shortcut-free, and contains no vertex
with outdegree
1
. It is easy to verify that the removal of shortcuts
cannot increase the level of the network. This together with
Lemma
3.25
implies that
N^{\prime}
is still level-
1
. By
Lemma
7.1
,
N^{\prime}
satisfies (PCC), and thus, it is
semi-regular. Thm.
4.8
now implies that
N^{\prime}
is
regular. Moreover, by Lemma
3.8
and
Lemma
3.11
, we have

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. By Prop.
3.42
,

N^{\prime}
is the unique regular network with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. The latter, in particular, implies
that the order of the operations “shortcut removal” and
“contractions” to obtain
N^{\prime}
from
N
does not matter. By
Cor.
7.2
,
N^{\prime}
is least-resolved.
Moreover, a network that still contains a shortcut or an arc
(u,w)
with

\outdeg(u)=1
cannot be least resolved by
Lemma
3.8
and
Lemma
3.11
, respectively. Taken together, the
latter arguments imply that
N^{\prime}
is the unique least-resolved network
w.r.t.
\mathscr{C}_{N}
that can be obtained from
N
by repeatedly
removing shortcuts and contracting arcs
(u,w)
with
\outdeg(u)=1
.
∎

As a direct consequence of Thm.
7.38
and
Prop.
7.39
together with the fact that regular
networks are phylogenetic, we obtain

Corollary 7.40
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system. Then there is a phylogenetic
level-1 network
N
such that
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
if and only if

\mathscr{C}
is closed and satisfies (L).

Corollary 7.41
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system that satisfies (L). Then
there is a unique shortcut-free phylogenetic level-1 network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
that moreover contains no vertex
v
with

\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
. This network
N
is regular and least-resolved.

Proof.

By Cor.
7.37
, the regular network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
is level-
1
. By Thm.
4.8
,

N
is shortcut-free and contains no vertex
v
with
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
.
Thus
N
is phylogenetic. Now let
N
be a shortcut-free phylogenetic
level-1 network with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
that moreover contains
no vertex
v
with
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
. By Lemma
7.1
and
Thm.
4.8
,
N
is a regular network, which is unique by
Prop.
3.42
. By
Cor.
7.2
,
N
is least-resolved.
∎

Most publications on phylogenetic networks assume that leaves always have
indegree
1
, see e.g.
[
36
]
.

Corollary 7.42
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system that satisfies (L). Then
there is a unique shortcut-free phylogenetic level-1 network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
such that every leaf has indegree
1

and all vertices
v
with
\outdeg_{N}(v)=1
are adjacent to leaves.

Proof.

By Cor.
7.41
, there is a unique shortcut-free
phylogenetic level-1 network
N^{\prime}
with
\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathscr{C}
and
for which no vertex has outdegree
1
. In
N^{\prime}
, all vertices with
outdegree
0
are leaves. Hence, we can simply apply
\expand(x)
for all
leaves
x
with
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(x)>1
. We can repeatedly (i.e., in each
expansion step) apply Lemma
3.13
to conclude that the
resulting digraph
N
is a phylogenetic network,
Cor.
3.14
to conclude that
N
is shortcut-free,
Lemma
3.27
to conclude that
N
is level-1 and
Lemma
3.13
to conclude that
N
satisfies

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathscr{C}
. In particular, every
leaf in
N
has indegree
1
by construction and all vertices with
outdegree
1
must be adjacent to leaves.

It remains to show that
N
is unique w.r.t. these properties. Let

\tilde{N}
be a phylogenetic shortcut-free level-1 network with

\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}=\mathscr{C}
and such that every leaf has
indegree
1
and all vertices
v
with
\outdeg_{\tilde{N}}(v)=1
are
adjacent to leaves. Hence, after application of
\contract(v,x)
to all
vertices
v
with outdegree
1
, we obtain a phylogenetic level-
1

network
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
that has no vertex with outdegree
1
at all.
Prop.
7.4
implies that
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
is regular and
Lemma
3.11
implies that
\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}^{\prime}}=\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}=\mathscr{C}
. By
Cor.
7.41
,
\tilde{N}^{\prime}\simeq N^{\prime}
. To obtain
N^{\prime}

from
\tilde{N}
, we applied precisely the “reversed” operation of the
operation to obtain
N
from
N^{\prime}
, which together with
\tilde{N}^{\prime}\simeq N^{\prime}
implies that
\tilde{N}\simeq N
. Hence,
N
is the unique network
with the desired properties.
∎

As an immediate consequence we obtain a characterization of the level-1
networks that are completely determined by the last common ancestor
function, and equivalently by their clusters.

Proposition 7.43
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network without shortcuts. Then the following
statements are equivalent:

(i)

\outdeg(v)\neq 1
for all
v\in V
.

(ii)

For every
v\in V
there is a pair of leaves
x,y\in X
such
that
v=\lca(\{x,y\})
.

Proof.

If
\outdeg(v)\neq 1
for all
v\in V
, then Prop.
7.4

implies that
N
is regular, i.e.,
\varphi\colon V\to V(\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N}))\colon v\mapsto\CC(v)
is a graph isomorphism
and thus a bijection. Therefore,
\CC(u)=\CC(u^{\prime})
implies
u=u^{\prime}
for all

u,u^{\prime}\in V
. Together with Eq. (
5
), i.e., the
identity
\CC(v)=\CC(\lca(\CC(v)))
, we obtain, for all
v\in V
, that

v=\lca(\CC(v))
and thus, by Lemma
7.11
, there is a pair of
leaves
x,y\in X
such that
v=\lca(\CC(v))=\lca(\{x,y\})
. Conversely,
suppose there is a vertex
v\in V
with a unique child
w
. Moreover,
assume for contradiction that there leaves
x,y\in X
such that

v=\lca(\{x,y\})
. Using Obs.
3.36
, we have

\{x,y\}\subseteq\CC(v)=\CC(w)
. Together with
w\prec_{N}v
, this
contradicts
v=\lca(\{x,y\})
.
∎

Finally, we show that every closed clustering system satisfying (L) is
represented by a unique “minimal” separated level-1 network. More
precisely, we have

Proposition 7.44
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system satisfying (L). Then
there is a unique separated phylogenetic shortcut-free level-1 network

N
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
. The network
N
is obtained from
the unique regular network
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
by applying
\expand(v)

to all hybrid vertices.

Proof.

By Cor.
7.41
, the unique regular network

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a level-1 network. By
Thm.
4.25
, there is a unique
semi-regular separated phylogenetic network
N
with

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{N}
, and this network is obtained from

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
by applying
\expand(v)
to all hybrid vertices.
The latter and Lemma
3.27
imply that
N
is also
level-1. Since
N
is semi-regular, it is shortcut-free. Hence, a
network with the desired properties exist. To see that
N
is unique,
let
\tilde{N}
be a separated phylogenetic shortcut-free level-1 network

\tilde{N}
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\tilde{N}}
. By
Lemma
7.1
, the shortcut-free network
\tilde{N}
satisfies
(PCC), and thus, it is semi-regular. In summary,
\tilde{N}
is a
semi-regular separated phylogenetic network with clustering system

\mathscr{C}
which is unique by
Thm.
4.25
.
∎

7.5
Compatibility of Clustering Systems and Intersection Closure

A frequent task in phylogenetics is the construction of networks based on
partial information of putative networks, e.g. subtrees

[
2
,
39
,
52
,
40
,
53
]
, subnetworks

[
35
,
54
,
49
]
, metrics or full
information about clusters
[
24
]
. A property or properties of
networks can be thought of as a subset
\mathbb{P}
of the set of all
rooted DAGs such that
N
has property
\mathbb{P}
whenever

N\in\mathbb{P}
. In this case we simply call
N
a
\mathbb{P}
-network. A
clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
is
compatible
w.r.t.
\mathbb{P}
-networks
if there is
\mathbb{P}
-network
N
on
X

such that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
.

Problem 7.45
.

Is a given clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}

compatible w.r.t. to (separated, phylogenetic) level-
k
networks?

We show that this question can easily be answered for level-1 networks by
computing the so-called “intersection-closure”
[
4
]
. To be
more precise, to every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
one can associate
the set
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
consisting of all non-empty intersections of an
arbitrary subset of clusters in
\mathscr{C}
.
Note that
A\in\mathscr{C}
implies
A\cap A=A\in\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
and so

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
. Recall that a clustering system satisfying (L)
is in particular a weak hierarchy (cf. Cor.
7.18
). In this
case, only pairwise intersections need to be considered since the
intersection of arbitrary subset of clusters coincides with a pairwise
intersection. As an immediate consequence, we have

Observation 7.46
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L).
Then
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}\cup\{C\cap C^{\prime}\mid C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\text{
overlap}\}
.

Lemma 1 of
[
4
]
asserts that
\mathscr{C}
is a weak hierarchy
if and only if
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is a weak hierarchy. We use this fact to prove an
analogous result for property (L).

Lemma 7.47
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) if and only if
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})

satisfies (L).

Proof.

Since (L) is a hereditary property, it suffices to show that, if

\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L), then
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
also satisfies (L). We show
first that the intersection of two overlapping clusters in
\mathscr{C}

cannot overlap any other cluster of
\mathscr{C}
. To this end, consider

C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
, and suppose that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
overlap and

(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\cap C_{3}\neq\emptyset
. Then, one easily verifies that
either
C_{3}\subseteq C_{1}\cap C_{2}
,
C_{1}\cup C_{2}\subseteq C_{3}
, or
C_{3}

overlaps at least one of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. In the latter case, (L) implies

C_{1}\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}
or
C_{2}\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}
, and thus

(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}
. That is, the intersection of two
overlapping clusters in
\mathscr{C}
cannot overlap any other cluster of

\mathscr{C}
. It remains to show that the intersection
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
of
an overlapping pair of clusters
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
also cannot
overlap with the intersection
C_{3}\cap C_{4}
of another overlapping pair

C_{3},C_{4}\in\mathscr{C}
. Assume, for contradiction, that
C_{1}\cap C_{2}

and
C_{3}\cap C_{4}
overlap. Hence we have
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\cap C_{3}\neq\emptyset
and
(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\cap C_{4}\neq\emptyset
and also

C_{3}\setminus(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\neq\emptyset
and
C_{4}\setminus(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\neq\emptyset
. Moreover,
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\subseteq C_{3}
and
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\subseteq C_{4}
are not possible at the same time since otherwise

C_{1}\cap C_{2}\subseteq C_{3}\cap C_{4}
. Hence,
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
overlaps at
least one of
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
; a contradiction. In summary, all
overlapping pairs
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
are formed by clusters

C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, and thus
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
also satisfies (L).
∎

Algorithm 1

Check-L1-Compatibility

1:

Clustering system
\mathscr{C}

2:

Verifies if
\mathscr{C}
is compatible w.r.t. to a
phylogenetic level-1 network, and in the affirmative case, returns such
a network

3:

if

|\mathscr{C}|>\binom{|X|}{2}+|X|

then

return
“
no solution
”

4:

if

\mathscr{C}
satisfies Property (L)
then

5:

\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})\leftarrow\mathscr{C}\cup\{C\cap C^{\prime}\mid C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\text{ overlap}\}

6:

return

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))

7:

else

return

‘‘no solution’’

Theorem 7.48
.

Let
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
be a clustering system. Then,

Check-L1-Compatibility
correctly verifies if there
is a (separated, phylogenetic) level-1 network on
X
such that

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
and can be implemented to run in

O(|\mathscr{C}|^{2}|X|)\subseteq O(|X|^{5})
time.
Moreover, such a
network
N
can be constructed in
O(|X|^{5})
time.

Proof.

The proof (in particular, the part concerning the time complexity) is
rather lengthy and technical and is, therefore, placed to
Section
A.3
in the Appendix. We emphasize, that the
proof, however, contains interesting insights for those readers who want
to implement algorithm.
∎

Theorem 7.49
.

For every clustering system
\mathscr{C}
the following statements are
equivalent:

1.

\mathscr{C}
is compatible w.r.t. to a (separated, phylogenetic)
level-1 network;

2.

There is a (separated, phylogenetic) level-1 network with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
;

3.

\mathscr{C}
satisfies Property (L).

4.

\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
is a level-1 network.

Proof.

If Statement (1) is satisfied, then the network computed with

Check-L1-Compatibility
is a network with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})

and thus, Statement (2) holds. If there is a (separated, phylogenetic)
level-1 network with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
, then Thm.
7.38

implies that
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
satisfies (L). Since (L) is a hereditary property,

\mathscr{C}
must satisfy (L) as well. Hence, (2) implies (3). Assume
that
\mathscr{C}
satisfies Property (L). By Lemma

7.47
,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
satisfies (L) and, by definition,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is
closed. By Thm.
7.38
and
Prop.
7.44
, there is a (separated,
phylogenetic) level-1 network such that
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}_{N}
. Since

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}_{N}
, Item (1) is satisfied. Hence,
Statements (1), (2) and (3) are equivalent. Assume that Statement (2)
holds. By Thm.
7.38
,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is closed and satisfies (L).
Cor.
7.41
implies that
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
is a level-1
network and thus, Statement (4) holds. Conversely, assume, that
Statement (4) is satisfied. Again, by Thm.
7.38
,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is closed
and satisfies (L). Prop.
7.44
implies
now Statement (2). In summary, the four statements are equivalent.
∎

8
Special Subclasses of Level-1 Networks

8.1
Galled Trees

In level-1 networks, the structure a block
B
is highly constrained if the
unique terminal vertex
v
of
B
has only two parents
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. The
absence of additional hybrid vertices implies, in particular, that the two
paths from
\max B
to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, are uniquely defined.

Observation 8.1
.

Let
N
be a level-1 network. Then every non-trivial block is an
(undirected) cycle if and only if every hybrid vertex
v
in
N

satisfies
\indeg(v)=2
.

We note that a similar result does not hold for level-
k
networks with

k>1
. As an example, Fig.
16
(A) shows two networks
whose hybrid vertices have all indegree
2
but whose blocks are not
(undirected) cycles.

Lemma 8.2
.

If
N
is a level-1 network and every hybrid vertex
v
in
N
satisfies

\indeg(v)=2
, then
N
is outerplanar.

Proof.

By Obs.
8.1
, every non-trivial block on
N
is a cycle.
Therefore, the underlying undirected graph of
N
does not contain a
subdivision of the graph
K_{4}
, i.e., the complete graph on
4
vertices,
nor of the complete bipartite graph graph
K_{2,3}
. By Thm. 1 in

[
18
]
,
N
is outerplanar.
∎

In
[
25
]
,
galled trees
were introduced as phylogenetic
networks in which all cycles are vertex disjoint. Here we consider a more
general version, where cycles are allowed to share a cutvertex and the
network is not required to be phylogenetic. More constrained types of
networks will be discussed in the subsequent sections.

Definition 8.3
.

A
galled tree
is a network in which every non-trivial block is an
(undirected) cycle.

Lemma 8.4
.

Every galled tree is level-1.

Proof.

Let
N
be a galled tree. Every trivial block
B
contains at most one
hybrid vertex distinct from
\max B
. Thus consider a non-trivial block

B
and assume, for contradiction, that
B
properly contains two hybrid
vertices
h
and
h^{\prime}
. Since
B
is a cycle, the two vertices in
B

that are adjacent with
h
must exactly be the two in-neighbors of

h
. The same holds for
h
. It is now easy to see that the two path in

B
that connect
h
and
h^{\prime}
must each contain a vertex whose two
neighbors in the cycle
B
are out-neighbors. Hence, these two distinct
vertices are
\preceq_{N}
-maximal in
B
, which contradicts the
uniqueness of
\max B
.
∎

Lemma
8.4
and Obs.
8.1
imply

Corollary 8.5
.

A network is a galled tree if and only if it is level-1 and satisfies

\indeg(v)=2
for all hybrid vertices
v
.

Observation 8.6
.

In a galled tree, every non-trivial block consists of two internally vertex
disjoint paths connecting
\max B
and
\min B
. Moreover, every vertex
contained in
B
that is distinct from
\max B
and
\min B
has precisely
one out-neighbor in
B
.

As we shall see below, this implies that its clustering system satisfies
the following property:

Definition 8.7
.

(N3O)

\mathscr{C}
contains no three distinct pairwise
overlapping clusters.

Lemma 8.8
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is the clustering system of a galled tree, then

\mathscr{C}
satisfies (N3O).

Proof.

Suppose there is a galled tree
N
with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
.
In particular,
N
is level-1 by Lemma
8.4
. Now
suppose, for contradiction, that (N3O) is not satisfied. Thus there are
three distinct vertices
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in V(N)
such that

C_{1}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(u_{1})
,
C_{2}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(u_{2})
, and

C_{3}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(u_{3})
overlap pairwise. By
Lemma
3.33
, it must hold that
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
are
pairwise
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. By Lemma
7.8
,

C_{1}\cap C_{2}\neq\emptyset
implies that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are located in a
common block
B
. Clearly,
\preceq_{N}
-incomparability of
u_{1}
and

u_{2}
implies
u_{1}\neq\max B
. By similar arguments,
u_{1}
and
u_{3}
are
located in a common block
B^{\prime}
and
u_{1}\neq\max B^{\prime}
. Since
u_{1}\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
, we can apply Lemma
3.19
to
conclude that
B=B^{\prime}
. Hence, for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, there is a
directed path
P_{i}
in
B
from
u_{i}
to
\min B
. Now consider, for
distinct
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
, the
\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex
v
in
P_{i}

that is also a vertex in
P_{j}
(which exists since
\min B
is a vertex
of both paths). We have
v\notin\{u_{i},u_{j}\}
because
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Therefore, the unique parents
v_{i}
and
v_{j}

of
v
in
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
, resp., must be distinct. Therefore,
v
is a
hybrid vertex in
B
and clearly distinct from
\max B
. Hence, it must
hold that
v=\min B
. Since
i
and
j
were chosen arbitrarily, the
paths
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
, and
P_{3}
only have vertex
\min B
in common. This
together with the fact that
\min B\notin\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}
implies that

\min B
has at least indegree 3. By Obs.
8.1
, therefore,

N
has a non-trivial block that is not an undirected cycle. Hence,
N

is not a galled tree; a contradiction.
∎

The converse of Lemma
8.8
is not true since, in
addition to (N3O), closedness and (L) are required:

Theorem 8.9
.

\mathscr{C}
is the clustering system of a galled tree if and only if

\mathscr{C}
is closed and satisfies (L) and (N3O). Moreover, in this
case,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a phylogenetic galled tree.

Proof.

Suppose first that
N
is the clustering system of a shortcut-free galled
tree. By Lemma
8.4
,
N
is level-1, and thus, it is
closed and
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) by Thm.
7.38
. By
Lemma
8.8
,
\mathcal{C}
also satisfies (N3O).
Now suppose, that
\mathscr{C}
is closed, satisfies
(L)
, and does not
contain three pairwise overlapping clusters. By
Cor.
7.41
, the unique regular network
N\coloneqq\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is a
shortcut-free phylogenetic level-1 network. Hence, it satisfies (PCC) by
Lemma
7.1
. Suppose, for contradiction that
N
is not a
galled tree, i.e., it contains a non-trivial block, that is not an
undirected cycle. By Obs.
8.1
, there is a hybrid vertex
w\in V(N)
with (at least) three distinct in-neighbors
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, and

u_{3}
. By Obs.
2.6
and since
N
is shortcut-free,
u_{1}
,

u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
must be pairwise
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. By
(PCC), it therefore holds
\CC_{N}(u_{i})\not\subseteq\CC_{N}(u_{j})
for all
distinct
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Moreover, it holds

\emptyset\neq\CC_{N}(w)\subseteq\CC_{N}(u_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Taken together, the latter two arguments imply that
\CC_{N}(u_{1})
,

\CC_{N}(u_{2})
, and
\CC_{N}(u_{3})
overlap pairwise; a contradiction.
Hence,
N
must be a galled tree.
∎

Definition 8.10
.

[
20
,
10
]
A clustering system

(X,\mathscr{C})
is
pre-pyramidal
if there exists a total order

\lessdot
on
X
such that, for every
C\in\mathscr{C}
and all

x,y\in C
, it holds that
x\lessdot u\lessdot y
implies
u\in C
. That
is, all clusters
C\in\mathscr{C}
are intervals w.r.t.
\lessdot
.

A necessary condition
[
46
,
17
]
for
\mathscr{C}
to be
pre-pyramidal is

(WP)

If
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
have pairwise non-empty
intersections, then one of the three sets is contained in the union of
the other two.

Taken together, (L) and (WP) imply (N3O). More precisely, we have

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a pre-pyramidal clustering system satisfying (L).
Then
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (N3O), i.e., there are no three pairwise
overlapping sets.

Proof.

Assume, for contradiction, that
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
overlap
pairwise. Then (L) implies that
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=C_{2}\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{3}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}\cap C_{3}\eqqcolon C\neq\emptyset
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is
pre-pyramidal and the three pairwise intersections are non-empty, (WP)
implies that one of the three sets is contained in the union of the other
two. W.l.o.g., suppose
C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}\cup C_{3}
. Equivalently,
C_{1}=C_{1}\cap(C_{2}\cup C_{3})=(C_{1}\cap C_{2})\cup(C_{1}\cap C_{3})=C\subsetneq C_{1}
, a
contradiction.
∎

Pre-pyramidal set systems are also known as “interval hypergraphs”. A
characterization in terms of an infinite series of forbidden
sub-hypergraphs has been developed in

[
51
,
50
,
22
]
. It can be used to obtain a simple
necessary and condition in the presence of (L).

Proposition 8.12
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system satisfying (L). Then

\mathscr{C}
is pre-pyramidal if and only if it satisfies (N3O).

Proof.

Starting from
[
22
, Thm. 7.2]
one observed that condition (L)
excludes all induced forbidden subhypergraphs with a single
exception. The remaining configuration, called
M_{1}
in

[
22
, Fig. 11]
, comprises three pairwise overlapping sets
that share at least one common point. Thus, if (N3O) holds, no

M_{1}
-subhypergraph is present in
\mathscr{C}
. Since (L) and (N3O)
together exclude all forbidden subhypergraphs and thus
\mathscr{C}
is
pre-pyramidal. Lemma
8.11
now completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic shortcut-free level-1 network with clustering
system
\mathscr{C}
. Then
\mathscr{C}
is pre-pyramidal if and only if

\indeg(v)\leq 2
for all
v\in V
, i.e., if and only if
N
is a galled
tree.

Proof.

(
\implies
) Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is pre-pyramidal with
corresponding total order
\lessdot
of
X
and, moreover, assume, for
contradiction, that
w
is hybrid vertex with
\indeg_{N}(w)\geq 3
. Hence,
let
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in\parent_{N}(w)
be pairwise distinct. Since
N
is
shortcut-free, Obs.
2.6
implies that
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, and

u_{3}
are pairwise
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Together with
Lemma
7.1
, this implies
\CC(u_{i})\not\subseteq\CC(u_{j})

for distinct
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Moreover,
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in\parent_{N}(w)

and Lemma
3.33
yield
\emptyset\neq\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(u_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, i.e.,
\mathscr{C}
contains three
pairwise overlapping clusters. On the other hand,
\mathscr{C}

satisfies (L) by Cor.
7.17
, thus Lemma
8.11
implies
that
\mathscr{C}
cannot contain three pairwise overlapping clusters;
a contradiction.

(
\impliedby
) Suppose
\indeg(v)\leq 2
for all
v\in V
. By
Obs.
8.1
, this holds if and only if
N
is a galled tree. By
Lemma
8.4
, Cor.
7.17
, and
Lemma
8.8
,
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) and
(N3O). Hence,
\mathscr{C}
is pre-pyramidal by
Prop.
8.12
.
∎

Definition 8.14
.

[
9
]

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is a
paired hierarchy
if a cluster

C\in\mathscr{C}
overlaps with at most one other cluster in
\mathscr{C}
.

Observation 8.15
.

Every hierarchy is a paired hierarchy and every paired hierarchy
satisfies (L) and (N30).

Proposition 8.16
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system. Then,
\mathscr{C}
is a
paired hierarchy if and only if there is a shortcut-free phylogenetic
galled tree
N
with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
where all non-trivial
blocks consist of four vertices.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mathscr{C}
is a paired hierarchy. Since

\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) and (N3O) by Obs.
8.15

and is closed, we can apply Thm.
8.9
to conclude
that
N\coloneqq\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a shortcut-free phylogenetic
galled tree with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
. Since
N
is, in
particular, a phylogenetic level-1 network
(cf. Lemma
8.4
), Lemma
7.1
implies
that
N
satisfies (PCC). By definition, every non-trivial block
contains at least
3
vertices. If a block
B
woulds contain exactly
three vertices, then one easily sees that
N
contains the shortcut

(\max B,\min B)
; a contradiction. Hence, every non-trivial block in

N
contains at least
4
vertices. Assume, for contradiction, that
N

contains a non-trivial block
B
with at least
k\geq 5
vertices. Note,

B
refers to an (undirected) cycle in
N
. Hence, there are two internal
vertex disjoint paths in
B
connecting
\max B
and
\min B
. Since
N

is shortcut-free and
k\geq 5
, we can conclude that one path contains a
vertex
v
and the other path contains vertices
u_{1},u_{2}
that are all
distinct from
\max B
and
\min B
. Since
B
is an undirected cycle,
one easily verifies that
v
and
u_{1}
as well as
v
and
u_{2}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. By Lemma
3.33
, we have

\emptyset\neq\CC(\min B)\subseteq\CC(v),\CC(u_{1}),\CC(u_{2})
. This
together with (PCC) and the fact that
v
and
u_{1}
as well as
v
and

u_{2}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable implies that
\CC(v)
must overlap
with both
\CC(u_{1})
and
\CC(u_{2})
; a contradiction.

Assume now that there is a shortcut-free phylogenetic galled tree
N

with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
where all non-trivial blocks consists of
four vertices. Suppose, for contradiction,
\CC(v)\in\mathscr{C}

overlaps with two distinct clusters
\CC(u_{2})
and
\CC(u_{2})
in

\mathscr{C}
. Note,
v
,
u_{1}
, and
u_{2}
must be pairwise distinct. By
Lemma
3.35
, we have
v,u_{1}\in B_{1}^{0}
and
v,u_{2}\in B_{2}^{0}

for non-trivial blocks
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
in
N
. In particular, we have

v,u_{1}\notin\{\min B_{1},\max B_{1}\}
and
v,u_{2}\notin\{\min B_{2},\max B_{2}\}
. We can therefore apply Lemma
3.19
to conclude
that
B_{1}=B_{2}\eqqcolon B
. In particular,
B
contains at least five
pairwise distinct vertices
\min B
,
\max B
,
v
,
u_{1}
, and
u_{2}
; a
contradiction.
∎

It is worth noting that for paired hierarchies, and in particular also for
hierarchies,
\mathscr{C}
there are not only galled trees but also
shortcut-free and phylogenetic level-
k
networks
N
that are not
level-
(k-1)
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
. Fig.
11
(A) serves as an
example.

8.2
Conventional and Separated Level-1 Networks

The literature on phylogenetic networks often stipulates that the leaves

v\in X
have indegree
1
, see
e.g.
[
36
]
. Furthermore, level-1 networks are
often defined such that every non-trivial block has exactly one hybrid
vertex.

Definition 8.17
.

A network
N
is
conventional
if (i) all leaves have indegree at
most
1
and (ii) every hybrid vertex is contained in a unique
non-trivial block.

We remark that, if
|X|>1
all leaves have indegree
1
in a
conventional network. In Fig.
3
, network
N
is
conventional, while
N^{\prime}
is not.

Proposition 8.18
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a closed clustering system on
X
satisfying (L).
Then
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is conventional if and only if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(\{x\})=\emptyset
for all
x\in X
and

\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Top(C))=\emptyset
for all
C\in\mathscr{C}
with

\mathcal{B}^{0}(C)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.39
,
\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})

is a phylogenetic network with leaf set
X_{\mathfrak{H}}\coloneqq\{\{x\}\mid x\in X\}
. Moreover, Lemma
7.29
implies
that
\indeg(\{x\})_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq 2
if and only if

\mathcal{B}^{0}(\{x\})\neq\emptyset
. By Obs.
2.1

and Lemma
3.19
, two distinct non-trivial blocks
B

and
B^{\prime}
share at most one vertex
v\in\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
. Thus every
hybrid vertex is contained in a unique non-trivial block if and only if

\max B\neq\min B^{\prime}
for any pair of non-trivial blocks. Since the
non-trivial blocks in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
are exactly the blocks

\mathcal{B}(C)\neq\emptyset
, this is equivalent to requiring that, for
every
C\in\mathscr{C}
with
\mathcal{B}(C)\neq\emptyset
we have

\Top(C)
is not the minimum of another non-trivial block, i.e.,

\mathcal{B}(\Top(C))=\emptyset
.
∎

Proposition 8.19
.

If
N
is separated, then
N
is conventional.

Proof.

Suppose
N
separated. Then all leaves in
N
must have indegree at most

1
since they have outdegree
0
and, by definition, hybrid vertices
have outdegree
1
. By Lemma
3.19
, a vertex
v
that
is contained in two non-trivial blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
must be the unique
maximal vertex of one of them. In this case,
Cor.
3.18
implies
\outdeg_{N}(v)\geq 2
. However,
since
v
is hybrid vertex in a separated network, we have

\outdeg(v)=1
. Therefore, such a hybrid vertex
v
that is contained in
two non-trivial blocks cannot exist. Hence,
N
is conventional.
∎

8.3
Binary Level-1 Networks

Recall that a network is
binary
if it is phylogenetic, separated,
and all vertices have in- and outdegree at most 2. Equivalently, in a
binary network, every tree vertex is either a leaf or has exactly two
children, and every hybrid vertex has exactly two parents and one child.
As an immediate consequence of the definition,
Prop.
5.8
, Prop.
8.19
, and
Cor.
8.5
we have:

Observation 8.20
.

Binary level-1 networks are always phylogenetic, separated, conventional,
tree-child, galled trees.

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
N
be a binary level-1 network. Then,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})\simeq N

if and only if
N
is a tree.

Proof.

Let
N
be a binary level-1 network. By definition,
N
is phylogenetic.
If
N
is a tree, then
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})\sim N

(cf. Cor.
3.47
). Assume now that
N
is not a tree and
thus,
N
contains hybrid vertices all with outdegree
1
in
N
.
However, Prop.
7.4
implies that
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
does
not contain any vertex with outdegree
1
. Consequently,

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})\not\sim N
.
∎

Hence,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C}_{N})
can never be binary in case
\mathscr{C}_{N}

contains overlapping clusters.

Definition 8.22

(2-Inc)
.

A clustering system
\mathscr{C}
has Property (2-Inc) if, for all
clusters
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there are at most two inclusion-maximal
clusters
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
with
A,B\subsetneq C
and at most two
inclusion-minimal clusters
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
with
C\subsetneq A,B
.

Lemma 8.23
.

Let
N
be a binary network that satisfies (PCC). Then
\mathscr{C}_{N}

satisfies (2-Inc).

Proof.

Let
N
be a binary network on
X
with clustering system
\mathscr{C}
.
Assume, for contradiction, that
\mathscr{C}_{N}
does not satisfy Property
(2-Inc) for some cluster
C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
.
Assume first that there are (at least) three inclusion-minimal clusters

A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
that satisfy
C\subsetneq A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
.
Hence,
C\neq X
. Since
C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
, there is a

\preceq_{N}
-maximal vertex
v\in V(N)
with
\CC(v)=C
. Note,
v
has
at least one but at most two parents in
N
since
N
is binary and

v\neq\rho_{N}
. Let
v_{i}
be a vertex in
N
with
\CC(v_{i})=A_{i}
,

i\in\{1,2,3\}
. By Obs.
4.3
, we have

v\prec_{N}v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
. Therefore, and because
v
has at most two
parents, at least two of
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
must be ancestors of the same
parent
w
of
v
in
N
. W.l.o.g. assume that
w\preceq_{N}v_{1},v_{2}
.
Since
v
is
\preceq_{N}
-maximal w.r.t.
\CC(v)=C
, it must hold that

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)
. Lemma
3.33
implies that

\CC(w)\subseteq\CC(v_{1}),\CC(v_{2})
. Note, however, that

\CC(w)=\CC(v_{1})
is not possible, since then
A_{1}\neq A_{2}
and

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)
imply that

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)=\CC(v_{1})\subsetneq\CC(v_{2})
; a contradiction
to the inclusion-minimality of
\CC(v_{2})=A_{2}
. By similar arguments,

\CC(w)=\CC(v_{2})
is not possible. Hence,

\CC(v)\subsetneq\CC(w)\subsetneq\CC(v_{1}),\CC(v_{2})
must hold; again
a contradiction to the inclusion-minimality of
\CC(v_{1})=A_{1}
and

\CC(v_{2})=A_{2}
.

Assume now that there are (at least) three inclusion-maximal clusters

A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\in\mathscr{C}
that satisfy
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subsetneq C
.
Hence,
C
cannot be a singleton. Since
C\in\mathscr{C}_{N}
, there is a

\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex
v\in V(N)
with
\CC(v)=C
. Since
C
is
not a singleton and
N
is binary, we can conclude that
v
has at least
one but at most two children in
N
. Let
v_{i}
be a vertex in
N
with

\CC(v_{i})=A_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Since
v
has at most two children,
at least two of
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
must be descendants of the same child
w

of
v
in
N
. Since
v
is
\preceq_{N}
-minimal w.r.t.
\CC(v)=C
it
must hold that
\CC(w)\subsetneq\CC(v)
. Now, we can apply similar
arguments as in the first case to obtain a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 8.24
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a clustering system on
X
. Then, there is a binary
level-1 network
N
with
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
if and only if

\mathscr{C}
is closed and satisfies Properties (L) and (2-Inc).
In this case, the (unique) cluster network with clustering system

\mathscr{C}
is a binary level-1 network.

Proof.

Assume first that
\mathscr{C}
is closed and satisfies Properties (L)
and (2-Inc). Taken Thm.
7.38
and
Prop.
7.39
together, the regular network

\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a level-1 network. Since
\mathscr{C}

satisfies (2-Inc), every vertex in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
must have in-
and outdegree at most 2. By Thm.
4.25
, we
can uniquely construct a cluster network
N
by applying
\expand(v)
to
all hybrid vertices of
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
. Hence,
N
is
binary. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})
is a level-1 network,
Lemma
3.27
implies that
N
is a level-1
network. Hence, a binary level-1 network
N
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
exists. The latter, in particular, shows
that the cluster network
N
is a binary level-1 network. Conversely,
suppose that
N
is a binary level-1 network on
X
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathscr{C}
. By Thm.
7.38
,
\mathscr{C}
is
closed and satisfies Property (L). By Lemma
7.1
,
N

satisfies (PCC) and thus, by Lemma
8.23
, is also
satisfies (2-Inc).
∎

Since a phylogenetic level-1 network satisfies (PCC) by
Lemma
7.1
, it is semi-regular if and only if
it is shortcut-free. Theorem
4.19
therefore
yields the following characterization of level-1 cluster networks:

Corollary 8.25
.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic level-1 network. Then
N
is a cluster network
if and only if it is shortcut-free and separated.

Obs.
8.20
then implies

Corollary 8.26
.

Let
N
be a binary level-1 network. Then
N
is a cluster network if and
only if it is shortcut-free.

We finally consider the problem as whether a clustering system

\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
compatible w.r.t. to a binary level-1 network.

Theorem 8.27
.

A given clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
is compatible w.r.t. to a binary level-1 network if and only if
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) and
all hybrid vertices
w
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
have
\indeg_{\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))}(w)=2
.

Proof.

Assume first that
\mathscr{C}
is compatible w.r.t. to a binary level-1
network and let
N
be such a network with

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
. By Thm.
8.24
,

\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L). Moreover, by Thm.
7.38
,

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed and thus

\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}_{N})=\mathscr{C}_{N}
. To see that
all hybrid vertices
w
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
have

\indeg_{\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))}(w)=2
, suppose for contradiction that there is a
vertex
w
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
with indegree larger than
2
, i.e., there is

C\in\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
with at least three distinct inclusion-minimal supersets

C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
. Since

C\subsetneq C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
