and these cluster are inclusion-minimal (and
thus not contained in one another), they overlap pairwise. By
Obs.
8.20
,
N
is a galled tree. Hence, by
Thm.
8.9
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
contains no three
pairwise overlapping clusters; a contradiction.

Assume now that
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) and that all hybrid vertices

w
in
\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
have
\indeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(w)=2
. In the
following we use caterpillars
\mathrm{CAT}_{n}
, i.e, binary trees on
n

leaves such that each inner vertex has exactly two children and the
subgraph induced by the inner vertices is a directed path with the root

\rho_{\mathrm{CAT}_{n}}
at one end of this path. By
Thm.
7.49
,
\mathfrak{H}
is a level-1 network. This together
with Obs.
8.1
implies that every non-trivial block in

\mathfrak{H}
is a cycle and thus,
\mathfrak{H}
must be a galled tree. In
particular, for every non-trivial block
B
,
\max B
has exactly two
children in
B
. Let
v
be vertex in
\mathfrak{H}
with

\outdeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(v)>2
. We now “resolve”
v
as follows: If

\indeg_{\mathfrak{H}}(v)=2
, then expand
v
. Otherwise,
v
is a tree vertex.
In this case, let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of all non-trivial blocks
B

in
\mathfrak{H}
with
v=\max B
and
\mathcal{C}
be the children of
v

that are not contained in some
B\in\mathcal{B}
. We now replace
v
by
a caterpillar
\mathrm{CAT}_{n}
with
n=|\mathcal{C}|+|\mathcal{B}|
and thus, we can find a
1-to-1 correspondence between the
n
leaves of the caterpillar and the
elements in
\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{B}
. The elements in

\mathcal{C}
are now identified with their corresponding leaves.
Observe that
|\mathcal{B}|>1
is possible, i.e.,
v=\max B
for
more than one non-trivial block
B
. We therefore re-attach, for each
block
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the two children of
v=\max B
that are
contained in
B
as children of the leaf of the caterpillar that
corresponds to
B
. Note that this construction is well-defined since, by
Lemma
3.19
, no two such children can be children of
two distinct blocks
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
. Since we do not change the
structure of non-trivial blocks, the network
N^{\prime}
obtained in this way
remains a level-1 network whose hybrid vertices still have indegree
2
.
Moreover, it is an easy task to verify that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{H}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
. Repeated application of
the latter steps to all vertices eventually results in a binary level-1
network
N
with
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{H}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
.
∎

Using
Check-L1-Compatibility
and the results in
Thm.
7.48
, we obtain

Corollary 8.28
.

Determining if a clustering system
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
is
compatible w.r.t. to a binary level-1 network and, in the affirmative
case, the construction of such a network can be done in
O(|X|^{5})

time.

Corollary 8.29
.

For all phylogenetic level-1 networks
N
whose hybrid vertices
w
have

\indeg_{N}(w)=2
, there is a binary level-1 network
N^{\prime}
with

\mathscr{C}_{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

If all hybrid vertices
w
in
N
have
\indeg_{N}(w)=2
, then
N
is a
galled tree by Obs.
8.1
.
By Thm.
8.9
,

\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed (i.e.,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
) and satisfies (L) and,
moreover,
\mathfrak{H}(\mathscr{C})=\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
is a galled tree.
Applying Obs.
8.1
again to
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
yields that all hybrid
vertices
w
in
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
have
\indeg_{\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))}(w)=2
. Now apply
Thm.
8.27
.
∎

The converse of Cor.
8.29
is not true, i.e., a
phylogenetic level-1 networks
N
for which there is a binary level-1
network
N^{\prime}
with
\mathscr{C}_{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N^{\prime}}
may contain a
hybrid vertex
w
with
\indeg_{N}(w)>2
. To see this, consider a binary
level-1 network
N^{\prime}
with a non-trivial block
B
such that
(\max B,\min B)\notin E(N^{\prime})
and the non-binary network
N
that is obtained from
N^{\prime}

by adding the shortcut
(\max B,\min B)
. Clearly,
N
is still
phylogenetic and level-
1
and by Lemma
3.8

satisfies
\CC_{N}=\CC_{N^{\prime}}
but the vertex
\min B
has indegree
3
.

8.4
Level-1 Networks Encoded by their Cluster Multisets

In this part we focus on a particular subclass of networks:

Definition 8.30
.

A network
N
is a
quasi-binary
if

\indeg_{N}(w)=2
and
\outdeg_{N}(w)=1
for every hybrid vertex
w\in V(N)

and, additionally,
\outdeg_{N}(\max B)=2

for every non-trivial block
B
in
N
.

We note that, in particular, all binary networks are quasi-binary.
Moreover, quasi-binary networks are separated. Therefore and by
Cor.
8.5
, we obtain

Observation 8.31
.

Quasi-binary level-1 networks are separated
galled trees.

Theorem
4.23
shows that all semi-regular networks are
encoded by their multisets of clusters. None of the conditions (PCC) or
shortcut-free can be omitted, as shown be the examples in
Fig.
8
. Nevertheless, replacing some of these
conditions by a different one might be possible. In the following, we
replace shortcut-freeness by requiring that
N
is a phylogenetic
quasi-binary level-1 network. In this case, we obtain Property (PCC) as an
immediate consequence of Lemma
7.1
. Again, we observe that
neither of the properties phylogenetic or quasi-binary or level-1 can be
dropped: Fig.
16
(A) shows two phylogenetic
quasi-binary networks that are not level-1;
Fig.
16
(B) shows two quasi-binary level-1 networks
that are not phylogenetic; Fig.
16
(C) shows two
phylogenetic level-1 networks that are not quasi-binary where, in all three
examples, the respective networks have the same multisets of clusters but
are not isomorphic. As a by-product, we obtain the following result:

Observation 8.32
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
denote the class of
all
networks
N
for which
either precisely one or at least one of the following conditions hold:

1.

N
is level-
k
,
k\geq 2
but not level-1 and contains at least
three
leaves.

2.

N
does not satisfy (PCC);

3.

N
is not quasi-binary;

4.

N
is not shortcut-free;

5.

N
is not phylogenetic.

Then, no network
N\in\mathbb{P}
is encoded (w.r.t.
\mathbb{P}
) by
its multiset
\mathscr{M}_{N}
of clusters and thus, by its set

\mathscr{C}_{N}
of clusters.

Figure 16:
Three pairs of non-isomorphic networks
N
and
N^{\prime}
for which

\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{N^{\prime}}
. (A)
N
and
N^{\prime}
are binary
level-2 tree-child networks. (B)
N
and
N^{\prime}
are separated galled
trees but not phylogenetic. (C)
N
and
N^{\prime}
are separated phylogenetic
level-1 networks.

Theorem 8.33
.

Let
N
be a phylogenetic quasi-binary level-1 network. Then

N
is the unique phylogenetic quasi-binary level-1 network
tree whose cluster multiset is
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.

Proof.

Suppose that both
N
and
\tilde{N}
are phylogenetic quasi-binary
level-1 networks and
\mathscr{M}_{N}=\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{N}}
.
Lemma
7.1
implies that both
N
and
\tilde{N}
satisfy
(PCC). We show that
\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{PCC}\colon V(N)\to V(\tilde{N})
is a graph isomorphism. By Lemma
4.22
,

\varphi
is a bijection between
V(N)
and
V(\tilde{N})
that is the
identity on the common leaf set
X
. In the following, we write

\tilde{v}\coloneqq\varphi(v)
for all
v\in V(N)
, and make free use of
the facts that, by Lemma
4.22
,

\CC_{N}(v)=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{v})
and
v
is a leaf if and only if

\tilde{v}
is a leaf, and moreover
u\prec_{N}v
if and only if

\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
for all
u,v\in V(N)
. In the
following, we will make frequent use of the fact that both
N
and

\tilde{N}
are galled trees (cf. Obs.
8.31
).

It remains to show that, for all
u,v\in V(N)
, it holds
(v,u)\in E(N)

if and only if
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\in E(\tilde{N})
. To this end,
suppose
(v,u)\in E(N)
and, for contradiction, that

(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\notin E(\tilde{N})
. Since
N
is acyclic and
finite, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
(v,u)\in E(N)
is a

\preceq_{N}
-minimal arc that is “missing” in
\tilde{N}
, i.e., there
is no arc
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in E(N)
with
u^{\prime}\prec_{N}u
and

(\tilde{v}^{\prime},\tilde{u}^{\prime})\notin E(\tilde{N})
. We have
u\prec_{N}v
and
thus also
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
. The latter together
with
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\notin E(\tilde{N})
implies that there is

\tilde{w}\in V(\tilde{N})
such that
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{w}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
. This in turn implies
u\prec_{N}w\prec_{N}v
, and thus,
(v,u)
is a shortcut in
N
. In particular,

u
is a hybrid vertex and, since by definition the non-trivial blocks in
the galled tree
N
correspond to the undirected cycles,
v=\max B
for
some non-trivial block
B
of
N
whose unique hybrid vertex is
u=\min B
.

We continue with showing that
\tilde{u}
is also a hybrid vertex. Since

N
is quasi-binary, the hybrid vertex
u
has a unique
child
c
. By the

\preceq_{N}
-minimal choice of
(v,u)\in E(N)
,
\tilde{c}
must be a
child of
\tilde{u}
in
\tilde{N}
. Suppose, for contradiction, that

\tilde{u}
is a tree vertex. Hence, since
\tilde{N}
is phylogenetic,
it must have a second child

\tilde{c}^{\prime}\in\child_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{u})\setminus\{\tilde{c}\}
. Now

\tilde{c}^{\prime}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{u}
implies that
c^{\prime}\prec_{N}u
.
Therefore and by the choice of
(v,u)
, there is an arc
(p,c^{\prime})\in V(N)

(where
p\neq u
since
c
is the only child of
u
) such that

(\tilde{p},\tilde{c}^{\prime})
is also an arc in
\tilde{N}
. Thus

\tilde{c}^{\prime}
is a hybrid vertex with distinct parents
\tilde{u}
and

\tilde{p}
. Hence, neither of
c^{\prime}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
is a leaf.
Therefore, and because
N
is phylogenetic,
c^{\prime}
either has a second
parent
p^{\prime}
(which is also distinct from
u
), or at least two
children. By the choice of
(v,u)
, the images of these vertices are
adjacent with
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
in both cases. Hence, if
c^{\prime}
has a second
parent
p^{\prime}
, then
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
has three distinct parents
\tilde{u}
,

\tilde{p}
, and
\tilde{p}^{\prime}
.
If on the other hand
c^{\prime}
has at least two children, then the
hybrid vertex
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
also has at least two children.
Both cases, therefore, contradict
that
\tilde{N}
is quasi-binary.
Hence,
\tilde{u}
must be a hybrid vertex.

Let
z
be the parent of the hybrid vertex
u
that is not
v
and
observe that
z\preceq_{N}w\prec_{N}\max B=v
. Moreover,
u\prec_{N}z

implies
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}
. Suppose, for
contradiction, that
(\tilde{z},\tilde{u})\notin E(\tilde{N})
. In this
case,
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}
implies that there is

\tilde{z}^{\prime}
with
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}^{\prime}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}
and thus
u\prec_{N}z^{\prime}\prec_{N}z
. Hence,
u
must have a
parent
z^{\prime\prime}
with
z^{\prime\prime}\preceq_{N}z^{\prime}\prec_{N}z(\prec_{N}v)
. Since
N

is acyclic, it holds that
z^{\prime}\neq v
and thus,
z^{\prime\prime}\neq z,v
. This
contradicts the fact that
z
and
v
are the only two parents of
u
in

N
. Therefore,
\tilde{z}
must be one of the two parents of
\tilde{u}

in
\tilde{N}
. In particular, it holds

\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{z}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
.

Now let
\tilde{q}
be the parent of the hybrid vertex
\tilde{u}
that
is not
\tilde{z}
. Since we assumed that
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\notin E(\tilde{N})
, it holds that
\tilde{q}\neq\tilde{v}
(and thus
q\neq z
). We have
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{q}
and thus
u\prec_{N}q
. Therefore and since
q\notin\{v,z\}=\parent_{N}(u)
, we must have

z\prec_{N}q
or
v\prec_{N}q
. Since
z\prec_{N}\max B=v
, we
have, in both of the latter two cases, that
z\prec_{N}q
. Moreover,
since the non-trivial block
B
consists of two paths that have only
v

and
u
in common and which do not contain additional hybrid vertices and
since
v
and
z
are the unique two parents of
u
, it holds that

z\prec_{N}q
and (a)
q\prec_{N}v
or (b)
v\prec_{N}q
. In
particular, we also have
(\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}})\;\tilde{z}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{q}
, which implies that

(\tilde{q},\tilde{u})
is a shortcut.

Figure 17:
Illustration of Cases (a) and (b) in the proof of
Thm.
8.33
. Dashed arrows indicate directed paths
(possibly consisting of a single vertex).

Case (a):

q\prec_{N}v
. The situation that we will obtain in the
following up to the final contradiction is illustrated in
Fig.
17
. Together with
z\prec_{N}q

and Lemma
3.16
,
q\prec_{N}v
implies that
q

is also contained in
B
. In particular,
\min B=u\prec_{N}q
and thus

q
is a tree vertex. Since
N
is a galled tree,
B
is exactly the
undirected cycle that is formed by the shortcut
(v,u)
and the directed
path from
v=\max B
to
u=\min B
(which contains
q
as an inner
vertex). Hence,
q
has exactly one child
c_{1}
in
B
and, since
N
is
phylogenetic, at least one
child that is not in
B
. Consider an arbitrary such child
c_{2}
that is
not in
B
. Since all vertices of
B
lie on a directed path and

z\prec_{N}q
, we must have
z\preceq_{N}c_{1}\prec_{N}\max B
. In
particular,
c_{1}
is also a tree vertex since
N
is level-
1
. The
vertices
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
must be
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. To see this,
suppose
c_{1}\prec_{N}c_{2}
. Then
(q,c_{1})
is a shortcut, and thus,
because
N
is a level-
1
network whose non-trivial blocks correspond to
undirected cycles,
N
contains a non-trivial block
B^{\prime}
that is formed
be the shortcut
(q,c_{1})
and a directed path from
q
to
c_{1}
that
passes through
c_{2}
. In particular,
q=\max B^{\prime}
and both of
c_{1}
and

c_{2}
are contained in
B^{\prime}
. Therefore,
B
and
B^{\prime}
share the two
vertices
q
and
c_{1}
and we have
B=B^{\prime}
by
Obs.
2.1
; a contradiction to
c_{2}\notin V(B)
.
Similarly,
c_{2}\prec_{N}c_{1}
is not possible and thus
c_{1}
and
c_{2}

are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable. Suppose, for contradiction, that there
is a vertex
x\in\CC_{N}(c_{1})\cap\CC_{N}(c_{2})
. Then,
Lemma
3.34
implies that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are contained in
some non-trivial block
B^{\prime}
of
N
. Since they are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable, it holds
c_{1}\neq\max B^{\prime}
, and thus, the
unique parent
q
of the tree vertex
c_{1}
must also be contained in

B^{\prime}
. Similar as before, we therefore obtain
B=B^{\prime}
, a contradiction to

c_{2}\notin V(B)
. Hence,
\CC_{N}(c_{1})
and
\CC_{N}(c_{2})
are
disjoint. In particular, we have
\CC_{N}(c_{1})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\CC_{N}(c_{2})\subseteq\CC_{N}(q)
, and, because both of
\CC_{N}(c_{1})

and
\CC_{N}(c_{2})
are non-empty, we obtain
\CC_{N}(c_{i})\subsetneq\CC_{N}(q)
,
i=1,2
. Since
c_{2}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have

\CC_{N}(c_{i})\subsetneq\CC_{N}(q)
for all children

c_{i}\in\child_{N}(q)
. Together with Lemma
3.33
and
the fact that every vertex
w^{\prime}
with
w^{\prime}\prec_{N}q
satisfies

w^{\prime}\preceq_{N}c_{i}
for some
c_{i}\in\child_{N}(q)
, these
inclusions imply that
\CC_{N}(w^{\prime})\subsetneq\CC_{N}(q)
. Hence,
q

is a
\preceq_{N}
-minimal vertex with cluster
C^{*}\coloneqq\CC_{N}(q)(=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{q}))
. Since
(\tilde{q},\tilde{u})
is a
shortcut,
\tilde{q}=\max\tilde{B}
of some non-trivial block

\tilde{B}
in
\tilde{N}
. Since
\tilde{N}
is quasi-binary,

\tilde{q}
has precisely two children
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{c}_{3}
.
Since
(\tilde{q},\tilde{u})
is a shortcut and the non-trivial blocks of

N
correspond to undirected cycles, we have
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{c}_{3}
and thus
\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{u})\subseteq\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{c}_{3})
by Lemma
3.33
. Therefore, we
obtain
\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{q})=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{u})\cup\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{c}_{3})=\CC_{\tilde{N}}(\tilde{c}_{3})
. Together
with
\tilde{c}_{3}\prec_{N}\tilde{q}
, this implies that
\tilde{q}
is
not a
\preceq_{\tilde{N}}
-minimal vertex with cluster
C^{*}
in

\tilde{N}
(as opposed to
q
in
N
); a contradiction to the
construction of
\varphi
. In summary, therefore, Case (a) cannot occur.

Case (b):

v\prec_{N}q
. This implies
\tilde{v}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{q}
. Since
(\tilde{q},\tilde{u})
is a shortcut, it holds

\tilde{q}=\max\tilde{B}
and
\tilde{u}=\min\tilde{B}
for some
non-trivial block
\tilde{B}
in

\tilde{N}
. Lemma
3.16
, together with

\tilde{v}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{q}
and
\tilde{u}\prec_{\tilde{N}}\tilde{v}
, implies that
\tilde{v}
is also contained in
\tilde{B}
.
We can now apply similar arguments as in Case (a), where the roles of
N

and
\tilde{N}
are interchanged, to conclude that Case (b) is also
impossible. The situation up to the final contradiction is again
illustrated in Fig.
17
.

In summary, therefore,
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\in E(\tilde{N})
must hold.
By analogous arguments,
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})\in E(\tilde{N})
implies

(v,u)\in E(N)
. Hence,
\varphi
is a graph isomorphism that is the
identity on
X
and thus
N\simeq\tilde{N}
. Therefore,
N
is the
unique phylogenetic quasi-binary level-
1
network
whose cluster multiset is
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.
∎

Our colleagues Simone Linz and Kristina Wicke drew our attention to an
alternative proof for Theorem
8.33
that proceeds by
induction on the size of the leaf set of
N
. It utilizes the concepts of
cherries and reticulated cherries that have been used extensively in the
literature, see e.g.
[
13
,
44
,
49
]
. We
opted for a non-inductive proof that remains closer to the construction
utilized throughout this contribution.

Corollary 8.34
.

Let
N
be a binary level-1 network. Then
N
is the unique binary
level-1 network whose cluster multiset is
\mathscr{M}_{N}
.

Again, we can observe that neither of the properties binary or level-1 in
Cor.
8.34
can be dropped:
Fig.
8
(A) shows two level-1 networks (where
one is not binary) and Fig.
16
(A) shows two binary
level-2 networks (that are not level-1) where, in both examples, the
respective networks have the same multisets of clusters but are not
isomorphic.

9
Summary

Table 3:
Summary of main results: Mutual dependencies between the types
of networks and clustering systems considered in this paper.
The properties highlighted in black and green refer to
properties of networks and clustering systems, respectively. Properties
in blue text are combinations of “basic” properties of networks. An
entry at position
(i,j)
in the matrix is colored orange, turquoise,
and white, if the property at pos.
i
implies
j
, is implied by
j
,
or does have a non-empty overlap with
j
, respectively. Gray colored
entries refer to equality. References within the matrix indicate the
result where the respective dependencies are shown. All other colors
in the matrix are either trivial observations or were derived by
computing the transitive closure over the proven implications.

In this contribution, we investigated the mutual dependencies between the
different concept of networks in the literature and their connection to
clustering systems. Most of our findings are summarized in
Table
3
. As one of the main results, level-1 networks
as well as some of their subclasses, such as galled trees or binary
phylogenetic level-1 networks, are characterized by the structure of their
clustering system
\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, we showed that semi-regular
networks and phylogenetic quasi-binary level-1 (and thus, binary level-1
networks) are uniquely determined by their multiset of clusters.
Furthermore, regular and cluster networks (and their subclasses as
e.g. phylogenetic trees) are uniquely determined by their clustering
system. We provided a plethora of examples that show, however, that most
classes of networks cannot be encoded in such way if there are not
sufficiently many extra restriction placed on such networks. In addition,
we showed that it is possible to determine in polynomial time whether a
clustering system is compatible with a level-1 network and to construct
such a network in the affirmative case.

It remains an open question as whether general level-
k
networks can be
characterized by their clustering systems. Moreover, under which
conditions is a clustering system compatible with other specified networks
and what is the computational complexity to determine them? While we have
shown that some types of networks can be encoded by the multisets of
clusters, a characterization of multisets that encode the underlying
networks as well as reconstruction algorithms are part of future research.

From the point of view of clustering systems, phylogenetic networks suggest
properties that may also be of relevance in practical data analysis beyond
applications in phylogenetics. A clustering systems between hierarchies and
weak hierarchies
[
11
]
, (L)-clustering systems appear as an
attractive alternative e.g. to pyramidal clustering
[
10
]
for
data that are not naturally linearly ordered.

Appendix A
Additional Results and Proofs

A.1
Expansion, Contraction, and Blocks

We first provide the proof of

Lemma
.

3.10

Let
N
be a network and
(u,w)\in E(N)
be an arc that is not a
shortcut. Then,
\contract(u,w)
applied on
N
results in a network

N^{\prime}
with leaf set
X
or
X\setminus\{w\}
and

V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{u\}
. Moreover, for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V(N^{\prime})
,

1.

v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
implies
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
, and

2.

v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
implies
(i)
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
or
(ii)
w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
and
v\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
for some

w^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
that is
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable
with
w
.

In particular,
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
always implies
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
or
v
and

v^{\prime}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.

Proof.

Assume that
(u,w)
is not a shortcut in
N
and recall that

\contract(u,w)
consists in replacing arcs
(v,u)
by
(v,w)
for all

v\in\parent_{N}(u)
, replacing arcs
(u,v)
by
(w,v)
for all

v\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
, and deleting
(u,w)
and
u
. Observe
that only the arcs incident with
u
are removed and all inserted arcs
are incident with
w
. While
u
does not exist in
N^{\prime}
anymore, both
the in- and out-neighborhood of
w
may change in such a way that
w
may
get additional in/out-neighbors of
u
.

We show first that
N^{\prime}
has a single vertex with indegree
0
. Suppose
first
u\neq\rho_{N}
, and thus
\rho_{N}\in V(N^{\prime})
. Since clearly

\rho_{N}\notin\child_{N}(u)
, we did not insert the arc
(w,\rho_{N})
.
Together with the fact that all inserted arcs are incident with
w
, this
implies that
\rho_{N}
still has indegree
0
in
N^{\prime}
. Since
u\neq\rho_{N}
, vertex
u
has at least one in-neighbor, which becomes an
in-neighbor of
w
if it was not already an in-neighbor of
w
in
N
.
Now suppose, for contradiction, that there is a vertex
v\in V(N^{\prime})\setminus\{\rho_{N},w\}
with
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)=0
. Since
v\neq\rho_{N}
, there must be an arc
(u^{\prime},v)
in
N
which is no longer
contained in
N^{\prime}
. By construction and since
v\neq u
, we must have

u^{\prime}=u
. But then
(u,v)=(u^{\prime},v)\in E(N)
implies that
(w,v)
is an arc in

N^{\prime}
, contradicting that
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)=0
. Now consider the case
u=\rho_{N}
. Then
u
is the unique in-neighbor of
w
in
N
. To see this,
assume, for contradiction, that there is
v\in\parent_{N}(w)\setminus\{u\}

and thus
w\prec_{N}v
. Since
u=\rho_{N}
is the unique root, there is a

uv
-path. Since
v\neq u
, this path passes through some child
w^{\prime}
of

u
. Since
w\prec_{N}v\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
, we must have
w\neq w^{\prime}
and thus

w\prec_{N}w^{\prime}\prec_{N}u
, i.e.,
(u,w)
is a shortcut; a contradiction.
Therefore,
u
is the unique in-neighbor of
w
. Moreover, since

\parent_{N}(u)=\emptyset
,
w
does not get any new in-neighbors. After
deletion of
u
,
w
has outdegree
0
in
N^{\prime}
. Re-using the arguments
from the case
u\neq\rho_{N}
, there is no vertex
v\in V(N^{\prime})\setminus\{\rho_{N},w\}
with
\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)=0
. Hence,
N^{\prime}
is a
directed graph that has a unique vertex with indegree
0
, i.e., a
unique root
\rho_{N^{\prime}}
, in both cases.

We continue by showing that
N^{\prime}
is a DAG. Assume, for contradiction,
that
N^{\prime}
contains a directed cycle
K
comprising the vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}
,
k\geq 2
, in this order, i.e.,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
(v_{k},v_{1})
are arcs in
N^{\prime}
. If all arcs along
K
are
arcs of
N
, then
K
is a directed cycle in
N
; a contradiction. Hence,
at least one arc
e
in
K
cannot be contained in
N
. By construction,

e
must be of the form
(v,w)
with
v\in\parent_{N}(u)\setminus\parent_{N}(w)
or of the form
(w,v)
with
v\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\child_{N}(w)
. Since
w
can appear in at most two
arcs of this form, all other arcs in
K
must also be arcs in
N
.
Assume w.l.o.g. that
v_{1}=w
. Then the following cases have to be
considered:

(a)

(w,v_{2})=(v_{1},v_{2})\notin E(N)
but all other arcs are
contained in

N
,

(b)

(v_{k},w)=(v_{k},v_{1})\notin E(N)
but all other arcs are
contained in

N
, and

(c)

exactly the arcs
(w,v_{2})=(v_{1},v_{2})
and
(v_{k},w)=(v_{k},v_{1})
are not contained in
N
.

In case (a), we must have
(u,v_{2})\in V(N)
by construction, i.e,

v_{2}\in\child_{N}(u)
. Since all arcs except
(v_{1},v_{2})
exist in
N
and

w=v_{1}\neq v_{2}
, there is a
v_{2}w
-path in
N
, i.e.,
w\prec_{N}v_{2}
. This together with
w,v_{2}\in\child_{N}(u)
implies that
(u,w)
is a
shortcut; a contradiction. In case (b), we must have
(v_{k},u)\in V(N)

by construction. Then
N
contains a directed cycle along

v_{0}\coloneqq u,v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}
in this order, i.e.,

(v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
0\leq i\leq k-1
and
(v_{k},v_{0})=(v,u)
are arcs in

N
; a contradiction. In case (c), we must have

(v_{k},u),(u,v_{2})\in V(N)
by construction. Replacing
v_{1}
and its
incident arcs in the cycle
K
by vertex
u
(which is not already in

V(K)\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
) and arcs
(v_{k},u)
and
(u,v_{2})
thus yields a
directed cycle
K^{\prime}
in
N
; a contradiction. In summary, neither of the
three cases is possible and thus
N^{\prime}
is acyclic. Since
N^{\prime}
has a unique
root and acyclicity is preserved as well,
N^{\prime}
is a rooted network.

Since
\outdeg_{N}(u)>1
, we do not delete any leaf of
N
, i.e.,

X\subseteq V(N^{\prime})
. Moreover, the only vertices whose out-neighborhood
changes are
w
and the vertices in
\parent_{N}(u)
. Since all vertices
in
\parent_{N}(u)
have
w
as out-neighbor in
N^{\prime}
, they do not become
leaves. Hence, the leaf set of
N^{\prime}
is either
X
or
X\setminus\{w\}
.

We continue with showing that
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
implies
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}

for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V(N^{\prime})
. Thus assume that
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
i.e., there is
a
v^{\prime}v
-path
P\coloneqq(v^{\prime}=v_{1},\dots,v=v_{k})
in
N
. If
P
does not
contain
u
, then
P
is also a
v^{\prime}v
-path in
N^{\prime}
since only arcs that
are incident with
u
are removed. Now suppose
P
contains
u
. Then
clearly
u=v_{i}
for some
1<i<k
. Since
u
appears in
P
at most once
and only arcs incident with
u
were removed, all arcs in
P
except

(v_{i-1},u)
and
(u,v_{i+1})
are also arcs in
N^{\prime}
. Observe that

(v_{i-1},w)\in E(N^{\prime})
holds by construction. If
v_{i+1}=w
, then

(v_{i-1},v_{i+1})
is an arc in
N^{\prime}
and thus

\tilde{P}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\dots,v_{k})
is a
v^{\prime}v
-path in

N^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we have
v_{i+1}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
and thus

(w,v_{i+1})
is an arc in
N^{\prime}
. The vertex
w
is not contained in

P
. To see this, observe first that
w\neq v_{i+1}
and

w\in\child_{N}(u=v_{i})
implies
w\prec_{N}v_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq i
. If

w=v_{j}
for some
i+1<j\leq k
, then
w\prec_{N}v_{i+1}
which implies that

(u,w)
is a shortcut; a contradiction. Taken together, these arguments
imply that
\tilde{P}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1},w,v_{i+1},\dots,v_{k})
is a

v^{\prime}v
-path in
N^{\prime}
. Hence, we have
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
is all cases.

Conversely, suppose
v\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
, i.e., there is a
v^{\prime}v
-path

P^{\prime}\coloneqq(v^{\prime}=v_{1},\dots,v=v_{k})
in
N^{\prime}
. If all arcs in
P^{\prime}
are
arcs in
N
, then
P^{\prime}
is a
v^{\prime}v
-path in
N
and thus
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
. Now suppose that
P^{\prime}
contains at least one arc that is not in

N
. By construction, any such arc must be incident with
w
and thus

P^{\prime}
contains at most two arcs that are not in
N
. These are then
consecutive in
P^{\prime}
. We therefore have to consider three cases:

(a’)

(w,v_{i+1})=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\notin E(N)
for some
1\leq i<k
but all other arcs in
P^{\prime}
are contained in
N
,

(b’)

(v_{i-1},w)=(v_{i-1},v_{i})\notin E(N)
for some
1<i\leq k
but all other arcs in
P^{\prime}
are contained in
N
, and

(c’)

exactly the arcs
(v_{i-1},w)=(v_{i-1},v_{i})
and

(w,v_{i+1})=(v_{i},v_{i+1})
with
1<i<k
in
P^{\prime}
are not
contained in
N
.

In case (a’), we have by construction that
(u,v_{i+1})\in E(N)
, i.e.,

v_{i+1}\in\child_{N}(u)
. Since all other arcs of
P^{\prime}
are arcs in
N
,
the subpath of
P^{\prime}
from
v^{\prime}
to
w
is a
v^{\prime}w
-path in
N
and thus

w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
. Similarly, the subpath from
v_{i+1}(\neq w)
to
v

is a
v_{i+1}v
-path in
N
implying that
v\preceq_{N}v_{i+1}
with

v_{i+1}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
. Clearly
w\prec_{N}v_{i+1}
is
not possible since otherwise
(u,w)
would be a shortcut in
N
. Hence,
we have either
v\preceq_{N}v_{i+1}\prec_{N}w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
or
w
and

v_{i+1}
are
\preceq_{N}
-incomparable children of
u
.
In case (b’), we have by construction that
(v_{i-1},u)\in E(N)
, i.e.,

v_{i-1}\in\parent_{N}(u)
. Since all arcs of
P^{\prime}
except

(v_{i-1},v_{i})
are also in
N
, we have
v\preceq_{N}v_{i}=w\prec_{N}u\prec_{N}v_{i-1}\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
.
In case (c’), we have by construction that
(v_{i-1},u),(u,v_{i+1})\in E(N)
. Since all arcs of
P^{\prime}
except
(v_{i-1},v_{i})
and
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
are also arcs in
N
, we obtain
v\preceq_{N}v_{i+1}\prec_{N}u\prec_{N}v_{i-1}\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
. In summary, in all cases it holds that at
least one of (i)
v\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
or (ii)
w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
and
v\preceq_{N}w^{\prime}
for some
w^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
that is

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable with
w
is true.

For the final statement, assume that
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. If
v^{\prime}\preceq_{N}v
would, then (1) implies
v^{\prime}\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v
; a
contradiction. Consequently, we have
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
or
v
and
v^{\prime}
are

\preceq_{N}
-incomparable.
∎

In the following, we show that the operation
\expand(w)
does not
introduce shortcuts and that neither
\expand(w)
nor
\contract(w^{\prime},w)

increases the level of a network. Besides providing additional results, we
give here the proofs for statements that were omitted in the main text.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
N_{1}
be a network,
N_{2}
be the network obtained from
N_{1}
by
applying
\expand(w_{1})
for some
w_{1}\in V(N_{1})
, and
w_{2}
be the unique
vertex in
V(N_{2})\setminus V(N_{1})
. Then, for every shortcut
(u,v)\in E(N_{i})
, it holds either (i)
v\neq w_{i}
and
(u,v)
is a shortcut in

N_{j}
or (ii)
v=w_{i}
and
(u,w_{j})
is a shortcut in
N_{j}
such that

i,j\in\{1,2\}
are distinct.

Proof.

In the following, we put
N=N_{1}
,
N^{\prime}=N_{2}
,
w=w_{1}
and
w^{\prime}=w_{2}
. Recall
that by Lemma
3.13
,
N
and
N^{\prime}
are

(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving.

Suppose first that
(u,v)\in E(N^{\prime})
is a shortcut in

N^{\prime}
, i.e., there is a
v^{\prime}\in\child_{N^{\prime}}(u)\setminus\{v\}
such that

v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. The fact that
N^{\prime}
is acyclic,
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u
and
(u,v)\in E(N^{\prime})
together imply that

\indeg_{N^{\prime}}(v)\geq 2
. By construction, it holds

\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)=\{w^{\prime}\}
and
\child_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\{w\}
which yield
v\neq w

and
u\neq w^{\prime}
, respectively.

(i) Suppose first that
v\neq w^{\prime}
. Then
(u,v)
is also an arc in
N

since moreover
u\neq w^{\prime}
and all newly inserted arcs are incident with

w^{\prime}
. Similarly, if in addition
v^{\prime}\neq w^{\prime}
, then
(u,v^{\prime})\in E(N)
and
moreover
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
implies
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
. Hence,
(u,v)
is a
shortcut in
N
. If on the other hand
v^{\prime}=w^{\prime}
, then
w\in\child_{N}(u)

and
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}=w^{\prime}
implies that there is a
w^{\prime}v
-path in
N^{\prime}
. Since

w
is the unique child of
w^{\prime}
and
v\neq w^{\prime}
, this path must pass
through
w
and thus
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}w
which together with
v,w\in V(N)

implies
v\prec_{N}w
. Hence,
(u,v)
is a shortcut in
N
.

(ii) Suppose now that
v=w^{\prime}
. Then, by construction,
(u,w)\in V(N)
.
Moreover, we have
w\prec_{N^{\prime}}w^{\prime}\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
. In particular,
u
,

w
, and
v^{\prime}
are all vertices in
N^{\prime}
. Hence,
w\preceq_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}

implies
w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
and, since all newly inserted arcs are
incident with
w^{\prime}
,
(u,v^{\prime})\in V(N^{\prime})
is also an arc in
N
. In summary,
therefore,
(u,w)\in V(N)
and
w\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}
for

v^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{w\}
, i.e.,
(u,w)
is a shortcut in
N
.

Suppose now that
(u,v)\in E(N)
is a shortcut in

N
, i.e., there is a
v^{\prime}\in\child_{N}(u)\setminus\{v\}
such that

v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}
.

(i’) Suppose
v\neq w
. By construction, the arc
(u,v)
still exists in

N^{\prime}
. Since moreover
u,v,v^{\prime}\in V(N)\subset V(N^{\prime})
,
v\prec_{N}v^{\prime}\prec_{N}u
and
N
and
N^{\prime}
are
(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving
(cf. Lemma
3.13
), we have
v\prec_{N^{\prime}}v^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u
. Hence,
(u,v)
must be a shortcut in
N^{\prime}
.

(ii’) Finally suppose
v=w
. Observe that
u^{\prime}\preceq_{N}v^{\prime}(\prec_{N}u)
for some
u^{\prime}\in\parent_{N}(v)\setminus\{u\}
and that
u,u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in V(N)
. In particular, it holds
u^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u
since
N
and
N^{\prime}
are

(N^{\prime},N)
-ancestor-preserving (cf. Lemma
3.13
). By
construction, we have
(u,w^{\prime}),(u^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in V(N^{\prime})
and thus
w^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u^{\prime}
. In summary, we have
w^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u^{\prime}\prec_{N^{\prime}}u
, which implies
that
(u,w^{\prime})
is a shortcut in
N^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
N
be a network,
(w^{\prime},w)\in E(N)
be an arc that is not a
shortcut, and
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\contract(w^{\prime},w)
. If two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})
are in a
common non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
, then either (i)
w\notin\{u,v\}
and

u,v\in V(N)
are in a common non-trivial block of
N
or (ii)
w\in\{u,v\}
and the unique element in
\{u,v\}\setminus\{w\}
and
w^{\prime}
are
in a common block in
N
.

Proof.

Suppose two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
are in a
common non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
.
Thus,
u
and
v
are contained in an undirected cycle
K^{\prime}
in
N^{\prime}
.
If (the directed versions of) all arcs in
K^{\prime}
are arcs of
N
, then

K^{\prime}
is an undirected cycle in
N
, and thus,
u
and
v
are contained
in a non-trivial block of
N
. Now suppose that at least one arc
e
in

K^{\prime}
is not contained in
N
. By construction,
e
must be of the form

(p,w)
with
p\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\parent_{N}(w)
or of the form

(w,c)
with
c\in\child_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(w)
. Since such an arc
is incident with
w
, the only arcs of
N^{\prime}
that possibly are not
contained in
N
are the two arcs incident with
w
in
K^{\prime}
. Hence,
we have to consider the following cases:

(1)

both of the incident arcs of
w
in
K^{\prime}
are not contained in

N
but all other arcs are contained in
N
,

(a)

w
is incident in
K^{\prime}
with two distinct parents
p_{1},p_{2}\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\parent_{N}(w)
,

(b)

w
is incident in
K^{\prime}
with two distinct children
c_{1},c_{2}\in\child_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(w)
, or

(c)

w
is incident in
K^{\prime}
with a parent
p\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\parent_{N}(w)
and a child
c\in\child_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(w)
.

(2)

exactly one of the arcs incident with
w
in
K^{\prime}
is not
contained in
N
, while all other arcs of
N^{\prime}
are contained in
N
.

(a)

w
is incident in
K^{\prime}
with a parent
p\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\parent_{N}(w)
, or

(b)

w
is incident in
K^{\prime}
with a child
c\in\child_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(w)
.

Observe that
w^{\prime}\notin V(N^{\prime})
and thus
w^{\prime}
is not contained in
K^{\prime}
.
One therefore easily verifies that, in each of cases
(1a)
,

(1b)
, and
(1c)
, replacing
w
by
w^{\prime}
in
K^{\prime}
yields an
undirected cycle
K
in
N
. If
w\notin\{u,v\}
, then
u

and
v
are contained in
K
and thus contained in a common non-trivial
block of
N
. If on the other hand
w\in\{u,v\}
, then the unique
element in
\{u,v\}\setminus\{w\}
and
w^{\prime}
are contained in
K
and
thus contained in a common non-trivial block of
N
. In
case
(2a)
,
K^{\prime}
contains the arc
(p,w)
by construction of

N^{\prime}
. Replacing this arc by vertex
w^{\prime}
and the arcs
(p,w^{\prime}),(w^{\prime},w)\in V(N)
therefore yields an undirected cycle
K
in
N
that contains all
vertices in
K^{\prime}
. In particular, therefore,
u,v\in V(K^{\prime})
are contained
in a common non-trivial block in
N
. The latter is also true in
case
(2b)
by similar arguments.
∎

From Lemma
3.17
, we obtain

Corollary A.3
.

Let
N
be a network and
u,v,w\in V(N)
three distinct vertices. If
u

and
v
are contained in block
B_{uv}
,
u
and
w
are contained in
block
B_{uw}
, and
v
and
w
are contained in block
B_{vw}
, then

B_{uv}=B_{uw}=B_{vw}
.

Proof.

If
u\notin\{\max B_{uv},\max B_{uw}\}
, then
Lemma
3.19
immediately implies that
B_{uv}=B_{uw}
.
Thus suppose (a)
u=\max B_{uv}
or (b)
u=\max B_{uw}
. In Case (a),
we have
v\prec_{N}u
and
v\neq\max B_{uv}
. If
v\neq\max B_{vw}
, then

B_{uv}=B_{vw}
by Lemma
3.19
. Now assume
v=\max B_{vw}
which implies
w\prec_{N}v
and
w\neq\max B_{vw}
. If
w\neq\max B_{uw}
, then
B_{uw}=B_{vw}
by Lemma
3.19
. The
case
w=\max B_{uw}
is not possible since otherwise
u\prec_{N}w
, and
thus
u\prec_{N}w\prec_{N}v\prec_{N}u
; a contradiction. One argues
similarly in Case (b). Hence, in all possible case,
u
,
v
, and
w

are contained in a common non-trivial block
B
. Since each of
B_{uv}
,

B_{uw}
, and
B_{vw}
shares two vertices with
B
, it holds

B=B_{uv}=B_{uw}=B_{vw}
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
N
be a network,
(w^{\prime},w)\in E(N)
be an arc that is not a shortcut,
and
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying
\contract(w^{\prime},w)
.
Let
B^{\prime}
be a non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
. Then there is a non-trivial
block
B
of
N
with
V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
. Moreover,

w\in V(B^{\prime})
and
w\notin V(B)
imply
w^{\prime}\in V(B)
.

If
v\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}
is a hybrid vertex and properly contained
in
B^{\prime}
, then
v
is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B
. If
w

is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B^{\prime}
, then at least one of
w

and
w^{\prime}
is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B
.

Proof.

Suppose
B^{\prime}
is a non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
and thus, it contains at
least three vertices. In particular, we can find two distinct vertices in

u,v
such that
w\notin\{u,v\}
. By
Lemma
A.2
,
u,v\in V(N)
are in a common
non-trivial block
B\coloneqq B_{uv}
of
N
. Now suppose there is

u^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w,u,v\}
. By
Lemma
A.2
,
u,u^{\prime}\in V(N)
and
v,u^{\prime}\in V(N)
,
resp., are in common non-trivial blocks
B_{uu^{\prime}}
and
B_{vu^{\prime}}
of
N
.
By Cor.
A.3
,
u
,
v
, and
u^{\prime}
are contained in

B=B_{uv}=B_{uu^{\prime}}=B_{vu^{\prime}}
of
N
. Since
u^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w,u,v\}
was chosen arbitrarily and blocks that share two vertices are
equal by Obs.
2.1
, we have
V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
.

Now suppose
w\in V(B^{\prime})
and
w\notin V(B)
. By
Lemma
A.5
(ii),
u,w^{\prime}\in V(N)
and
v,w^{\prime}\in V(N)
,
resp., are in common non-trivial blocks
B_{uw^{\prime}}
and
B_{vw^{\prime}}
of
N
.
By Cor.
A.3
,
w^{\prime}
is contained in

B_{uw^{\prime}}=B_{vw^{\prime}}=B_{uv}=B
.

Suppose
v\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}
is a hybrid vertex and properly
contained in
B^{\prime}
. By Lemma
3.23
, all of the at
least two vertices in
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(v)
are contained in
B^{\prime}
. Hence, let

p,p^{\prime}\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(v)
be two distinct parents, and assume
w.l.o.g. that
p\neq w
. Note that
v,p\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
. Moreover, since all newly inserted arcs to obtain

N^{\prime}
from
N
are incident with
w
, the arc
(p,v)\in E(N^{\prime})
is also an
arc in
N
. Now consider
p^{\prime}
. If
(p^{\prime},v)\in E(N)
, then
v
is a
hybrid vertex in
N
. If
(p^{\prime},v)\notin E(N)
, then, by construction of

N^{\prime}
, we must have
p^{\prime}=w
and
v\in\child_{N}(w^{\prime})\setminus\child_{N}(w)
. Hence, we have
(w^{\prime},v)\in E(N)
. Together with
w^{\prime}\neq p

(since
p\in V(N^{\prime})=V(N)\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
) this implies that
v
is a
hybrid vertex in
N
also in this case. Therefore and since

p\in\parent_{N}(v)
and
v,p\in V(B)
, Lemma
3.23

implies that
v
is properly contained in
B
.

Suppose
w
is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B^{\prime}
. By
Lemma
3.23
, all of the at least two vertices in

\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)
are contained in
B^{\prime}
. We distinguish cases (a)
w\in V(B)
and (b)
w\notin V(B)
.

Case (a):

w\in V(B)
. Suppose first that there is
p\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)\cap\parent_{N}(w)
. We have
p\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
. Moreover,
w
has at least the two distinct parents

w^{\prime}
and
p
in
N
, i.e.,
w
is a hybrid vertex in
N
. By
Lemma
3.22
and since
w
and its parent

p
are both contained in
B
,
w
is properly contained in
B
. Suppose
now that
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)\cap\parent_{N}(w)=\emptyset
. By
construction of
N^{\prime}
, this implies that all of the at least two vertices
in
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)
must be vertices in
\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})
. Hence,
w^{\prime}

is a hybrid vertex in
N
with at least two parents
p
and
p^{\prime}
that are
parents of
w
in
N^{\prime}
and thus contained in
V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
. By Lemma
3.22
,

\{w^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}\}\in V(\tilde{B})
for some block
\tilde{B}
of
N
. Since

B
and
\tilde{B}
are blocks in
N
that share the two vertices
p
and

p^{\prime}
, we conclude
B=\tilde{B}
. In particular,
w^{\prime}
is a properly
contained hybrid vertex in
B
.

Case (b):

w\notin V(B)
. We have already seen that this
implies that
w^{\prime}\in V(B)
. Since
w
is properly contained in
B^{\prime}
, all
of its at least two parents in
N^{\prime}
are also contained in
B^{\prime}
. Let
p\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)
. Suppose, for contradiction, that
p\in\parent_{N}(w)
. Then
w
has at least the two distinct parents
w^{\prime}
and

p
in
N
, i.e.,
w
is a hybrid vertex in
N
. By
Lemma
3.22
,
\{w,w^{\prime},p\}\in V(\tilde{B})

for some block
\tilde{B}
of
N
. Since
B
and
\tilde{B}
are blocks
in
N
that share the two vertices
w^{\prime}
and
p
, we conclude

B=\tilde{B}
and thus
w\in V(B)
; a contradiction. Hence,
p\notin\parent_{N}(w)
. Together with
p\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)
, this implies
p\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})
. Since the latter is true for all of the at least two
vertices
p^{\prime}\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w)
,
w^{\prime}
must be a hybrid vertex in
N
.
Therefore, and because
p\in\parent_{N}(w^{\prime})
and
w^{\prime},p\in V(B)
,
Lemma
3.23
implies that
w^{\prime}
is properly
contained in
B
.
∎

We are now in the position to prove Lemma
3.25
. To
recall, Lemma
3.25
states that
\contract(w^{\prime},w)
of a
non-shortcut arc
(w^{\prime},w)
in a level-
k
network
N
preserves the property
of the resulting network
N^{\prime}
to be level-
k
.

Proof of Lemma
3.25
.

Suppose, for contraposition, that
N^{\prime}
is not level-
k
. Hence, there is
a block
B^{\prime}
in
N^{\prime}
that properly contains at least
k+1
hybrid
vertices. Denote the set of these vertices by
A
. By
Lemma
A.4
, it holds
V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}\subseteq V(B)
for some non-trivial block
B
of
N
, and in particular,
all vertices in
A\setminus\{w\}
are properly contained hybrid vertices
in
B
. If
w\notin A
, then
B
properly contains at least
k+1
hybrid
vertices. Otherwise,
w
is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B^{\prime}

and
B
properly contains at least
k
hybrid vertices in
A\setminus\{w\}
, and, by Lemma
A.4
, at least on of

w,w^{\prime}\notin A\setminus\{w\}
is an additional properly contained hybrid
vertex in
B
. Therefore, the block
B
in
N
properly contains at
least
k+1
hybrid vertices in both cases, and thus,
N
is not
level-
k
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Let
N
be a network,
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)
, and
w^{\prime}
be the unique vertex in

V(N^{\prime})\setminus V(N)
. If two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})
are in a
common non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
, then either (i)
w^{\prime}\notin\{u,v\}
and

u,v\in V(N)
are in a common non-trivial block of
N
or (ii)
w^{\prime}\in\{u,v\}
and
w
and the element in
\{u,v\}\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
are in a
common block in
N
.

Proof.

Suppose two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(N^{\prime})
are in a common non-trivial
block
B^{\prime}
of
N^{\prime}
. Then
u
and
v
lie in an undirected cycle
K^{\prime}
in

N^{\prime}
.

(i) Assume first that
w^{\prime}\notin\{u,v\}
, and thus
u,v\in V(N)
. If

w^{\prime}
is not contained in
K^{\prime}
, then all arcs in
K^{\prime}
are also arcs in

N
since all newly inserted arcs are incident with
w^{\prime}
. Hence,
u
and

v
lie on an undirected cycle in
N
and are thus contained in a common
non-trivial block
B
in this case. Now suppose that
K^{\prime}
contains
w^{\prime}
.
Assume that
w^{\prime}
is incident with its unique child
w
in
K^{\prime}
. Then, by
construction of
N^{\prime}
, the second vertex that is incident with
w^{\prime}
in

K^{\prime}
must be a vertex
p\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\parent_{N}(w)
. In particular,
we have
(p,w)\in E(N)
and
(p,w),(w,p)\notin E(N^{\prime})
. The latter implies
that
p
and
w
are not incident in
K^{\prime}
and therefore
K^{\prime}
contains at
least one additional vertex
x\notin\{p,w^{\prime},w\}
. Together with the fact
that
N
contains all arcs in
K^{\prime}
except
(p,w^{\prime})
and
(w^{\prime},w)
, the
latter arguments imply that there is an undirected cycle
K
in
N

formed by the vertices in
V(K^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
and
the arcs in
(E(K^{\prime})\setminus\{(p,w^{\prime}),(w^{\prime},w)\})\cup\{(p,w)\}
. Hence,

u,v\in V(K^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
lie in a common non-trivial block of
N
.
Now suppose
w^{\prime}
is not incident with its unique child
w
in
K^{\prime}
, and
thus the two incident vertices in
K^{\prime}
must be two distinct element

p_{1},p_{2}\in\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\parent_{N}(w)
. Thus, we have

(p_{1},w),(p_{2},w)\in E(N)
. Since
(p_{1},w^{\prime})
and
(p_{2},w^{\prime})
are the only
arcs incident with
w^{\prime}
in
K^{\prime}
, all other arcs in
K^{\prime}
are also arcs
in
N
. Thus consider the (not necessarily induced) subgraph
K
of
N

formed by the vertices
(V(K^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\})\cup\{w\}
and arcs

(E(K^{\prime})\setminus\{(p_{1},w^{\prime}),(p_{2},w^{\prime})\})\cup\{(p_{1},w),(p_{2},w)\}
. If

w\notin V(K^{\prime})
, then one easily verifies that
K
is an undirected cycle in

N
that contains
u
and
v
and thus they are contained in a common block

B
of
N
. On the other hand, if
w\in V(K^{\prime})
, then its two incident
vertices in
K^{\prime}
are two distinct elements

c_{1},c_{2}\in\child_{N^{\prime}}(w)=\child_{N}(w)
since, by assumption,
w
is not
incident in
K^{\prime}
with its unique parent
w^{\prime}
. In this case, one easily
verifies that
K
consists of two undirected cycles that share only
the vertex
w
and each of the two cycles contains
exactly one of
p_{1},p_{2}\in\parent_{N}(w)
and one of

c_{1},c_{2}\in\child_{N}(w)
. If
u
and
v
are contained in the same of
these two cycles, then they are contained in a common non-trivial block
of
N
. Otherwise, they are contained in non-trivial blocks
B
and

B^{\prime}
, resp., that each contain
w
and one of its parents. The latter
implies that
w\notin\{\max B,\max B^{\prime}\}
. Hence, we have
B=B^{\prime}
by
Lemma
3.19
.

(ii) Suppose now that
w^{\prime}\in\{u,v\}
, say
v=w^{\prime}
. We can essentially
re-use the arguments from case (i), with exception of the case that
w^{\prime}

is not contained in
K^{\prime}
(which is impossible since
w^{\prime}=v\in V(K^{\prime})
),
since we always have constructed an undirected cycle in
N
that contains

u,w\in V(N)
. Hence, they are contained in a non-trivial block of
N
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
N
be a network,
N^{\prime}
be the network obtained from
N
by applying

\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)
, and
w^{\prime}
be the unique vertex in

V(N^{\prime})\setminus V(N)
.
Let
B^{\prime}
be a non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
. Then there is a non-trivial block

B
of
N
with
V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}\subseteq V(B)
.
Moreover,
w^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})
implies
w\in V(B)
.

If
v\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
is a hybrid vertex and properly contained
in
B^{\prime}
, then
v
is a properly contained hybrid vertex in
B
.
If
w^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})
and
w^{\prime}
is a hybrid vertex, then
w
is a properly
contained hybrid vertex in
B
.

Proof.

Suppose
B^{\prime}
is a non-trivial block of
N^{\prime}
and thus, it contains at
least three vertices. In particular, we can find two distinct vertices in

u,v
such that
w^{\prime}\notin\{u,v\}
. By
Lemma
A.5
,
u,v\in V(N)
are in a common
non-trivial block
B\coloneqq B_{uv}
of
N
. Now suppose there is

u^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime},u,v\}
. By Lemma
A.5
,

u,u^{\prime}\in V(N)
and
v,u^{\prime}\in V(N)
, resp., are in common non-trivial
blocks
B_{uu^{\prime}}
and
B_{vu^{\prime}}
of
N
. By Cor.
A.3
,

u
,
v
, and
u^{\prime}
are contained in
B=B_{uv}
. Since
u^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime},u,v\}
was chosen arbitrarily and blocks that share
two vertices are equal by Obs.
2.1
, we have

V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}\subseteq V(B)
.

Now suppose
w^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})
. If
w\in V(B^{\prime})
, then we have already seen
that
w\in V(B)
. Hence, suppose
w\notin V(B^{\prime})
. By
Lemma
A.5
,
u,w\in V(N)
and
v,w\in V(N)
,
resp., are in common non-trivial blocks
B_{uw}
and
B_{vw}
of
N
. By
Cor.
A.3
,
w
must also be contained in
B
.

Suppose
v\in V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
is a hybrid vertex and properly
contained in
B^{\prime}
. Since
w
has a unique parent in
N^{\prime}
, we have
v\neq w
and thus
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(v)=\parent_{N}(v)
. Moreover, since
v
is
properly contained in
B^{\prime}
, we have
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(v)\in V(B^{\prime})
and thus

\parent_{N^{\prime}}(v)\in V(B)
. Taken together, the latter arguments imply
that
v\neq\max B
, i.e.,
v
is properly contained in
B
.

Suppose
w^{\prime}\in V(B^{\prime})
and
w^{\prime}
is a hybrid vertex. Since
w^{\prime}
has a
unique child and must lie on an undirected cycle in
N^{\prime}
,
w^{\prime}
must be
properly contained in
B^{\prime}
. Therefore, all of its at least two parent are
also contained in
B^{\prime}
and thus in
B
. Together with the fact that
w\in V(B)
and
\parent_{N^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})=\parent_{N}(w)
, we obtain that
w
is a hybrid
vertex and properly contained in
B
.
∎

We now prove Lemma
3.27
.
To recall, Lemma
3.27
states that two networks

N
and
N^{\prime}
are always level-
k
whenever
N^{\prime}
is obtained from

N
by applying
\expand(w)
for some
w\in V(N)

and at least one of them is level-
k
.

Proof of Lemma
3.27
.

Denote by
w^{\prime}
the unique vertex in
V(N^{\prime})\setminus V(N)
.
Suppose that
N^{\prime}
is not level-
k
. Hence, there is a block
B^{\prime}
in
N^{\prime}

that properly contains at least
k+1
hybrid vertices. Denote the set of
these vertices by
A
. Observe that
w\notin A
since it has a single
parent
w
in
N^{\prime}
. By Lemma
A.6
, it holds

V(B^{\prime})\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}\subseteq V(B)
for some non-trivial block
B
of

N
, and in particular, all vertices
A\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
are properly
contained hybrid vertices in
B
. If
w^{\prime}\notin A
, then
B
properly
contains at least
k+1
hybrid vertices. Otherwise,
B
properly
contains at least
k
hybrid vertices in
A\setminus\{w^{\prime}\}
and, by
Lemma
A.6
, additionally the hybrid vertex

w\notin A
. Therefore, the block
B
in
N
properly contains at least

k+1
hybrid vertices in both cases, and thus,
N
is not level-
k
.

Conversely, suppose that
N^{\prime}
is level-
k
. Observe that
N
is recovered
from
N^{\prime}
by applying
\contract(w^{\prime},w)
. By
Lemma
3.25
, therefore,
N
is also level-
k
.
∎

A.2
Closed Clustering Systems

As promised, we provide here a short proof of Lemma
3.32
,
which states that a clustering system
\mathscr{C}
is closed if and only if

A,B\in\mathscr{C}
and
A\cap B\neq\emptyset
implies

A\cap B\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.32
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be closed and let
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
. Since
\cl
is
enlarging, we have
A\cap B\subseteq\cl(A\cap B)
. Moreover, since

A\cap B\subseteq A,B
, it holds by Equ. (
2
) that

\cl(A\cap B)\subseteq A\cap B
. Hence,
\cl(A\cap B)=A\cap B
and the
definition of “closed” implies
A\cap B\in\mathscr{C}
. Assume now
that
C\cap C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
.
Equ. (
2
) implies
\cl(A)=A
for all
A\in\mathscr{C}
. It
remains to show that, for
A\in 2^{X}
,
\cl(A)=A
implies

A\in\mathscr{C}
. By Equ. (
2
),
\cl(A)
can be written as
the intersection
\cl(A)=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}
of a finite number
k
of
clusters
C_{i}\in\mathscr{C}
with
A\subseteq C_{i}
. Since

A\subseteq X\in\mathscr{C}
we have
k\geq 1
. If
k\in\{1,2\}
,

\cl(A)=A\in\mathcal{C}
follows immediately from the assumption that

C\cap C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
. Otherwise, we
can construct a series of intersections
C^{\prime}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, by
setting
C^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq C_{1}
and
C^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq C^{\prime}_{j-1}\cap C_{j}
for

2\leq j\leq k
. By definition,
C^{\prime}_{1}=C_{1}\in\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, if

C^{\prime}_{j-1}\in\mathscr{C}
, then
C^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathscr{C}
holds by the
assumption and the fact that
C_{j}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
2\leq j\leq k
. By induction, therefore, we obtain
C^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{C}
. We have

C^{\prime}_{k}=\cl(A)
by construction and thus
\cl(A)=A\in\mathcal{C}
.
∎

A.3
Algorithmic Details

We show here the correctness and runtime results for

Check-L1-Compatibility
.

Proof of Thm.
7.48
.

Let
\mathscr{C}\subseteq 2^{X}
be a clustering system. Suppose first
that
Check-L1-Compatibility
returns a network. In particular,

\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) in this case. By Obs.
7.46

and Lemma
7.47
,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is correctly computed and satisfies
(L). By definition,
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
is closed. The latter two arguments together
with Prop.
7.44
imply that there is a
(separated, phylogenetic) level-1 network on
X
such that

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}_{N}
.

Conversely, suppose that
Check-L1-Compatibility
returns

‘‘no solution’’
. Assume for contradiction that there is a level-1
network
N
with
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}
. By
Thm.
7.38
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}
is closed and satisfies (L). By
Cor.
7.18
, Property (L) implies that
\mathscr{C}
is weak
hierarchy. As shown in
[
4
]
(right below Lemma 1), this in
turn implies that
|\mathscr{C}|\leq|\mathscr{C}_{N}|\leq\binom{|X|+1}{2}=\binom{|X|}{2}+|X|
. Inputs
larger than this bound are therefore correctly rejected immediately. If,
on the other hand, Property (L) is not satisfied for
\mathscr{C}
, then by
the definition of Property (L), its superset
\mathscr{C}_{N}
also violates
(L); a contradiction. Therefore, such a network
N
cannot exist and

Check-L1-Compatibility
correctly exits with a negative answer.

We now proceed to show that the algorithm can be implemented to run in

O(|\mathscr{C}|^{2}|X|)\subseteq O(|X|^{5})
time.
To this end, we first enumerate the elements in
X
from
1
to
|X|
. We then initialize a list
\mathcal{L}
containing a bitvector

b_{i}
of size
|X|
for each
C_{i}\in\mathscr{C}
that has a

1
-entry at position
j
if and only if the
j
th element of
X
is
contained in
C_{j}
, and a
0
-entry otherwise; requiring a total effort of

O(|\mathscr{C}||X|)
time. Moreover, we create an
initially arcless auxiliary graph
G
whose vertices are (unique
identifiers of) the clusters in
\mathscr{C}
. In the end, two clusters

C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathscr{C}
will be connected by an arc in
G
precisely if
they overlap. Moreover, every arc
\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
will be associated with a
pointer to a bitvector that represents the intersection
C_{i}\cap C_{j}
.

To achieve this, we proceed as follows for every pair
C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathscr{C}
. We compute the bitvector
b
corresponding to the
intersection
C_{i}\cap C_{j}
as
b\leftarrow b_{i}\wedge b_{j}
. The
clusters
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
overlap if and only if the number of
1
-entries
in
b
is greater than
0
but less than
|C_{i}|
and
|C_{j}|
(the latter cardinalities can be pre-computed for all clusters in

\mathscr{C}
). If the clusters do overlap, then we continue as follows.
If
C_{i}
is already adjacent with some other cluster in

\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
, then we pick
C_{k}
among them
arbitrarily. Let
b^{\prime}
be the bitvector associated with the arc

\{C_{i},C_{k}\}
in
G
. By (L),
b
and
b^{\prime}
must be equal, which can be
checked in
O(|X|)
. If they are not equal, we can exit

‘‘no solution’’
. If existent, we proceed analogously with some
neighbor
C_{l}\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
of
C_{j}
in
G
with
associated bitvector
b^{\prime\prime}
. We add the arc
\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
to
G
. If at
least one of
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
previously had a neighbor
C_{k}
or
C_{j}
,
respectively, then we associate the bitvector
b^{\prime}
or
b^{\prime\prime}
, respectively,
with the new arc
\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
and discard
b
. Otherwise, we associate
b

with
\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
and add
b
to
\mathcal{L}
. In summary, for a pair

C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathscr{C}
, we only perform a constant number of operations,
all of which require at most
O(|X|)
time. Hence, we obtain a
total effort of
O(|\mathscr{C}|^{2}|X|)
time for
processing all pairs of clusters in
\mathscr{C}
.

One easily verifies that
\mathscr{C}
satisfies (L) if the algorithms
did not exit at this point because all overlaps are represented by arcs
in
G
and, for each
C\in\mathscr{C}
, the intersections with its
overlapping clusters are stepwisely added and compared to the overlaps
that where already computed. Moreover, we have added at most
\lfloor|\mathscr{C}|/2\rfloor
bitvectors to
\mathcal{L}
. To see
this, recall that we only added a new bitvector
b
(associated with arc

\{C_{i},C_{j}\}
) if neither of
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
had any other neighbors at
that point. Since each arc is incident with two clusters, we can clearly
do this at most
\lfloor|\mathscr{C}|/2\rfloor
times until
all but possible one clusters are adjacent with some other cluster.
Hence,
\mathcal{L}
still contains only
O(|\mathscr{C}|)

bitvectors. In particular,
\mathcal{L}
contains all clusters in

\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}\cup\{C\cap C^{\prime}\mid C,C^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\text{
overlap}\}
(represented by their bitvectors) at least once.

We now sort
\mathcal{L}
lexicographically in
O(|X||\mathscr{C}|\log|\mathscr{C}|)=O(|X|\,|\mathscr{C}|\log|X|)
, where the additional factor
|X|

originates from the fact that each comparison of bitvectors requires

O(|X|)
comparisons of their entries. Removal of all duplicates in

\mathcal{L}
now takes
O(|\mathscr{C}||X|)
time, e.g. by
iterating through the list and comparing each vector to the last added
bitvector in a newly constructed list. The bitvectors in this final list

\mathcal{L}
are thus in a 1-to-1 correspondence with the clusters in

\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
.

If we are only interested in the existence of a (separated, phylogenetic)
level-
1
network
N
such that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{N}

and the clustering system
\mathscr{C}_{N}
, then we can stop here after
a total effort of
O(|\mathscr{C}|^{2}|X|)\subseteq O(|X|^{5})
time.

Otherwise we continue with the construction of the inclusion order, i.e.,
the Hasse diagram of
\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})
. In the following, the cluster
C_{i}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})

corresponds to the
i
th bitvector in
\mathcal{L}
. We initialize a

|\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})|\times|\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})|
-matrix
M
with all zero entries
(requiring
O(|\mathscr{C}|^{2})
time and space). In the end, we
will have
M_{i,j}=1
if and only if
C_{i}\subsetneq C_{j}
. Since

\mathcal{L}
is still sorted lexicographically, observe that, if

C_{i}\subsetneq C_{j}
, then
i<j
. Hence, it suffices to check for all
1\leq i<j\leq|\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C})|
whether all
1
-entries in
b_{i}
are also

1
-entries in
b_{j}
and, if so, set
M_{i,j}=1
. Finally, the adjacency
matrix of
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
is obtained from
M
by
Transitive
Reduction
. As shown in
[
1
]
for DAGs this task has the same
complexity as
Transitive Closure
, which, in our setting, is
bounded
O(|X|^{2\omega})
. Here,
\omega\leq 2.3729
is the
“matrix multiplication constant”. Thus
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
can also be
constructed in quintic time. Thus we obtain a regular (and thus
phylogenetic) level-1 network
N\sim\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
in
O(|X|^{5})

time. This network can be modified to be separated by arc expansion at all
hybrid vertices
v
with
\outdeg(v)
. Since
\mathfrak{H}(\mathcal{I}(\mathscr{C}))
has
O(|X|^{2})
vertices, for each vertex, one checks in constant time whether

\indeg(v)>1
and
\outdeg(v)>1
, and
\expand(v)
requires constant effort
for moving the list of out-neighbors from
v
to the newly inserted vertex,
the total effort for the modification of the Hasse diagram is bounded by

O(|X|^{2})
.
∎
